## A Reply to Sebastian Castellio's Calumnies, by Which He Strives to Overturn
## the Sole Foundation of Our Salvation, That Is, God's Eternal Predestination

### Preface to Sebastian Castellio's calumnies against the doctrine whose
### author he makes John Calvin

“Your doctrine certainly has many supporters, John Calvin, a man celebrated
through almost the whole world; but that same doctrine also has many
opponents. For my part, since I desire that there should be one doctrine, just
as there is one truth, and that everyone should agree in it if that is
possible, I thought that I should give you a friendly notice of the things
which are said or commonly alleged against your doctrine. If they are false,
you may refute them and send us the refutation, so that we may be able to stand
against those opponents; and you should do this with arguments of a kind that
ordinary people can understand.

“There are, moreover, many matters on which many disagree with you. For the
present, however, leaving the rest to another time, I shall deal with you about
the single subject of Fate, or Predestination. This article both causes great
disturbances in the church, which we desire to see extinguished, and the
opponents' arguments on this subject seem to be of such a kind that they cannot
yet be refuted from the books you have published up to now.

“Certain articles excerpted from your books are being circulated on this
subject. I shall set them down here in a rough-and-ready order, and then append
what is commonly said against each one, so that you may see what needs an
answer.”

### Response

Although we have always endeavored to explain that doctrine which is not ours
but Jesus Christ's in such a way as to kindle as many people as possible with
desire and love for it, you are nevertheless greatly mistaken, Sebastian, if
you suppose that we are concerned about the number either of its opponents or
of its supporters. We are not so ignorant of human affairs, nor so forgetful
even of Christ's own words, that we do not know that far more people have
always been found to assail the truth than to defend it.

But you, as I see, when thinking up some clever preamble, chose the one most
suited to your own nature and disposition. For this is generally what happens
to people, especially those with a guilty conscience: they measure others by
their own character. Whom do you suppose to be either so grim that he would not
laugh at you, or so lighthearted that he would not be indignant with you, if he
heard you proclaiming that you desire nothing more than unity of doctrine?
You, I say, were the first to dare to introduce Academic *akatalepsia* into
these regions in which God's light had begun to shine again. Surely this is the
way and method for establishing unity of doctrine: to profess that the Lord's
word is so equivocal and ambiguous that, however one takes it, it is
insufficient to decide controversies of religion, and that some new revelation
from heaven must be awaited! You can readily remember where you wrote this in
so many words. Away, then, with that hypocrisy. Why do you not come out from
your hiding places? Why do you conceal your name? Why do you not openly declare
war on the truth? You require from us arguments

[Printed p. 338; PDF p. 3; logical scan 362; OCR object 0371]

that ordinary people can understand. We answer that we profess a doctrine
which can appear to the world to be nothing but foolishness. How true this
answer is may best be learned, we say, from Christ's own example. For if he
does not satisfy you, whom may we suppose will do so? Yet you must admit that
the crowds which continually followed him made very little progress in his
doctrine, while even those twelve intimate companions to whom he explained his
parables seem not to have understood sufficiently the nature of his kingdom
when their teacher was on the very point of ascending into heaven. In case you
do not know it, “the mind of the flesh” is precisely what prevents us from
seeing God's light; nor does it inhere only in those wise men, but in people of
every kind. If someone is subject to this disease—and everyone not reborn by
the Spirit is subject to it—you labor in vain to prove the mysteries of the
Christian religion to him, whether you stammer with the common people or
pretend wisdom with those wise men, that is, whether you wish to go mad by
means of reason. We must embrace these things by faith. Faith, moreover, is
God's gift, and it embraces arguments not devised in our own brain but drawn
from God's Word. If you do not grasp them, you have no ground either to allege
that the matters are excessively obscure or to demand greater clarity in
Calvin or anyone else. You have within yourself the darkness that causes you
to be blind in the clearest light. You neglect the writings of learned men, or
you approach them not in order to learn but to find fault. Pride blinds you
here, however much you conceal it beneath a false appearance of freedom and
simplicity. Give glory to God, then, and earnestly ask him to open your eyes.
Only then will you discover that the arguments which you now condemn as
obscure and insufficiently firm are plain, clear, and necessary.

You also state by way of preface that you have resolved to deal with the one
subject of Fate, or Predestination. I am astonished that anyone can possess
enough effrontery to dare cast that odious name “Fate” at Calvin. I shall not
hesitate to quote in his own words what he wrote about it long ago, so that
from the very outset anyone may perceive the spirit you have brought to the
conduct of this case. Calvin writes as follows: “Those who wish to make this
doctrine odious slander it as the Stoics' dogma of Fate; this was also once
cast as a reproach against Augustine. Although we are reluctant to quarrel
over words, we nevertheless do not accept the term *Fate*, both because it
belongs to the class of profane novelties which Paul teaches us to avoid, and
because through hatred of that word they try to burden God's truth.” Those are
Calvin's exact words. If you did not know them, what rashness it was to condemn
what you did not know! But if you did know them, what shamelessness to fasten
upon him a charge which you yourself knew to be false! Yet I shall indulge you
here. You will say that he abolishes Fate in word but establishes it in
reality. Hear, then, what he adds in that very place: “The Stoics' dogma itself
is falsely and maliciously imputed to us. For we do not invent with the Stoics
a necessity arising from a perpetual connection of causes and from an
entangled chain contained in nature. Instead, we establish God as the arbiter
and governor of all things, who according to his wisdom decreed from the
remotest eternity what he was going to do, and who now executes by his power
what he decreed. Hence we affirm that by his providence not only heaven and
earth and inanimate creatures, but even human counsels and wills, are so
governed as to be led to the end appointed by it.” So far Calvin. He then adds
how, if you consider secondary causes in themselves, something may occur by
chance and contingently. If you do not understand those discussions, you ought
to blame yourself for not having learned at least from Cicero and Plutarch what
the controversy between Chrysippus and Diodorus was.

But to what purpose is all this? It is so everyone may understand how much
credence should be given to an accuser who lies so confidently in his very
opening, though even the most wicked men ordinarily present some appearance of
honesty in that part of a speech. As for the matter itself, we shall settle
these points more carefully in their proper place. “Certain articles excerpted
from your books,” you say, “are being circulated on this subject. I shall set
them down here in a rough-and-ready order.” Is that really so? You make such a
great show of laboring to settle disturbances raised in God's church, yet did
it not occur to you to ask those hawkers who circulate these slanders first of
all to substantiate their assertions by competent witnesses? Tell me: what
would you say if I declared in a single sentence that everything imputed to
Calvin here is false? You would either suddenly admit that you yourself are
the accuser—a role you disguise as much as you can—or else be forced to
condemn your own imprudence for thinking you had to ask whether the accusation
was just or unjust before asking whether it was true or false. Indeed, as you
will soon understand, most of what you call “articles” are of such a kind that
nothing more than a simple denial is needed. But come: let us suppose that
your supreme zeal for reconciling the church swept you off course. What sort
of business is it, even so, to handle so grave a matter in a rough-and-ready
way? Consider again and again

[Printed p. 339; PDF p. 4; logical scan 363; OCR object 0372]

whether anyone might suspect—for people are shrewd nowadays—that nothing here
is being conducted by you either seriously or in good faith. But since you are
so very clever, you doubtless have a ready answer. Bring it forward, then, lest
while seeking to help your friends you instead lose your reputation for wisdom
and modesty.

### Heading of the calumnies

“Articles on Predestination excerpted from John Calvin's books, both Latin and
French.”

### Response

You ought rather to have used this heading: **Calumnies shamelessly and falsely
ascribed to John Calvin by certain unlearned and malicious men.**

### The first article, that is, the first calumny

“God created the greater part of the world for destruction by the bare and
sheer choice of his will.”

### Refutation

How false this calumny is will appear as follows from Calvin's own writings.
For he says: “I say with Augustine that those whom the Lord undoubtedly
foreknew would go to destruction were created by him, and that this happened
because he so willed. Why he willed it, however, it is not ours to demand an
account, since we cannot comprehend it; nor is it fitting for the divine will
to descend into controversy before us. Whenever that will is discussed, the
supreme rule of justice is designated by its very name.” Again: “We say what
Scripture clearly shows: the Lord by his eternal and immutable counsel once
determined whom he would in due time take into salvation and whom in turn he
would devote to destruction. We affirm that this counsel, as regards the
elect, is founded on his free mercy, with no regard for human worthiness; but
that to those whom he assigns to condemnation, the entrance to life is closed
by his just and irreproachable, yet incomprehensible, judgment.” Again: “If we
are not ashamed of the gospel, we must confess what is openly taught there:
God, by his eternal good pleasure whose cause depends on nothing else,
destined to salvation those whom he saw fit, rejecting the others; and he
illumines by his Spirit those whom he honored with free adoption, so that they
receive the life offered in Christ, while the others are voluntarily
unbelieving in such a way that, deprived of the light of faith, they remain in
darkness.” Again, as Augustine also writes: “God's will is so completely the
supreme rule of justice that whatever he wills must be considered just by the
very fact that he wills it. When it is therefore asked why the Lord acted so,
the answer must be, ‘Because he willed it.’ If you go on to ask why he willed
it, you seek something greater and higher than God's will, and such a thing
cannot be found.” Again: “We must always return to the sole choice of the
divine will, whose cause is hidden in himself.” Again: “The Apostle did not
look about for underground escape routes as though he had been caught; rather,
he indicated that the rationale of divine justice is too high either to be
measured in a human fashion or to be comprehended by the weakness of the human
mind.” These are Calvin's words, and there are very many others of this kind
throughout his writings. From them, I say, one can understand perfectly well
that whatever you recount about God's bare choice is said slanderously.

For people call a choice “bare” when something is determined without any
regard for whether the thing determined is just or unjust. We, however,
confess with Paul that all things occur by the counsel of the divine will, so
that God, as one who chooses whom he wills, loves or hates those not yet born,
before they have done anything good or evil, and accordingly in due time has
mercy or hardens. But we add that there is no injustice in God on that account:
he determines these things in such a way that he nevertheless does nothing
except justly. We prove this by many arguments. First, God's will is the
supreme rule of justice. The inference that something is unjust because human
reason sees no cause for justice of that kind does not follow. What then? Does
the sun fail to shine because a blind man lives in darkness? Second, we say
that those people act foolishly who wish something to be just in itself before
God wills it. On the contrary, God must first will something before it can be
just. God's will does not depend on the qualities of things; instead, the
qualities of things follow God's will in the order of causes. Third, we also
add that in a decree of this kind, by which God from eternity destined
whichever vessels of his wrath he pleased, the execution of the counsel must
be distinguished from the counsel itself—that is, the purpose of reprobating
must be distinguished from

[Printed p. 340; PDF p. 5; logical scan 364; OCR object 0373]

condemnation. For we understand the cause of the counsel to be nothing other
than God's own will, which cannot fail to be perfectly just even if the blind
wisdom of the flesh decides otherwise. The Lord, however, has amply disclosed
to us the causes of the execution—that is, of condemnation—namely, Adam's
voluntary corruption transmitted to us and the fruits of that corruption. But
here human reason rises up again. “From where,” it asks, “did those causes of
condemnation come? Did they occur against God's will?” Certainly not, for then
he would not be omnipotent. They occurred with his will, therefore. He does not
permit unwillingly; and to posit an idle foreknowledge in God is the dogma of
the Epicureans, overturned by innumerable testimonies of Scripture. “But if he
decreed that these things should happen in this way, and I could not resist his
decree, am I not free of blame, with all the blame resting upon him?” This is
the knot which human beings cannot untie, except those who have learned from
God's own Word to think reverently about him. Accordingly, when Paul reaches
this point, he does not deny that this is God's will, which the ungodly cannot
resist. Instead, he emphatically denies the conclusion which the ungodly drew
from this assumed axiom, because God cannot be unjust; and he calls us back to
consider our human condition so that we may adore so sublime a mystery, much
less condemn it.

But are you wiser than the Apostle? For you except those things as not having
happened by God's will. If that is so, it is astonishing that it did not occur
to the Apostle to make precisely that answer. Yet you do not notice where this
headlong rashness carries you: namely, into entangling yourself more and more
while supposing that God's infinite wisdom can be comprehended by you. Let us
grant what you ask, that the causes of condemnation occur without any
preceding decree of God—I say the causes of condemnation, not condemnation
itself. See how many absurd and impious consequences follow.

First, if they occurred against his will, you abolish God's omnipotence, since
he could not prevent what he wished to prevent. But if they occurred while he
was unconcerned, how far are you from Epicureanism? It remains, therefore,
that he willingly permitted them, and the same difficulty returns. If you say
that our sins, whether foreknown or already committed, supplied a cause for
this will, you have said nothing. For we are asking about the source of those
very sins, namely, about the causes of corruption. You can no more exclude
God's decree from those causes than when the fruits of corruption are under
discussion, since here too the same difficulties surround you and you are held
in precisely the same labyrinth.

Consider besides how finely you defend God's justice. Whatever he permitted,
even if he did not decree it—let us grant you that point—he could nevertheless
have prevented by a mere nod. Why, then, did he not restrain Satan in time? Why
did he not confirm Adam and Eve completely? Or, if you prefer not to go all the
way back to them, why did he not command that fountain of sin to remain in
Adam, lest it flow down to his descendants? Was it because the descendants'
sins deserved this? But those sins would never have existed if corruption had
not been transmitted to them. Why does he daily loosen Satan's reins when he
can hold them? Whichever way you turn, human reason certainly dictates that a
person who can prevent someone from rushing to destruction and nevertheless
does not prevent him is no less at fault than one who pushes him over the edge
himself. Thus even if your solution held, God would not cease to appear unjust
to human reason.

“But,” you will say, “these questions are prying.” They certainly are, and for
that reason we account it sacrilege for anyone to creep into these divine
secrets. Yet we also say it is prying, and indeed impious, to ask how God is
not at fault if he orders the causes of condemnation. You have undertaken to
explain that question, whereas with the Apostle we judge it inexplicable to
human understanding. Which of us, then, is prying? Which is sacrilegious? Which
searches God's secrets outside his Word? Is it you, who investigate the
rationale of divine wisdom and thereby knowingly and deliberately plunge
yourself over a precipice? Or is it rather I, who, when God's will is reached,
remember the human condition and silently adore God's majesty?

Let this, then, finally close the matter and mark the furthest boundary of
human judgment. Although the cause of the decree by which God determined from
eternity to create some people in whose just destruction he would manifest the
glory of his justice is inscrutable, and although for that reason everyone
destined to destruction cannot fail to perish in due time, God is nevertheless
free from every fault in their condemnation because he condemns none but the
corrupt and guilty. He justly decrees even those things which human beings do
unjustly, and therefore also punishes justly. Even if human reason cannot bear
this, it does not follow that it is false. Let everything that is flesh give
way—I prefer to speak so rather than in your fashion—so that we may rest
wholly in God's Word alone.

“But where is God's Word?” you will say. You certainly cannot deny that in
handing down these things we have followed, as it were, in Paul's footprints.
We shall nevertheless consider this when we engage you at close quarters. For
now I shall have fulfilled my duty if I point out to every reader, as with a
finger, how empty these calumnies are. Therefore, to return to the point from
which I digressed, I shall set two further passages from Calvin against your
calumnies. He writes as follows: “ALTHOUGH before Adam's defection God had
determined for

[Printed p. 341; PDF p. 6; logical scan 365; OCR object 0374]

hidden reasons what he was going to do, we nevertheless read in Scripture that
nothing but sin is condemned by him. It remains, therefore, that he had just
but hidden reasons for rejecting part of humanity, and that he hates and
condemns nothing in a human being except what is alien to his justice.” Again:
“WE LEARN that God's providence must be considered by us in such a way that we
give praise and glory to his omnipotence. For God's wisdom and justice must
always be joined to his power. Just as the Scriptures therefore teach that
the Lord does this or that by his justice and wisdom, so they teach that there
is a definite end for which he does it. The invention of the Schoolmen about
God's absolute power is an execrable blasphemy. It is the same as if they said
God were some tyrant who determines a thing according to whim rather than
equity. Their schools are filled with such blasphemies, and they are not
unlike the pagans who said that God plays with human affairs. But in Christ's
school we are taught that God's justice shines in his works, of whatever kind
they may be, so that every mouth may be stopped and glory given to him alone.”

Do you recognize at last, you slanderer, what kind of conscience you had when
you stitched these articles together? Yet you added other calumnies besides.
First, where Calvin said that part of humanity is rejected for just but hidden
reasons, you omit all mention of the reasons and say that we teach the greater
part of humanity was created for destruction. We do not, however, wish to
quarrel about the number. From the Scriptures and the constant experience of
every age, we know that by far the greater number enter by the broad and
spacious road which leads to destruction. You nevertheless do not cease to be
a slanderer, because in making the charge you add something of your own.

Next, you write as if we said that the purpose for which the reprobate were
created was their eternal ruin. I refute that calumny in Calvin's own words:
“IT OUGHT to be agreed among everyone, as Solomon says, that God made all
things for himself, even the wicked for the evil day. Since the disposition of
all things is in God's hand, and the decision of salvation and death resides
with him, he so orders matters by his counsel and nod that among human beings
some are born from the womb devoted to certain death, who by their destruction
glorify his name.” To conclude briefly, therefore, Calvin does not think that
the reprobate were created simply in order that they might perish, but that,
perishing through their own fault, they might vindicate God's judgment. He
also thinks their destruction depends on God's predestination in such a way
that the entire cause and material of destruction is found in themselves, and
that although this decree of God is incomprehensible to human understanding,
it is nevertheless just. Why, then, do you omit all that is said about the
fault of those who perish and about God's glory? But come, let us hear the
solid arguments by which you yourself attack your calumny.

### Castellio's arguments against Article I

“They say that the first article is contrary to nature and to Scripture. They
argue from nature as follows: Every animal naturally loves its own offspring.
But this nature is from God. It follows that God loves his own offspring. For he
would not cause animals to love their offspring unless he too loved his own.
They prove this by the following reasoning. The Lord said, ‘Shall I cause
birth, and not myself give birth?’ It is as though he said, ‘What I cause
others to do, I myself also do. I cause others to give birth; therefore I too
give birth.’ From this they draw a similar argument: God causes animals to
love their offspring; therefore he loves his own. But all human beings are
God's offspring, for God is the father of Adam, from whom all human beings
were born. Therefore he loves all human beings.”

### Refutation

First of all, I say that you argue ineptly in this place about God's love or
hatred for human beings. For the question here is not whether he hates some,
but whether he has rejected some. Although those two things are distinct, you
have nevertheless judged them to be the same. I shall speak more plainly so
that you cannot complain of obscurity. We say that the Lord established a
definite order of causes. Although he beholds all things at once as present,
he nevertheless willed some causes to follow and others to precede in that
order—an order of causes which the philosophers too recognized and called
*hypallēlon*, or subordination. We therefore affirm that, in the order of
causes, God's hatred toward reprobate human beings does not precede God's
eternal decree, which we rightly place at the highest level of all causes, but
rather follows it. Thus, although the Lord in due time hates everyone whom he
predestines from eternity to destruction, to predestine to destruction is not
itself to hate, but to destine to his hatred. At the same time, when the Lord
destines to his hatred whom he pleases, he lays beneath it the just causes of
his future hatred, so that the whole material of that hatred inheres in the
human beings destined to destruction, and consequently his justice shines in
pursuing them with hatred and finally condemning them. But you err in this:
you suppose that God first

[Printed p. 342; PDF p. 7; logical scan 366; OCR object 0375]

hates someone and only then destines him to destruction. That is just as
inept as saying that he begins to hate before deciding whom he is going to
hate. The matter can therefore be settled with you in one sentence. We agree
thus far: both sides confess that God hates and condemns nothing in human
beings except sin and guilt. We disagree in this: you think God's hatred is
the cause for which God destines some to destruction, whereas we say that this
hatred is not the cause of the divine decree but its effect.

It does not stand in our way that Paul, when reciting Malachi's words, “Esau I
hated,” seems to take hatred for the eternal decree of reprobation. I say that
the word “I hated” in that passage means nothing other than “I decreed to
hate,” since Paul discusses God's decree, not the execution of that decree.
Nor are clear testimonies of Scripture lacking by which we confirm this
interpretation. “He chose us,” Paul says, “before the foundations of the world
were laid”—that is, he determined to choose us. You must surely admit that God
executes in time what he ordained before all time. Again: “God's grace was
given to us through Christ Jesus before eternal times”—that is, before
eternal times God purposed to give us his grace in Christ Jesus, although he
does not actually give it to us before he effectually calls us to himself.
Thus the Lamb is said to have been slain from the foundation of the world,
insofar as he was foreordained before the world was made and was manifested in
the last times, as Peter himself explains. But why does Scripture speak this
way? So that we may understand what the Lord decreed would happen to be as
certain as we are certain that what we see accomplished has been done.

That matters stand as we say—that God's decree precedes both love and hatred
in the order of causes—is also plainly evident from this: otherwise you will
be forced to place the causes of the divine decree in human beings themselves.
It follows that God either did not deliberate beforehand about what he would
do with human beings until he had created them and idly foreseen that they
would fall; or, if he had deliberated, that after human nature changed he
willed to change his counsel. No one fails to see how alien both alternatives
are from God, the supremely wise artisan, endowed with an invariable and, so
to speak, most constant nature. Moreover, if matters stood this way—if God
first hated some because of foreknown corruption or some of its fruits and
only afterward destined them to destruction—why was it necessary for Paul to
exclaim, “O man, who are you?” and “O the depth of the riches of the wisdom and
knowledge of God! How inscrutable are his judgments, and his ways past finding
out!”? Why, I ask, was it necessary to set God before us as a potter who forms
whichever vessels he pleases for honor or dishonor? The solution you offer was
ready to hand: it is no wonder that the Lord destines some to destruction, and
they complain of God's will without merit, since their own sins virtually
compel him to do it. Far be it from us to use an answer so absurd, so inept,
and so alien to God's whole nature. Let us say instead that it is the Lord who
has done whatever he willed, and indeed has done all things justly, since his
will is itself the rule of justice.

Besides, to what do all your arguments finally tend? Is it not to show that
God loves all human beings? If that is so, what remains but either that no one
will be condemned or that those whom God loves will nevertheless perish? If
you object that God eventually hates on account of sin those whom he loved at
the beginning, you tumble over another precipice. For if he loved them, he
surely also willed to save them. Why, then, does he not save them? Evidently
because their sins caused him to destroy those whom he had formerly willed to
save. What a marvelous God you invent: one who changes from day to day and
hour to hour; whose decision human beings overturn whenever they please; and
the outcome of whose counsels depends wholly, not on an eternal and immutable
decree, but on secondary causes! Indeed, if we follow your opinion, the human
will would be not a secondary but the primary cause of God's decree, so that,
as Augustine nicely says, the judgment would belong not to the potter but to
the clay. See where those crowd-pleasing notions carry you: what is written
has plainly happened to you, namely, that those who are not content with the
simple Word but curiously search God's secrets will be swallowed up by his
majesty.

But lest we seem to evade the issue, come, let us examine your arguments. You
argue as follows: “This doctrine is contrary to God's nature; therefore it is
false and impious.” I deny the minor premise, which you confirm as follows:
“God causes birth; therefore he gives birth. Similarly, God causes animals to
love their offspring; therefore he himself loves his own. Now all human beings
are God's offspring, because God is the father of Adam from whom all human
beings were born. Therefore he loves all human beings.” Those are your
arguments. Hear now in turn what I answer.

First of all, I say that the Prophet's passage about God's giving birth is
either maliciously or imprudently twisted by you away from its subject.

[Printed p. 343; PDF p. 8; logical scan 367; OCR object 0376]

For there the Prophet is dealing not with the common creation of all human
beings, but with the restoration of the church alone, that is, with the free
adoption of the elect in Christ. What sort of judgment is it to accommodate to
creation what is said about adoption? What is it to transfer to all human
beings what pertains to the elect alone, so that you share the elect's
peculiar grace even with the disowned and illegitimate? “But then,” you will
say, “who is the father of the rest?” The very one whom Christ expressly
named when, addressing those illegitimate and adulterous sons, he said, “You
are of your father the devil.” You have no cause to cry out that this makes me
a Manichaean. I refer this begetting of the devil's children not to creation,
but to the corruption of nature. God ordered that corruption in such a way
that he is not its author; rather, the devil and the human will, which freely
corrupted itself, are. “Then, as far as creation is concerned,” you will say,
“God is also their father.” I admit it. “Therefore he loves them,” you will
add. I flatly deny this, and shall soon prove it false. For now I ask only how
appropriately you cited the Prophet's testimony.

Next, what madness is this—to establish in God himself whatever God has placed
in animals? It is certainly astonishing that, so that you may prove nothing
which the unlearned populace cannot grasp, you do not also ascribe to God a
head, arms, hands, and even a womb. It would be astonishing if you did not
think he literally hears, sees, desires and digests food—as some have babbled
about the stars—or at least thinks, reasons, desires, and grows angry. “No,”
you will say, “I speak only of those things which can belong to God.” Then you
ought to have defined which those things are. Yet turn yourself in every
direction: you will never find anything attributed to animals which is said of
God in the same manner. I do acknowledge that Scripture—such is God's
kindness—sometimes lisps with us like a nurse, but nevertheless so as to
observe a certain discrimination. You therefore ought also to have bounded
your analogy by its proper limits.

I also acknowledge that the *storgai physikai*, the natural affections found
in created nature and especially in human beings, are striking proofs of
God's goodness. Yet who in his right mind would grant you that God loves those
whom he loves in no other manner than human beings love one another? Learn
from these examples how false and ridiculous that is. Which of us would want
children like toads, snakes, or spiders to be born to him? Yet the Lord, if we
may speak in your fashion, gives birth to all these things every day, and no
created thing exists which he does not cherish in his incredible goodness.
What then? Would you wish a lame child, a blind child, or a dull-witted child
to be born to you? Yet the Lord sets spectacles of this kind before our eyes
every day. What, moreover, when he raises up certain people in whom to display
his power, as Moses says, or when he forms vessels for dishonor, as Paul
writes? Indeed, what is more, when he wills that those sons dearest to him—I
do not mean that he chastens them for sins, but that for his glory he wills
the just to be oppressed, tortured, and finally killed with the greatest
cruelty by the unjust, as Scripture everywhere speaks and experience
confirms—will you reconcile this love of God with the inclinations of human
nature?

When the disciples saw the man born blind, they asked Jesus who had sinned,
the man himself or his parents, so that he was born blind. Jesus answered that
neither the man nor his parents had sinned—that is, that he was born blind
neither because of his own sins nor those of his parents, but so that God's
works might be manifested in him. What will you set against this? Here your
refuge in foreseen sins fails you. A father who was also a physician would be
deemed cruel if he—I will not say afflicted his son with diseases, but even,
to speak in your fashion, allowed him to be afflicted—so that by curing them
he might gain some reputation.

Do you see that these thoughts about God are bestial whenever God's love or
hatred is measured by the rule of created nature? It was no different with
David when he fell into these same thoughts and judged God's hatred and love
in a human manner. “I became like a beast before you,” he said, until he
entered God's sanctuary. You therefore ought to have perceived God's nature
before discussing his love or hatred.

For us who live in this world two paths lie open to the knowledge of God. The
contemplation of created things opens one of them, which Paul points out to the
Romans and Athenians when discussing the first principles of the Christian
religion. God has opened the other to us in his Word. I admit that neither
should be neglected; but how much more certain the latter is than the former
David proves by his own example, and, if not from ourselves, it may at least
be amply understood from the continual observation of every age. Who handed
down notions about God more alien to him than those wise Egyptians by whom
Greece was afterward instructed? Or from where, if not from those very men,
was idolatry born? Read Apollonius's conversations with the Brahmans and
Gymnosophists, and we shall learn what human theology is. Indeed, how often
does even the divine Plato speak foolishly, and how frequently does he depart
from those first principles which they call *koinai ennoiai*, common notions?
Those final words of the dying Socrates, “We owe a cock to Asclepius,” show
plainly enough how solid Socrates' knowledge of divine things was—unless you,
perhaps, think

[Printed p. 344; PDF p. 9; logical scan 368; OCR object 0377]

he began raving as he lay dying, and that Plato even wished to commit his
teacher's ravings to writing; or unless Socrates had not even then ceased
being ironic. Without doubt, however, Paul's words are true: “They became
futile in their reasonings, and their foolish heart was darkened; while they
supposed themselves wise, they became fools.” Likewise: “The world in God's
wisdom did not know God.” When Paul wrote these things, he certainly did not
condemn nature, which daily declares God's immense works to us, as David says;
he accused our blindness, which can be healed by God alone, and indeed by
hearing his Word. Those who do not consider this justly reap this fruit of
their ignorance: because they try to subject God's nature to the laws of this
created nature, they offer themselves to be mocked even by children. “But,”
you will say, “I present Scripture afterward.” I admit it; but you subject
Scripture to those axioms you have gathered from contemplating visible
things. You should have done the reverse: you should accept the axioms which
the order of nature suggests only insofar as they agree with the authority of
Scripture. We shall consider that shortly. Let this, then, be the sum of the
answer: God indeed loves his offspring, but in his own manner, which he has
revealed to us in his Word insofar as it concerned us.

I come to another claim written no less absurdly. “All human beings,” you say,
“are God's offspring, because God is the father of Adam, from whom all human
beings were born. Therefore God loves all human beings.” I answer first that
you act contrary to Scripture's usage when you attribute the name “father” to
God indiscriminately and with respect to all human beings at once; that is,
you share even with outsiders what belongs peculiarly to the one church in
Christ. Some wrongly accommodate that passage in Malachi to the common
procreation of all human beings, whereas it refers rather to Israel's one
common father, whether Abraham or Jacob, as is evident from a similar passage
of Moses. But lest I appear to quarrel over words—although this dispute is not
about nothing—let it be that the Lord created everyone in Adam and is thus far
the common father of all. If this is a sufficiently firm reason why God loves
everyone, how is it that the Apostle declares, to the contrary, that in Adam
everyone is a child of wrath and death, and consequently of hatred? Will you
answer that the Apostle considers human beings insofar as they are corrupt in
Adam and therefore disowned? Let it be so, for that is indeed the case. But
how do you consider them? Surely not as still standing in Adam, since you do
not say that God *was* the father of all but that he *is*, nor that he *loved*
all but that he *loves* them.

You must therefore flee to another position, namely, that God nevertheless
loves everyone because he offers grace to everyone. But if this is so, not
even foreseen corruption caused the Lord to disown any, since he so loves
everyone, although corrupt, that he offers them the grace of salvation—and in
such a way that he gives them the power to embrace the offered grace, since
otherwise the Lord would seem to us, as I shall say later in its place, not to
deal with human beings in good faith. Obstinacy must therefore be added to
corruption: that is, the offered grace must further be rejected. Yet if that
were so, we would not be born children of wrath and death, but would become so
from the time when we rejected the offered grace. Thus, as the Anabaptists
after Pelagius and that excellent little brother of yours, Servetus, teach,
children would not need God's grace in Christ, since they are not disowned by
nature. In short, what is it to abolish original sin, or at least so diminish
it that it is not an adequate cause of disowning, if this is not?

What, by contrast, does David say? “Behold, I was conceived in iniquities.”
Show, then, that not every iniquity deserves death before God. And what does
the Lord himself say? “What is born of the flesh is flesh.” The flesh,
moreover, is God's enemy, because those who are in the flesh cannot please
God. Either prove, therefore, that before they reject grace human beings are
not flesh; or confess that from conception itself everyone is guilty of wrath
and death, although in his singular goodness the Lord has mercy on whomever he
pleases. Far less can it be inferred that because God created everyone in
Adam, he therefore loves as a father everyone born from Adam. On the contrary,
I say that after Adam sinned, God ceased in Adam to be the father of the human
race, since he rightly and deservedly disowned everyone in Adam as corrupt;
but that he began to be the father of those whom he chose from eternity in the
second Adam, that is, in Christ. Christ himself plainly indicates this when,
addressing the Canaanite woman, he distinguishes the children from the dogs.
And what does the Apostle do? To prove that sin entered the world through one
human being, not by imitation, as Pelagius impiously thought, but by
propagation, he ascends from death to sin—that is, from the effects to the
cause. “For death reigned,” he says, “from Adam to Moses, even over those who
had not sinned in the likeness of Adam.” What answer do you have here? But so
that the proper sense may be understood more plainly of what you twisted from
the writings of certain learned men after drawing it

[Printed p. 345; PDF p. 10; logical scan 369; OCR object 0378]

from them—namely, that grace is offered to everyone—I think one must first ask
what you mean by grace. You are not ignorant that this name can include any
benefit of God; but this discussion absolutely requires you, unless you wish
to wander from the point, to understand by the name *grace* nothing other than
free reconciliation in Christ. With those whose writings you do not
understand, we indeed confess that this grace is now offered, not as formerly
to the one Jewish nation, but indiscriminately to nations everywhere, to every
class of human beings, and finally not only to the elect but also to very many
reprobate persons, although in a different respect. But how does this help you?

Your answer will not hold unless you say that the grace of salvation is
offered not only to all people generally but also to every single human being
without any exception. If you except even one person, your argument that God
loves everyone whom he created in Adam will limp. Surely you would not dare
deny that there has always been an innumerable multitude of human beings who
left this life before hearing anything of the gospel's doctrine? Besides, how
many are presented with external grace but are not drawn? For that latter
benefit is peculiar to the children. Indeed, even if you give the name *grace*
to the natural knowledge of God, it will not be true of every individual. How
many since the foundation of the world have either passed from the womb to the
grave or lived deprived of the common light of understanding? It remains,
therefore, that since every individual is born a child of wrath and the grace
of salvation is not offered to every individual, God does not love every
individual although he created every individual in Adam—contrary to the
position I see you wish to maintain.

Furthermore, if, as you wish, God loves everyone because he created everyone
in Adam, his love toward everyone will certainly be equal, since he is the
father of all for the same reason. Answer this: do you think he is Abraham's
father in no different way than he is the father of the Chaldeans or of other
nations? “God is known in Judah,” David says, and elsewhere, “He has not dealt
so with any nation.” From where, then, comes this distinction? It comes either
from God's will, where we have taught that we must take our stand, or from
human beings themselves, as you prefer. If from human beings, since all are
equally children of wrath, what greater obstinacy could God foresee in these
than in those, on account of which he would offer grace to the one group but
deny it to the other; show mercy to some but harden others, or even, as you
put it, allow them to be hardened? The invention of foreseen corruption,
unbelief, or sins is therefore futile. Consequently, you must say that this
distinction depends on God's will, although the rationale of this justice is
hidden from human beings.

Let us offer another, clearer example. God is equally the father of Esau and
Jacob, and that not merely in Adam but also in Isaac, with whom he established
a covenant of peculiar blessing. Will you dare say that God is the father of
Esau in no different way than of Jacob? Let us grant you that God may also be
regarded as Esau's father. If you dare say there is no difference, the Lord
himself will refute you, crying from heaven, “The elder will serve the
younger.” And lest you evade this by saying it means any ordinary kind of
servitude, the Lord again stands against you, testifying that Jacob's
preeminence was joined with his love, but Esau's rejection with his hatred. A
certain sign of this was that Esau was excluded from the promise of the land
of Canaan, which was the token of divine blessing and adoption. It remains,
therefore, that even if we grant you that God loved Esau as a father, he
nevertheless pursued Jacob with a very different love. Tell me, then: from
where comes this distinction, unless from him who has mercy on whom he wills
and hardens whom he wills, as the Apostle proclaims?

For if you retreat to Esau's foreseen obstinacy, the Apostle stands against
you. He says that Jacob was beloved, and consequently chosen, before he was
born, so that we should know election precedes all things not only in time,
since it is eternal, but also in the order of causes, and so that no place at
all should be left for foreseeing either faith or works, since these are not
the causes but the effects of election. Likewise, when he comes to the
opposite member, he testifies in precisely the same manner that Esau was hated,
and consequently destined to destruction, before he was born or had done
anything evil. This is so that on this side too we may understand not only
that the purpose of reprobating is eternal, but that it is also prior to all
causes of destruction, and that no place is left here for foreseeing
corruption, unbelief, or evil works. These causes of condemnation do inhere in
Esau alone, so that God is in no way implicated in the fault; but they are not
causes preceding the eternal decree of reprobation, but causes subordinated to
it. Otherwise, if you explain Paul's words, “Before they were born or had done
anything good or evil,” in entirely different ways in the one member and the
other, not only will the common axiom that opposites fall under the same
science amply refute you, but everyone will perceive that manifest violence is
done to the Apostle's words when one and the same sentence, joined in the same
words, is torn into wholly contrary interpretations. This will certainly
happen if you say that foreseeing is excluded on the one side but nevertheless

[Printed p. 346; PDF p. 11; logical scan 370; OCR object 0379]

established on the other. Finally, if the purpose of reprobating rested upon
foreseeing—apart from the fact that the same things would necessarily be
foreseen on both sides—from where would the objections which follow arise,
objections which the Apostle struggles so greatly to resolve? The elect would
have no ground for complaint unless they wished to complain about mercy; nor
would the reprobate, if they were rejected on account of their own foreseen
merits. It is one thing to ask why God hates; it is another to ask why he
destined to hatred.

Lest you think I invented all this, hear what Augustine thought about the
matter in chapter 98 of his *Enchiridion to Laurentius*: “Paul, perceiving how
what had been said could trouble those who cannot penetrate by understanding
this height of grace, says, ‘What then shall we say? Is there injustice with
God? By no means.’ For it appears unjust that without any merits of good or
evil works God should love one and hate another. If in this matter he wished
future works to be understood—either the good works of the one or the evil
works of the other, which God certainly foreknew—he would by no means say,
‘Not from works,’ but rather, ‘Not from future works.’ In that way he would
resolve the question; indeed, he would create no question which needed to be
resolved.” So far Augustine. It remains, therefore, that even if God is said
to be equally the father of all human beings in Adam, he still cannot be said
to love human beings equally—indeed, not even to love them all. Consequently,
this entire argument of yours is futile and inept, because God's purpose,
whether in love or in hatred, does not depend on that common benefit of
creation, but on God's eternal and, although hidden, perfectly just will.

### Another argument of Castellio

“To create for destruction belongs not to love but to hatred. Therefore God
created no one for destruction.”

### Refutation

Just as this argument depends on the preceding one, so when the preceding one
is overthrown, this one too must collapse. You wrongly concluded that everyone
is loved by God because everyone was created in Adam. We showed, to the
contrary, that by a decree which is incomprehensible yet just, God destined
whomever he pleased to his hatred and consequently to destruction. This is not
alien to God's nature, which ought least of all to be measured by common human
feeling. We further showed that God is not moved by hatred of someone to
destine him to destruction, but hates the one whom he has destined to
destruction. The cause of this hatred and destruction is known to us: the
voluntary corruption in Adam which, flowing down to his descendants, produces
fruits like the tree. This satisfies every moderate mind. Although the cause
of the divine decree is not known to them, they confess that it is just, both
because it is divine and because destruction is so decreed that at the same
time the just causes of destruction are established as inhering in human
beings themselves. For this reason we also proved that we do not customarily
speak as if anyone were created by God simply for destruction, but so that by
the person's just condemnation the Lord might manifest his own justice. The
condemnation of the reprobate is just because their destruction so depends on
God's predestination that nevertheless the cause and entire material of their
destruction is found in themselves.

Whoever understands these things will at least understand this: your statement
that creating for destruction belongs to hatred is utterly absurd. As I say
again, before the Lord creates human beings—we are speaking of the order of
causes—he neither hates nor loves them; rather, concerning those whom he
creates, he decreed from eternity either to love them in Christ or to hate them
in Adam. To create for destruction therefore does not belong to hatred. It
belongs to the one who decreed to hate, I say, to hate for a just cause which
he so subordinated to the decree of hatred that all the blame resides with the
one whom he hates. Besides, if no one was created so that God might be
glorified by the person's just destruction, then either the Lord determined to
save everyone, or he borrows the causes of his counsel from the foreknown
human will, so that those whom he foreknew would reject the offered grace he
destines to destruction for that reason, or at least permits them to fall
because that is what they will.

But how much is said here both ineptly and impiously! If God determined to save
everyone, as he certainly determined if he loves everyone, how does it happen
that he does not save them? Is it because he cannot? If so, he is not
omnipotent. Is it because he does not wish to? Then the Lord changes his
counsel, and when he says he repents we shall understand this to be said of God
not figuratively and improperly, but truly and properly. But if he seeks the
causes of his purpose from human beings themselves, what sort of craftsman is
this who depends on his own workmanship? Indeed, what sane person would think
to say that before deliberating about the purpose for which he would create
human beings, God first considered what sort they would be and formed his
counsel from that? Moreover, what will become of those to whom grace is never
offered, whom we demonstrated above to be very

[Printed p. 347; PDF p. 12; logical scan 371; OCR object 0380]

many? Will you say they are all saved, since the foreseen cause of rejecting
grace is not present in them? See into what precipices you are carried once
you have strayed from the right path. The right path is this: God made all
things for his glory, even the wicked for the evil day. There are no evasions
and nothing absurd in this doctrine, provided you establish what is most true:
God's will, even if at times it appears otherwise to us, is nevertheless
always the supreme rule of justice. Besides, I ask, how does it happen that
you labor so greatly to excuse the reprobate? Let us instead leave them alone,
I beg you, and leave them to be condemned by the eternal Judge. Let us rather
be occupied in giving thanks to God and magnifying his glory. Although he
could have destined us too to destruction, he instead chose to give us to his
Son, through whom, apprehended by faith, we are freely saved. This benefit of
God toward us shines most brightly by contrast with the reprobate's
destruction. The reprobate are set before us for consideration only so that we
may learn to worship the Lord with fear and trembling, not so that we may
plead their case.

### Another argument of Castellio

“Creation is a work of love, not hatred. Therefore God created all human
beings in love, not hatred.”

### Refutation

Again you strike the same rock, because you do not know how to subordinate
hatred to the decree, and to do so in such a way that the cause of hatred is
interposed between the decree and hatred—a cause whose fault inheres in human
beings alone, as I have now said so often. Furthermore, although we freely
acknowledge that God's goodness shines in the work of creation itself and
that, properly speaking, God hates not his work but its corruption, who will
grant you what you wish to infer from this argument: that because in his
singular goodness God creates even the reprobate, he therefore does not
destine them to his just hatred? Indeed, I shall indulge you here. Not only in
the creation of the reprobate but often throughout their entire life an almost
incredible beneficence of God shines, so that if you look only at the present
condition of affairs and rise no higher, the Lord may appear to favor the
wicked rather than the good. This thought greatly exercised David and deceived
the Epicureans.

But what does the Lord say, speaking about all the reprobate in the person of
Pharaoh? “For this purpose,” he says, “I raised you up,” that is, brought you
into the light, “so that I might declare my power in you”—namely, by mightily
destroying you. I am not unaware that some explain differently the verb “I
raised you up,” which corresponds to the Hebrew word *he'emadtikha*; but the
Apostle's context makes sufficiently clear that it must be explained in this
way, so that one must ascend all the way to creation itself. However you
explain it, it will certainly signify the Lord's benefits in Pharaoh, whom he
had nevertheless decreed from eternity to destroy by a just destruction, and
that because he willed it. Why else would Paul rise all the way to God's will,
rather than say, “He has mercy on whom he wills, and allows those who deserve
it, or whom he foreknew would deserve it, to be hardened”?

Likewise, the Lord destined Esau from eternity to his just hatred, but still
blessed him with the earth's abundance and heaven's dew. Ishmael, the son of
the slave woman, is not heir with the free woman's son, yet he grows into a
great nation. Thus great signs of God's beneficence exist even toward the
reprobate, but this does not yield what you wish: that God did not destine them
to his just hatred. Instead, it proves what Christ says, that God's goodness
is infinite, since he does good even to his enemies; and that the more of
God's benefits the reprobate receive, the more *anapologētous*—without
excuse—they will be. Paul therefore rightly calls us back to contemplate God's
*makrothymia*, or patience, toward the very vessels of wrath, yet he does not
on that account deny that they are vessels of wrath fashioned for destruction.

### Another argument of Castellio

“No beast is so savage—much less a human being—that it would wish to breed an
offspring for misery. How much less would God do so? Would he not be worse than
a wolf? Christ argues in this way: ‘If you, although evil, nevertheless know
how to give good gifts to your children, how much more does God?’ So too your
opponents argue: ‘If Calvin, although evil, would nevertheless not wish to
beget a son for misery, how much less would God?’ These and similar things they
say from nature.”

### Refutation

The Lord declares to the woman, “I shall greatly multiply your pain and your
conception.” He declares to the man, “In the sweat of your face you shall eat
bread until you return to the ground”; with

[Printed p. 348; PDF p. 13; logical scan 372; OCR object 0381]

misery you shall eat the earth's fruits, and it shall sprout thorns and
thistles for you. He declares to the earth itself, “Cursed is the ground
because of you.” The Apostle says that under this burden the entire created
universe labors and groans. Job cries out that a human being is born for
troubles and is filled with calamities in the few days he lives. The wise
Preacher testifies that nothing under heaven is more vain or calamitous than a
human being. David laments this in countless passages. The philosophers,
absorbed in this thought, break out into the blasphemy that nature is a
stepmother to human beings and that it is best not to be born. Common sense
dictates what Pliny truly wrote: nothing is more miserable than a human being,
because he inaugurates life with sufferings. Yet you alone have been found to
deny that a human being is begotten for misery.

“But,” you will say, “you wrong me. I do not simply deny that human beings are
begotten for misery, but that they are begotten or created by God for it.” I
ask you, however: what kind of love is it—for you wish to measure God's nature
by human feeling—when, although you could bless by a mere nod the people who
would otherwise be born miserable, you nevertheless prefer that they be born
calamitous, or at least, to speak in your fashion, permit it? You will perhaps
object that God rightly does this because he is angered by human sins, which
somehow compel him to lay aside paternal affection toward some, so that he no
longer loves everyone when he disowns many. Yet this answer is so far from
extricating you that, on the contrary, you entangle yourself more and more.
First, from where do these sins come if not corruption? Why does God allow the
corruption of the one Adam to be propagated to his descendants? You cannot
invent here a foreknowledge of sins which moved God, because a cause must be
placed before its effects. I shall speak more plainly so that, as you wish,
the common people may understand me. If God wishes no one to be born for
misery, why does he permit them to be born corrupt, since misery necessarily
follows corruption?

Will you not at last give glory to God here? Will you not disavow that
blasphemy that God would be worse than a wolf if he willed to create some
human beings for misery? Will you not finally resolve to rest silently in
God's secret and incomprehensible justice? Will this threefold and most solid
reasoning never tame your ferocity? Since God is just, he can do nothing
unjustly in his works, although he does infinitely many things that seem
unjust to us because we cannot grasp their causes. God does not simply create
anyone for misery, but at the same time orders the causes of that misery so
that the one born miserable is deservedly born miserable. In the purpose of
reprobating, God does not regard the destruction of the reprobate as the
ultimate end, but his own glory, which shines in their just condemnation. The
Spirit of God speaks so plainly, and will you continue answering back to God?
Will you never understand what impiety it is to wish to bind God to the laws
of this created nature and to acknowledge no light of wisdom and justice in
him except one whose rays you can endure?

But I shall press you further. Who will deny that blindness is a misery? No
one, I think, unless he is blinder even than the blind themselves. When the
unlearned disciples thought just as you do that the man born blind, whom I
mentioned above, had been born into this misery through either his own fault
or his parents', what did the Lord answer them and you? “Neither this man
sinned,” he says, “nor his parents.” That is, the Lord considered neither the
man's sins nor those of his parents when he willed that the man should be born
blind and consequently miserable; rather, he was born blind so that God's
works might be manifested in him. Go now and accuse the Lord himself of
blasphemy.

What, moreover, of Job, that upright and blameless man, who was treated so
cruelly by Satan that we cannot read what is related about him without horror?
Do you think, I ask, either that he was not then miserable when he burst into
those sorrowful laments; or that he fell into these miseries by chance; or
that God was hostile because of his sins; or that by some idle permission or
connivance God delivered his servant into Satan's hand? You can say none of
these things without being refuted by the narrative's context and by Job
himself, who blesses God and says, “The Lord gave; the Lord took away.” If the
Lord is most just even then, by his servants' own confession, when for his
glory he subjects them for a time to any troubles whatsoever, will you deprive
him of the praise of justice when, likewise for his glory, he creates for just
condemnation those whom he pleased? I say *just* condemnation, since he never
condemns anyone who is not guilty of sin.

Furthermore, the passage you cite proves again how faithfully you ordinarily
explain Scripture. How does it happen that, although up to this point you have
cited only two testimonies of Scripture, you have twisted both so
incompetently? “If you, although evil,” Christ says, “nevertheless know how to
give good gifts to your children, how much more does God?” What do you draw
from this? That because God is good he begets no one for misery. It is as
though the Lord were addressing everyone indiscriminately in that passage,
rather than confirming his church so that with confidence it may cry,

[Printed p. 349; PDF p. 14; logical scan 373; OCR object 0382]

“Abba, Father.” Are you really so ignorant that you do not know this at last
to be the voice of the Spirit of adoption? For so the apostle testifies. Yet
even if your exposition is accepted, what else can you draw from it except
this one thing: that God is accustomed to do good to all? But does it follow
from that that he neither creates vessels of his wrath, which nevertheless he
bears with the greatest patience, nor is accustomed to use his own children
for his glory as he pleases?

You next take Calvin as your hypothesis, who, you say, is evil. We are
certainly not the sort who hold Calvin for God (as you and your companions are
accustomed to prattle). And he is the man—if anyone else beneath heaven today
is—who most bitterly hates the very thing you seek above all, namely human
glory. Yet we can and ought to bear this testimony to the truth: he must be a
most excellent man whom no one in our age hates unless he has declared war on
piety and true religion, or at least has never known him. Finally, would that
your displeasure and that of your party were not so sure a testimony of true
piety, and that you preferred to repent rather than confirm the unfavorable
judgments which all good men have formed of you.

### Another Argument of Castellio

“From Scripture, moreover, they argue as follows: ‘God saw that everything he
had made was very good.’ Therefore the man whom he had made was very good. But
if he had created him for destruction, he had created a good thing for
destruction, and he loves to destroy what is good—something impious even to
think.”

### Refutation

Suppose I reasoned as follows: “God created me healthy; therefore he did not
create me to perish from disease.” Who would not think me mad? I grant that
the Lord created man upright and innocent, and nevertheless for destruction.
First, we are not accustomed to speak in this way, but say that man was
created for God's glory. Second, we distinguish this very point. For we affirm
that God formed some so that he might manifest his glory by saving them
through mercy, and others so that he might manifest it by condemning them in
just judgment. This is how Moses, Solomon, Isaiah, and Paul speak. “Therefore,”
you say, “he had created a good thing for destruction, and he loves to destroy
what is good.” But I say this inference is utterly inept. For you infer much
more than you assumed, and argue just as if you had proved that man was made
by God not only good but also such that he would never become evil. The latter
is false. Because the Lord was justly going to condemn some, their origin did
indeed have to be good: both because God, being good, can create nothing but
what is good, and because, had their origin been evil, he would not justly
condemn what he himself had made. Nevertheless, their origin had to be good in
such a way that, by corrupting itself of its own accord, it would open the way
to just condemnation—that is, to the just execution of God's eternal counsel.
Thus the beginning even of the reprobate was good, not so that the Lord might
condemn them as good, but so that, having become evil of their own accord, he
might justly condemn them in fact and at his own time, just as he decreed from
the beginning. For just as he cannot but love what is good, so he cannot but
hate and punish what is evil.

But also, so that you may learn this incidentally, for the sake of the elect
Adam had to be created good on the condition that he would become evil of his
own accord. For when the Lord had determined to exercise even greater
beneficence in saving his elect than in creating man—that is, when he had
decreed not to create them out of nothing for salvation, but freely to make
them, though deserving every punishment, participants in his eternal kingdom
in Christ—what place would there have been for this grace and incredible
beneficence if they had stood perpetually in Adam? For one who is not
miserable has no need of this mercy. What ground, then, do the elect have to
complain about that mutable condition of Adam, when their salvation is thereby
more glorious and even more fruitful? What ground do the reprobate have, when
they are condemned only as corrupted in Adam? Here I repeat what I have
already said often: the destruction of the reprobate so depends upon God's
decree that nevertheless the entire guilt and matter of their condemnation
inheres in themselves.

“Yet one excuse,” Calvin says finely, “is put forward: because it had been
decreed by God, Adam could not escape. But voluntary transgression is more
than sufficient for guilt. For the secret counsel of God is not the proper and
genuine cause of sin; rather, man's manifest will is. Since man finds the cause
of his evil within, what profit is there in going around to seek it in heaven?
His guilt plainly appears in the fact that he willed to sin. Why, breaking
into heaven's inner recesses, does he plunge himself into a labyrinth? For the
fact that God knowingly and willingly permitted man to fall can have a hidden
reason; it cannot have an unjust one.” See, then, by what right you cry out
that we make God a hater of good things.

[Printed p. 350; PDF p. 15; logical scan 374; OCR object 0383]

### Another Argument of Castellio

“God created one man so that he might place him in paradise, which is blessed
life. Therefore he created all human beings for blessed life. For all were
created in one.”

### Refutation

First I ask where you derived this—that one man was created by God for the
purpose of placing him in paradise. I read that Adam was placed in that most
delightful garden; nowhere do I read that the Lord had proposed this as his
ultimate end, namely, to place him there. Indeed, if we wish to dwell upon the
very words, I read instead that he was created to rule over every kind of
living creature. The basis of your argument therefore collapses, since it
rests not upon Scripture's authority but upon an empty dream. The Lord cast a
deep sleep upon Adam; when he made him, then, did he have this in view? He
sewed him a garment of skins; did he create him for that reason? You see how
ineptly you reason.

“But that paradise,” you will say, “is a type of blessed life.” Tell me, then,
where you derived this too. Is it because the Lord, and after him Paul and
John as well, understand heavenly glory by the name *paradise*? A splendid
argument indeed—as though the Greeks did not use this word for every most
delightful garden, and as though Scripture were not accustomed to shadow forth
eternal happiness with earthly images of this kind. But come, let us grant you
this. In innumerable passages Jerusalem signifies the church; are all who
inhabited that city therefore members of the church? Further, if that paradise
was blessed life, then the devil, death, and sin must also have been in blessed
life; and when that paradise was destroyed through the flood, blessed life
itself must have perished entirely. See where futile sophistries drive a man
headlong.

But lest you be able to complain that I evade your arguments instead of
solving them, I shall explain what we understand from God's Word concerning
this subject—that is, concerning the end for which the first man was created.
We say that God's counsels are understood by us in four ways. One is by
revelation through the prophets, examples of which appear everywhere in the
Scriptures. A second is by a special revelation of the Spirit, as we read of
Simeon and also of all believers, to whose spirit the Spirit of God testifies
what things have been given them by Christ. This is what we call faith and
*plērophoria*, or full assurance. It makes us as certain, after Paul's example,
that we have been freely elected by God in Christ as if we had been present at
his eternal counsel. A third is from the sequence of natural causes observed
by us, in the manner in which natural philosophers, prudent men, and those
experienced in human affairs often predict many things. But in many matters
this conjecture is deceptive and uncertain, because the Lord often bends
intermediate causes according to his hidden will. The fourth way is most
certain—certain without any exception—because, as they say, it judges *a
posteriori*.

I therefore say that there are two things which not only God's Word but common
sense itself dictates to us. One is that God can never change, and therefore
that the very fact that things change so often comes about by God's eternal
and immutable counsel. For God decrees not only things which have a stable
nature, of which there are very few, but also things which change almost every
hour, though only according to laws of change prescribed by him. For example,
the Lord blessed the earth which he had made, but after Adam's sin he curses
it. Did God change? Not at all. At the beginning he had blessed it only for a
time; when that time was completed, he had determined to curse it because of
man's sin. If someone asks where I get this, I shall answer that I get it from
the event. Since God does not change and the event occurred as I have said, we
must also confess that God had so determined from the beginning.

Likewise, through Moses the Lord delivered the ceremonies of the law to his
church; yet later he pronounces through the apostle, “If you are circumcised,
Christ will profit you nothing.” Does the Lord change his counsel so as to
approve what he had previously disapproved? Not at all. He had instituted the
ceremonies only for a time, namely, until the proclamation of Christ. At his
advent they had to vanish like darkness at the rising of the light. Likewise,
he designated Saul king of Israel, but only for a time. Although he did not
announce this at the beginning, he sufficiently demonstrated it when he cast
Saul off. At the same time as he decreed that he would cast him off, he was
establishing the cause by which he would justly take the kingdom from him
through Saul's fault alone. All these things are inferred with certainty from
their outcomes.

The second point, which common sense itself also dictates, is that nothing
happens while God is either ignorant, unwilling, or idle. For neither
ignorance nor weakness belongs to God's nature, nor can an idle foreknowledge
be attributed to him—especially in governing so excellent a work as man—
without the impious dogma of Epicurus being confirmed in very great part,

[Printed p. 351; PDF p. 16; logical scan 375; OCR object 0384]

namely, that mortals are not objects of God's care. For it would then follow
that God idly—that is, without doing anything—allowed Adam to be circumvented
by Satan and finally, as though waking up, hurried to bring a remedy. Scripture
speaks very differently, testifying that God governs even those things which
seem most fortuitous. It proclaims that not even little sparrows fall to the
earth without the will of the heavenly Father, and that all the hairs of our
head are numbered. Satan cannot even touch Job unless God so decrees and,
consequently, grants Satan permission. Indeed, the demons cannot even rage
against the pigs unless they first seek authority from Christ—authority which
he assuredly grants not unwillingly but willingly. It remains, therefore, that
we say everything occurs by God's will and, consequently, that whatever we
see has happened did not happen apart from God's decree, a decree which is
just, eternal, and immutable.

But if someone should cry out here that we introduce Stoic necessity, I
confess with Augustine—or rather with truth itself—that God's will is the
necessity of things; but I deny that this necessity is Stoic. For we do not
bind God to secondary causes, as Homer represents his Jupiter complaining that
although he wishes to resist fate, he cannot. We acknowledge, I say, no fate
of that kind. Rather, we say that the outcome of all things is fixed and
immutable, an outcome which the Lord freely, wisely, and justly established
from eternity. We nevertheless say that secondary causes are not compelled by
God's decree, except when he pleases to restrain his adversaries, but are
carried, already inclined, toward the end established by God. Finally, we not
only do not abolish motions that are purely natural and those that are
voluntary *ek proaireseōs*—from deliberate choice—but number them among the
chief secondary causes. As we have just said, these incline of their own accord
in the direction toward which the counsels of God, the most wise, free, and
just Creator, tend.

Let this be an example. God the Father, providing for the salvation of the
elect, decreed absolutely that Christ should die at a fixed moment. I say that
this so had to occur that it could not have occurred before his hour arrived
(as the Evangelists speak), and that after the appointed time had arrived it
could not have occurred otherwise, so far as the event itself is concerned.
Meanwhile, when Christ was still flourishing in the prime of life and was
without doubt in the best physical condition, if you consider separately the
*dynamis*, or capacity, of his nature, it was such that he could have remained
alive for many more years. His human will likewise had a nature which would
shrink in horror from a most cruel death joined with the wrath of God the
Father, and would even pray for its removal. Thus, if you consider the capacity
of the nature in itself, we shall say that Christ could have not died; likewise,
if you consider the *dynamis* of his will in itself, that he could have desired
not to die. Yet both the nature, by a natural motion, and the will, of its own
accord, consented to God's decree—that is, to the necessary death.

There is therefore no ground for you to charge us with the Stoics' dogma. That
dogma removes from God both the freedom to decree and the freedom to establish
secondary causes; that is, it strips God of his divinity, reduces him to the
ordinary order, and leaves no room for counsels or voluntary deliberations.
Nor is it an objection to us (to say this in passing) that force sometimes
seems to be applied to secondary causes, as when the sun halts its course and
the sea is divided. For we have said that God is not bound to secondary causes
so as to be unable to bend them wherever he pleases. Nor is there any doubt
that nature then yields of its own accord to its commanding Creator.

There is more difficulty in the corrupt will of the devil and of all who have
not been born again—a will which, it is certain, sometimes knowingly and
deliberately opposes itself completely to God's decrees. Here that
incomprehensible and inexplicable character of God's counsels plainly appears,
since the very will of the devil and his members, in the very act by which it
wholly opposes itself to God, nevertheless unwittingly serves God's works no
less than if it obeyed him with a headlong impulse. For consider, to select one
famous example from many: while Satan tries to destroy Joseph through his own
brothers, though God had determined to preserve the Israelites through him,
what did Satan accomplish? Precisely what the one whom he opposed had decreed:
that Joseph, having obtained authority in Egypt, should prepare a refuge for
the church of God. Therefore Joseph, looking to God's decree and comforting
his astonished brothers, says, “You did not send me here, but God.”

What I said, therefore, about secondary causes accommodating themselves, as
already inclined, to God's will must be understood as follows where the
corrupt will of the wicked is concerned: we are to regard the outcome rather
than the form of the will itself. But perhaps these matters have been pursued
more deeply than necessary. I therefore return to the subject. We have shown
that everything comes about from God's eternal and immutable decree, because
neither ignorance, impotence, *ameleia* or neglect, nor any change of counsel
belongs to him. We have also shown that those very things which change are
changed only by God's eternal and immutable counsel. And we have proved this
third point, which depends upon the preceding ones: the following inference is
most firm—“This happened in this way; therefore God's counsel was that it
should happen in this way.” From these points it is easy

[Printed p. 352; PDF p. 17; logical scan 376; OCR object 0385]

to infer, I say, what must be thought about the end for which the first man was
created. The outcome shows that he was created good, but on this condition:
at a fixed moment in time, of his own accord and through no fault of the
Creator, he would lose this goodness and unwittingly open the way both for
those to be saved through mercy, by whose salvation God decreed from eternity
to make his glory illustrious, and for those to be justly condemned, by whose
just destruction thereafter he purposed from eternity to manifest the
immensity of his justice and severity. Adam was therefore created good. If you
regard this condition of his nature in itself—that is, his integrity and
innocence—both he himself could and ought to have remained safe, and so could
his descendants whom he would have begotten like himself. But if you consider
