God's decree—that is, the end for which he made him good—you will find that,
so far as the outcome is concerned, Adam could not have remained good, nor
could his descendants have been born in any condition other than the very one
to which God had purposed from eternity that they should be born subject.

You ask where I get these things. I answer that I infer them from the outcome,
or *a posteriori*, as they say. Nor can you deny what I have said without
making God either variable and inconstant, weak, ignorant, or—insofar as the
ordering of causes is concerned—idle. I add, rash and imprudent as well. What,
then? Before he made man, did he propose no fixed end to himself for making
him, but only after the fall had been foreseen did he deliberate what he would
do with humanity? Who, I ask, could calmly endure such things being said about
God, things which would not apply even to human beings unless they were bereft
of all intelligence and judgment? But if he had proposed some end to himself,
I ask you what it was. You will say, “To bless all human beings.” Explain,
then, how God's counsels are not uncertain and weak, how God is not variable
and inconstant, and how he is not utterly powerless, if what he had determined
concerning the blessed life of all human beings is now in no way accomplished.
You cannot even say that those whom he saves are saved by that imaginary
decree of yours, since they are saved by grace, not nature. And there is no
doubt that very many are consigned to eternal punishments, since there is no
salvation outside Christ and faith comes by hearing.

“But it is by their own fault,” you will say. I grant it, but how does this
relieve you? For if God's decree was to save Adam and everyone made in him,
then surely God either does not will or is not able to accomplish what he had
decreed. If he does not will it, then he changed his counsel. If he cannot,
then he is not omnipotent. You see how foreign either alternative is to God's
nature. Thus, even on your own hypothesis, the kind of doctrine that drives us
into those blasphemies is false.

I suspect, nevertheless, that one further refuge remains for you: you may say
that God did indeed make Adam and all human beings comprehended in him for
blessed life, but under the condition that Adam should persist in his
integrity, something that lay within his power, and therefore that God does
not appear to have changed his counsel. I answer that what you say about the
condition is indeed true, but not in the sense you suppose. For God is not to
be thought, like human beings, to hang in suspense in his counsels and to
decree this or that according as secondary causes please. What kind of God
would depend upon the choice of created things? Thus, what is said about the
condition must be understood in a wholly contrary sense: Adam was created with
the condition of falling interposed, a condition which would certainly be
fulfilled—voluntarily, freely, and of his own accord if you consider Adam
himself, but necessarily if you take account of the divine decree, since God's
counsel cannot fail.

For God's decree did not depend upon Adam's will. On the contrary, Adam's will
depended upon God's decree. Yet his will was not compelled by the force of
that decree, but was carried by its own spontaneous motion toward the end
established by God, although Adam himself neither knew nor aimed at that end
and therefore sinned. That end was that a way should be paved both for God's
mercy and for his severe judgment.

For, as Augustine rightly writes, God so established the lower causes, among
which is man's will itself, that from them the thing of which they are causes
may come to be, but need not come to be. Yet he so concealed the higher and
remote causes that from them it must come to be, which he caused to be
possible from those interior causes. Thus, in one and the same work Adam, of
his own accord and therefore sinning, was attempting one thing, while the Lord
was undertaking another. The attempts of Adam and Satan were frustrated,
because there is no counsel against the Lord; but God, justly making use even
of a sinful instrument, accomplished precisely what he willed, because
whatever he determines absolutely must occur, and occur in the very manner in
which he determined it.

If you are not satisfied by these things, which rest upon these necessary
principles—that God is always just, even if human beings do not always
understand how he can be just; that God does not depend upon secondary causes
but always orders them justly, even when they act unjustly; that God does
nothing imprudently or without a fixed end established, neither is ignorant of
anything, nor wills or decrees anything he cannot accomplish, nor idly watches
what is going to happen or what is happening, but orders all things (as
Solomon says) unto

[Printed p. 353; PDF p. 18; logical scan 377; OCR object 0386]

his glory, and consequently decreed beforehand that they should do so; that
God cannot change, but even the things which change are changed by his
immutable counsel with all their circumstances fixed and certain—if, I say,
these things do not satisfy you, though they rest upon principles so certain
and so consonant with God's nature, and moreover upon innumerable testimonies
and examples of Scripture, I frankly say that I no longer take any pains to
satisfy you and your party. For anyone who does so surely acts just as if, as
the saying goes, he wished to be mad with reason on his side.

### Another Argument of Castellio

“If all fell in Adam, it is necessary that all stood in Adam, and under the
same condition as Adam.”

### Refutation

I grant you all these things, and indeed in such a way that they tell against
you. For since Adam's descendants stood under no condition other than that
under which Adam stood—namely, that of his own accord he would cast himself
headlong into destruction, from which the Lord would afterward rescue all
whom he had decreed from eternity to give to his Son—the very thing we maintain
follows. The elect have no reason to complain about this decree of God, since
their condition is now better than it would have been if Adam had not sinned,
insofar as it is more excellent to be saved in Christ the Son of God than in
Adam, and by grace than by nature. Nor can the reprobate complain about God,
since although they fell in Adam according to God's just decree, they
nevertheless fell in him voluntarily, not under compulsion, and consequently
are condemned only as those who deserve it. If this does not satisfy you and
them, I shall cry out with Paul, “O man, who are you to answer back to God?”

### Another Argument of Castellio

“I do not will the death of the sinner.”

### Refutation

See how eagerly you play to the crowd. You have forgotten the question under
discussion. We are not dealing with death, or eternal condemnation—for it is
agreed between us that no one is condemned except by his own desert—but with
the just purpose to condemn. Its cause, although hidden from us, is always
just, because whatever God wills is just; and God so decrees what he decrees
that, in executing his decree, he places just causes beneath it. Why, then, do
you pass over the intermediate causes and slide down from God's decree to its
execution—that is, from the purpose to condemn to condemnation itself? It is
as though from eternity the Lord had simply purposed, “I shall consign this
man to destruction,” instead of, “I shall consign this man to destruction by
his own fault, so that my justice may appear.”

I shall put it even more plainly. You act ineptly by citing a testimony about
death—that is, about the execution of the counsel—when the counsel itself is
under discussion. Indeed, the prophet whose two testimonies you misuse argues
against men of your own party. They complained that they had incurred God's
wrath by what they call God's absolute power, as though God dealt tyrannically
with them—that is, raged against them whether justly or unjustly. But what does
the Lord do through the prophet? He calls them back to the causes of
destruction inhering in themselves, as we too do every day. “There is no ground
for you to accuse me,” the Lord says through the prophet. “Just as I receive
the penitent, so I punish only the stubborn and obstinate. Since your own
conscience accuses you, why do you transfer the blame to me?” Anyone who
weighs those passages will confess this to be the prophet's meaning. And
surely, when all people of moderate judgment hear these things and examine
themselves, they will think about reforming their lives and obtaining a new
heart from the Lord rather than about assailing God's counsels.

In sum, then, I say that the prophet does not rise to that eternal decree of
God, but indicates the true use of this doctrine, which we too impress upon
people as diligently as we can: we must examine God's decree concerning us not
by searching those hidden recesses, but by considering it in the Word set
before us.

But you will press the word “I do not will,” as though the Hebrew verb חפץ
(*ḥāp̄ēṣ*) simply meant *to will* rather than denoting *to approve and regard as
pleasing*, for which the Greeks say εὐδοκεῖν (*eudokein*). You should know that
God wills many things which he nevertheless does not approve ἁπλῶς (*haplōs*,
absolutely), but only κατὰ τι (*kata ti*, in some respect). For example, when
he tries his own people, or even chastises them by the hand of the wicked, do
you think that what the wicked perpetrate is properly pleasing to him, though
they have been stirred up by him, as he speaks everywhere through the
prophets—even to such a degree that for this reason also

[Printed p. 354; PDF p. 19; logical scan 378; OCR object 0387]

Nebuchadnezzar is called the Lord's servant? Surely, if you think God simply
approves these acts, you are greatly mistaken, since he afterward avenged
these very crimes upon the Chaldeans so severely. What, then? God does not
approve them absolutely, insofar as they oppress his people. But insofar as by
this means the patience of the godly is strengthened, those who have sinned
are recalled to duty, his justice in punishing criminals is manifested, the
wicked fill up their measure, and, finally, these judgments make his glory
illustrious—so far the Lord approves the most just use and end of these
events. The destruction of the reprobate is also of this class. God does not
properly delight in that destruction, but in his own glory, for whose sake he
testifies through Solomon that he made even the wicked man for the day of
calamity. This is why I have often said that we are not accustomed to say that
God made certain people for the sake of their destruction, but that through
the just destruction of some he might make his glory illustrious. No end more
just or better than this can even be conceived.

Consequently, whether for the sake of his glory God destined some—namely, the
wicked—for destruction has nothing to do with the point of the argument you
have raised. You deny it; I affirm it with Moses and Solomon. Nor can you draw
anything else from these passages of Ezekiel than what we too say: God is so
kind that even when he treats people according to their deserts he does not
delight in their destruction, but takes thought only for the glory of his own
name. In the same way, merciful and equitable judges do not seek the torments
of the guilty, as though their purpose were to shed human blood. Nevertheless,
because right and equity require that the wicked be terrified by the
punishments of criminals and the good be defended from the wrongs of evil
people, the judges therefore hate not the people but their crimes, and punish
them according to the law's prescription.

This, I say, is the force of the phrase “I do not will” against those who
accused God as though he were a savage tyrant raging against people without
regard for either good or evil, whereas, on the contrary, he never punishes
any but the guilty and does so in such a way that he delights not in the
people's punishments but in his own justice. Otherwise, if you take the phrase
“I do not will” absolutely, you will certainly have to explain how, when God
does not will sinners to be condemned and consequently wills them to be saved,
so many are nevertheless punished with eternal penalties according to the
sentence of this same God who does not will it.

Lastly, you ought to have noticed those for whom this discourse of Ezekiel is
intended: certainly a criminal and stubborn people, yet nevertheless the
people of God. Since he had very many elect among them, it is no wonder that
the Lord used this language among those whom, as comprehended in his covenant,
he wished to call back to himself. But besides the other errors I have
mentioned, you added this one too: what applies only to the church of God you
extended to the whole human race.

### Another Argument of Castellio

“God wills no one to perish, but everyone to come to the knowledge of the
truth.”

### Refutation

In the passage which you twist in your usual fashion, Peter is strengthening
the elect against certain scoffers who, like far too many today, ridiculed
everything said about God's supreme judgment. “Beloved,” he says, “let this
one thing not escape you: with the Lord a thousand years are like one day, and
one day like a thousand years.” That is, as I interpret it, the Lord is not
bound in a human manner to any intervals of time, which some people number
with excessive anxiety. He then adds, “The Lord who promised does not delay
(as some reckon this to be delay), but is patient toward us, not willing any
to perish, but all to proceed to repentance.”

It is as if Peter said, “There are some among you to whom the Lord appears to
defer his second advent longer than is right, and they certainly think about
God far otherwise than they ought. First let us consider who it is that
promised at last to come and deliver his own completely. Surely it is God.
Could he therefore delay the promise by even one moment, when his word is most
certain and he himself fixed definite moments for his promise? Next, see how
wrongly some assess this delay because of their impatience. They think the
church is in some way scorned by God, or is not valued as highly as it should
be. On the contrary, the Lord would not delay that advent of his unless he
were exercising the utmost patience and kindness toward us. For he defers it
only until he has gathered his own, not one of whom he wills to perish, but
all of whom he wills instead to be saved through true repentance.”

That this is the simple, plain, and transparent meaning of Peter's words is
proved by many reasons. For this is also the purpose set before him: believers

[Printed p. 355; PDF p. 20; logical scan 379; OCR object 0388]

are to be strengthened against the Epicureans. Hence Peter brings forward
things which concern believers alone, whom he recalls to the promises. Then
Peter's whole concern is to teach them to bear the delay of the future
judgment more patiently. Such teaching certainly does not apply to the
stubborn and obstinate, who would desire God to postpone that judgment as long
as possible, as is evident from the demons' cry, “Have you come to torment us
before the time?”—much less would the Lord's delay appear excessively long to
them. Therefore, since he calls those whom he addresses *beloved* and joins
himself to them, saying εἰς ἡμᾶς (*eis hēmas*), “toward us,” not εἰς ὑμᾶς (*eis
hymas*), “toward you,” who is so dull as not to see that this entire discourse
pertains to the church alone—to the elect alone, I say? This is why it is said
elsewhere that at that judgment the Lord will first gather all his elect from
the whole world, their number then having been completed.

Finally, John is Peter's best interpreter. In John the souls of those killed
for God's Word are represented as weary of this longer delay and crying, “How
long, Lord, holy and true, before you judge and avenge our blood against those
who dwell on the earth?” What answer are they given? The very thing Peter
mentions: that they should rest for a little further time, namely, until the
remaining number of their fellow servants and brothers is completed.

These, then, Sycophant, are the ones not one of whom the Lord wills to perish,
and all of whom he calls to repentance at his own time. As for the rest, whom
he created for the evil day for the sake of his glory—as Solomon speaks, whom
it is far better to believe than you—there is no doubt that he wills them to
perish through their own fault. Scripture expressly testifies this about Eli's
sons and the rejected Jews. Here too, therefore, you strike the same reef on
which you suffered shipwreck shortly before. What is said of the sons alone,
you wish to apply even to the dogs. Even if I granted this to you, you would
still have to explain here how some are going to perish against God's will,
for an idle permission does not befit a judge.

### Another Argument of Castellio

“If God created the greatest part of the world for destruction, it follows
that his wrath is greater than his mercy. Yet the Scriptures teach that he is
slow to anger and inclined to mercy, so that his wrath extends only to the
third or fourth degree of a family, while his mercy stretches to the
thousandth.”

### Refutation

This argument contains almost more errors than syllables. What you prattle
about the greatest part of the world is entirely your own invention, as I said
before. For although Christ's authority and the constant observation of every
age could make it clear even to the blind that very few enter through the
narrow gate in comparison with those who walk upon the broad way that leads
to destruction, we are certainly not accustomed gladly to exhaust ourselves
in speculations about whether there are more vessels of wrath and dishonor
than of mercy and honor. Further, as I have often repeated, it is not ours to
say absolutely that God created some for destruction, but that he created them
to declare his glory through the just destruction of some. Moreover, the
inference you construct is invalid.

For even if God saved only one human being, we say that the magnitude of the
love which he declared in that secret and incomprehensible mystery of his
eternal Son is so great that it infinitely surpasses every severity against
the wicked, however great such severity can be conceived. For who in heaven
or on earth will comprehend the breadth, length, and depth of that love of God
toward us most wretched sinners? How great are these things: that eternal
Word, who in the beginning was God with God, was so emptied as to become man,
subject to all the sufferings of our flesh except sin, so that we in turn
might become sons of God; that the Eternal One was conceived, born, and
breathed this air common to us; that the Almighty was worn down for nearly
thirty-four full years by every labor and misery, by hunger, thirst,
watchfulness, terrors of both mind and body, and infinite insults; that Life
itself was killed by the cruelest and most exquisite torments and enclosed in
a tomb? For, after Paul's example, let us be permitted to speak this way—not
to confuse the natures, but to unite them while they remain distinct.

And what of this: even now, amid such great faithlessness in the world, that
inexhaustible love of Christ toward his church does not cease and never will?
Even if only one single person, I say, were made a participant in this
immeasurable and inexplicable

[Printed p. 356; PDF p. 21; logical scan 380; OCR object 0389]

benefit, what comparison could nevertheless be drawn between this love of God
and his severity toward all other human beings and even toward the apostate
angels? But you prefer to calculate the proportion between wrath and mercy
from the number of the condemned and the saved rather than from the nature of
each.

As for that twofold testimony of Moses, you misuse both parts of this as well.
What if I answered that God is there explaining the character in which he
reveals himself to his church? I would certainly be saying what is true, as
even this single example will show. Let us compare Saul's action with David's
crime. In defiance of God's command, which appeared exceedingly harsh, Saul
does not immediately kill the captive King Agag and reserves the fatter
animals from among the things devoted to destruction, either for sacrifices
to the Lord or even, through avarice, for himself. But what does David do—that
excellent prophet who had experienced God's kindness so often? He violates
another man's wife and indulges his sin; he arranges for a man of the greatest
integrity and courage to be killed by a most shameful betrayal; and, in order
to gratify his savage lust, he knowingly and deliberately exposes the whole
army to the most manifest danger, and the ark of the covenant and the very
name of God to the enemies' mockery. Yet Saul is soon cast off, and the Lord
cannot be entreated by any of Samuel's tears, while David is soon received
back into favor. Why is this? Surely the foreknowledge of their sins does not
help you here. You must come to this: God has mercy on whom he wills and
hardens whom he wills.

Saul nevertheless has no ground for complaint. For who first gave to the Lord
so that it might be repaid to him? No injustice is done to Saul, although
grace is shown to David. Yet who does not see in Saul God's ἀποτομία
(*apotomia*), or severity, as Paul speaks, but in David the express nature of
God which he himself describes in Moses when he says that he is inclined to
mercy and slow to anger?

I could therefore settle the matter with you at once by answering that in
that passage the Lord addresses only his elect, since he justly and deservedly
shows himself terrifying and inexorable to the rest. Yet, lest you cry that I
am evading the issue, I confess that the Lord acts with incredible kindness
and gentleness even toward the very vessels of wrath formed for destruction.
How is it that he does not immediately destroy Cain? That he delays the flood
for so many years? That he blesses Esau with the earth's abundance? That he
causes Ishmael to grow into a great nation? That he tolerates the Canaanites
and Amalekites for so long? That he does not immediately remove Saul, but
allows him for so long to enjoy both the benefit of this life and the glory
and advantages of the Israelite kingdom? Finally, not to pursue ancient
examples, how is it that he treats so many impious Turks, the vast tyranny of
Antichrist, and finally you yourself with so many false prophets—who never
cease drawing away from God's truth everyone they can—with such forbearance
and sustains them so mercifully? Great indeed, great and incomprehensible, is
God's goodness toward his enemies. Would that all the elect who lie hidden
among them might at last acknowledge it and flee to him who shows himself
truly merciful and slow to anger even toward his adversaries.

You err no less in explaining the other testimony of the same Moses—or rather
of the Lord. For the use of that teaching pertains to the church alone, for
which alone the law is properly enacted and the gospel preached. Human
dispositions vary, and there is also a twofold nature in regenerate people
themselves. Threats are therefore joined to promises, so that by the former
the flesh and whatever is servile may be restrained, and by the latter the
spirit and whatever is free and noble may be strengthened. Nor are those
threats merely empty terrors, since the Lord often does not stop with words,
but severely chastises his own children and subdues their adversaries with
terrible fury before their eyes.

Meanwhile, I grant that his mercy stands out, especially in his own church.
But how inept you are to misuse those testimonies in order to evade that
eternal and hidden mystery of the divine decree! Why do you not instead dwell
upon the proximate causes of destruction and follow in the apostle's
footsteps? Then you would admire God's kindness toward his enemies instead of
denying him what everyone permits to all potters without objection. Yet some
comparison can be made between clay and a potter, while no comparison can be
established between God and created things, whatever they may be, except
insofar as God freely adopts human beings for himself. For even if we ascend
as far as Adam, what proportion is there between the image and the reality
itself?

Nevertheless, I grant that God's benefits toward humanity are innumerable,
especially if they are considered in Adam. But you are greatly mistaken when
you therefore suppose that God strips himself of his divinity, so that he
could not, without a charge of cruelty, create as many people as he pleased in
whose just condemnation—for although he destined those whom he willed for
destruction, he consigns only the deserving to destruction—he might manifest
his hatred of sin and his own just

[Printed p. 357; PDF p. 22; logical scan 381; OCR object 0390]

justice. Do the trees and plants today rise up against God in your fashion
because they are eaten by human beings? Does every kind of animal complain
against God because they are destined for slaughter and exposed to human
voracity? Not at all. For the apostle says that τὴν κτίσιν (*tēn ktisin*), “the
creation,” groans and sighs—not as though complaining against God, but because
it frets against God's enemies. And so it certainly is. For if the earth
itself, heaven and the stars, all the elements of things, the almost infinite
species of cattle, fish, and birds, and the stones and rocks could speak,
although their condition might appear pitiable to human reason, they would
not complain against God as savage and cruel. Rather, they would freely thank
him because they had been regarded as worthy to serve his glory.

But you have been found accusing God of cruelty unless he destined everyone
for blessed life. Although you do not mention everyone in this passage, your
argument nevertheless requires it. For if even one person is destined for
eternal torments, then in your judgment God will not escape the charge of
cruelty. Although one who condemns many is crueler than one who condemns a
single person, in your judgment the one who condemns even one does not thereby
cease to be cruel, for you either will not or cannot distinguish the purpose
of a just condemnation from the condemnation itself.

But to omit all those things which you deny ordinary people can grasp, I say
that in twisting this testimony of Moses you resemble those who marvel that
more drunkards than physicians reach old age, without reflecting that this
happens because there are infinitely more drunkards than physicians. Thus,
even if—as you falsely suppose—God saved the descendants of every believer to
the thousandth degree without a single exception, but destroyed the children
of unbelievers only to the fourth degree, far more people to be condemned
would still remain, because there have always been far more in the world who
hated God than who loved him.

But you err both in supposing that here the Lord sets forth his nature to be
considered from the multitude of those saved or condemned, and in taking
these words as though the Lord bound himself without exception to save a
fixed number of anyone's descendants. Constant experience teaches that both
are utterly false. What, then? Is the Lord deceptive? Not at all. By these
words he merely explains how much more persevering he is in doing good than
he is tenacious in pursuing his adversaries and remembering wrongs. Yet he
does good at last to those by whom he is loved and hates those by whom he is
despised; and he reserves to himself the freedom freely to have mercy on whom
he wills and justly to harden whom he wills. He expressly testified and
demonstrated this in fact in the history of Eli the priest—to omit Ishmael and
Isaac, Jacob and Esau, and innumerable others.

## Calumny II

God not only predestined Adam to condemnation but also to the causes of
condemnation. He not only foresaw Adam's fall, but willed it by an eternal and
hidden decree and ordained that he should fall; and so that this might occur
in its own time, he placed the fruit before him as the cause of the fall.

### Castellio's Censure

“They likewise say that the second article is the devil's doctrine, and ask us,
Calvin, to show where it is written in the divine books.”

### Refutation

In refuting this calumny you have retained your character, for what you
prattle about Adam being predestined to condemnation and about the fruit which
the Lord placed before him was invented by you. These things, I say, were
fabricated by you, so that there is no need to answer them.

But how reverently Calvin was accustomed to think and write about these
things can be seen from the following testimonies, which I have transcribed
word for word, partly from the *Institutes of the Christian Religion* and
partly from the book on eternal predestination: “The destruction of the
reprobate so depends,” he says, “upon God's predestination that its cause and
matter are located in themselves. For the first man fell because the Lord had
judged that it was expedient; why he judged so is hidden from us. Yet it is
certain that he judged so only because he saw that the glory of his name would
thereby be made illustrious. Where you hear mention of God's glory, there
think of justice. For that it is just

[Printed p. 358; PDF p. 23; logical scan 382; OCR object 0391]

is required if it deserves praise. Man therefore falls, God's providence so
ordering it; but he falls through his own fault. The Lord had declared shortly
before that everything he had made was very good. From where, then, came that
depravity in man by which he departed from his God? Lest it be thought to come
from creation, God had approved with his commendation what had proceeded from
himself. Man's own malice therefore corrupted the pure nature he had received
from the Lord, and by his ruin he dragged his entire posterity with him into
destruction. Let us therefore contemplate the evident cause of condemnation
in the corrupted nature of the human race rather than search for it in God's
hidden and utterly incomprehensible predestination. Nor let it trouble us to
submit our understanding thus far to God's immense wisdom, so that it yields
before many of his mysteries. With regard to things which it is neither given
nor lawful to know, ignorance is learned; the appearance of knowledge is a
form of madness.”

These are Calvin's words. Even they alone will amply satisfy every moderate
mind in dissolving this calumny. Yet we shall also add the following in
abundance, so that the matter may become clearer and clearer: “Three things,”
he says, “must be considered here. First, God's eternal predestination, by
which he determined what would happen concerning the whole human race and
each individual before Adam fell, was fixed and decreed. Second, Adam himself
was consigned to death according to the desert of his apostasy. Finally, the
whole offspring was so condemned in the person of Adam, already lost and
desperate, that God deigns to honor with adoption those whom he freely chooses
from it.”

And a little later in the same place: “When predestination is discussed,” he
says, “I have always steadfastly taught, and still teach today, that one must
begin from this point: all the reprobate who are dead and condemned in Adam
are justly left in death; those who are by nature children of wrath justly
perish; thus no one has cause to complain of God's excessive severity, since
all carry guilt enclosed within themselves. If one comes to the first man, he
was created upright and fell voluntarily, and thereby brought destruction
upon himself and his descendants by his own fault. Now, although Adam fell
only with God knowing and so ordaining, and destroyed himself and his
posterity, this has no bearing either upon lightening his fault or implicating
God in a crime. For this must always be regarded: he deprived himself of the
uprightness which he had received from God; he voluntarily consigned himself
to servitude under sin and Satan; voluntarily he hurled himself headlong into
destruction. One excuse is put forward: since it had been decreed by God, he
could not escape. But voluntary transgression is more than sufficient for
guilt. For the hidden counsel of God is not the proper and genuine cause of
sin, but man's manifest will.

“The foolish complaint of Medea is rightly ridiculed in the ancient poet:
‘Would that in the Pelian grove …,’ and so forth. She had betrayed her
fatherland, seized by a mad love for a foreign and unknown man. Although she
is conscious of her treachery and barbarous cruelty, and although the scourge
of unchastity binds her, she absurdly turns to causes far removed. Since man
finds the cause of his evil within, what profit is there in going around to
seek it in heaven? His guilt plainly appears in the fact that he willed to
sin. Why, breaking into heaven's inner recesses, does he plunge himself into a
labyrinth? Although human beings try to deceive themselves by wandering
through immense circuits, they will never so stupefy themselves that they do
not retain the sense of sin engraved upon their hearts. Impiety therefore
strives in vain to absolve the man whom his own conscience condemns. The fact
that God knowingly and willingly permitted man to fall can have a hidden
reason; it cannot have an unjust one.”

This is Calvin's judgment concerning Adam's fall. You ought to have said why
it displeases you instead of censuring it so confidently. Yet there are two
things you are accustomed to put forward as pretexts, and I shall answer each
separately. First, you complain that on this account God is made the author of
all sins, since all of them flowed from Adam's fall, whose fault must be
attributed to God if Adam fell by his inevitable decree. Second, you demand an
express word of God by which that doctrine can be confirmed.

As for the former, you have already heard why your inference, “God ordained
this; therefore he is at fault,” is invalid. Calvin discloses the reason from
Augustine in these words: “This must be held without controversy,” he says,
“that sin has always been hateful to God. For the commendation with which
David adorns him truly applies to him: that God does not will iniquity.
Rather, in ordaining man's fall he had the best and most upright end, from
which the very name of sin recoils. Although I say that he ordained it, I do
not grant that he was properly its author.”

You hear Calvin expressly refuting the very charge of blasphemy which you
falsely lodge against him. Yet those words still do not satisfy you. But
whether you will or not, they certainly show sufficiently how great the
wickedness is in daring to charge anyone with something which he has expressly
testified that he detests as impious blasphemy. But

[Printed p. 359; PDF p. 24; logical scan 383; OCR object 0392]

let us bring forward further testimonies from the same book. “Not to be too
long,” he says, “I judge that what Augustine teaches was fulfilled completely.
These are Augustine's words: ‘These are the Lord's great works, sought out
according to all his wills, and sought out so wisely that, when the angelic
and human creature had sinned—that is, had done not what he willed but what
the creature itself willed—he fulfilled what he willed even through that same
will of the creature by which what the Creator did not will was done. As the
supremely good one, he used even evils well: for the condemnation of those
whom he justly predestined to punishment and for the salvation of those whom
he kindly predestined to grace. So far as they themselves were concerned,
they did what God did not will; but so far as God's omnipotence was concerned,
they were in no way able to accomplish it. For by the very fact that they
acted against God's will, God's will was done concerning them. The Lord's
works are therefore great, sought out according to all his wills, so that in a
wonderful and ineffable manner nothing occurs outside his will even when it
occurs against his will. For it would not occur if he did not permit it; and
he certainly permits it not unwillingly, but willingly.’”

And the same Augustine a little earlier: “‘It must not be doubted,’ he says,
‘that God does well by permitting whatever things are done badly. For he
permits this only by a just judgment. Although evil things, insofar as they are
evil, are not good, nevertheless it is good that not only good things but also
evil things exist. For unless it were good that evils should also exist, they
would in no way be permitted to exist by the omnipotent Good. For him it is
without doubt as easy not to permit what he does not will to exist as it is to
do what he wills. Unless we believe this, the very beginning of our faith is
endangered, by which we profess to believe in God almighty.’”

After Calvin has cited these and very many other things, he finally adds the
following too, to meet the calumnies which you have drawn from Pighius's pools.
“If I had ever said,” he writes, “that by the impulse of the divine Spirit the
first man was caused to alienate himself from God, rather than everywhere
maintaining that he was impelled by the devil's instigation and his own
heart's motion, Pighius might perhaps deservedly exult over me. But now, when
by removing the proximate cause of the action from God I also remove every
fault from him, so that man alone incurs guilt, he calumniously and wickedly
twists against me the statement that man's apostasy is one of God's works.
How, by God's foreknowledge and decree, what was going to happen concerning
man stood fixed, while God nevertheless must not be drawn into partnership in
the fault as though he were either the author or approver of the
transgression—since it is clear that this mystery far surpasses the
penetration of the human mind—let us not be ashamed to confess our ignorance.
Indeed, let no believer be troubled not to know what the Lord swallows up in
the radiance of his inaccessible light.”

Moreover, since Calvin did not so much write these things so expressly as copy
them from Augustine, why should we need another defense, at least among sane
people? But lest we appear to wish to overwhelm you by the authority of a name
alone, if whatever happens by God's ordination occurs in such a way that,
whenever it is done unjustly, God must therefore be drawn into partnership in
the fault, tell me, I ask—passing over for now innumerable examples which we
shall discuss shortly—how will you excuse God in that deed, the most hideous
of all things ever not merely perpetrated but even conceived by human beings?
I mean the betrayal, condemnation, and insults of the Son of God, and finally
his most cruel death.

Your business is no longer with Augustine and Calvin, nor with anyone else
from the company of those who are wise in the church of God but fools to you;
it is with Peter himself. For he says openly and popularly, as you demand,
that Jesus was seized and crucified by the Jews after being delivered up by
God's definite counsel and providence. And what does the entire church at
Jerusalem say? “Truly,” it says, “Herod and Pilate gathered together against
your holy Son Jesus, whom you anointed, to do whatever your hand and counsel
had determined should occur.” These are the words of the Holy Spirit speaking
through Peter and the apostles, not in order to transfer the fault of those
infamous men to God himself—a crime from which we too so recoil that it has
never even entered our mind—but to set the hidden counsel of God the Father
and his incredible love toward his own against the scandal of the cross.

Do we really speak otherwise? And will you be so circumspect as to suppose
that you can speak even more holily than the Holy Spirit himself has spoken;
or rather so presumptuous as to dare accuse in anyone as blasphemous that of
which the Holy Spirit himself is acknowledged as author? We say that Adam did
indeed fall of his own accord and by his own fault, yet not by chance, but as
God's definite counsel, providence, and hand had justly determined should
occur. Isaiah affirms the very same thing about Christ, whom he says was
struck and wounded for our iniquities—certainly by him who did not spare his
own Son for us—although the wicked, both Jews and Romans, aimed at nothing
less. Peter expresses these same things, and the whole most holy

[Printed p. 360; PDF p. 25; logical scan 384; OCR object 0393]

assembly of the church confirms them. Now show what deed is more wicked than
this one, or grant us permission to use the apostles' language without
suspicion of blasphemy, or devise new escapes so that you may continue to be
like yourself.

It remains for me to answer the other question you are accustomed to ask:
where in the divine books is it written that Adam fell not apart from God's
decree? Why should I not be permitted, in confirmation of the matter under
discussion, to bring forward Paul's statement that God works all things
according to the counsel of his will? For you must confess that God has always
been the same. Further, when the same apostle expressly testifies that God,
like a potter, destined from the same lump some vessels for honor and mercy and
others for dishonor and wrath, how will you so twist that statement as not to
ascend all the way to the very making of Adam? For the name *lump* applies not
to the human race already made but to the race in the process of being made,
much less can the corrupted human race be understood by the designation
*lump*. By the human race *to be made*, moreover, I mean not only that God had
not yet actually made it, but that he did not consider or foresee it as made,
much less as corrupted.

For if the apostle stopped at Adam's sin or corruption as though it were the
highest level among all the causes of election and reprobation—that is, if he
wished the analogy to be understood of human beings who were already made and
corrupted, whether actually or according to God's foreknowledge—then it should
not have been said that God *makes* vessels of wrath. It should instead have
been said that he leaves in their miserable state those whom he foreknew would
be such by their own nature, or who were already vessels of wrath. But then
the analogy of the whole comparison would collapse. If it does not agree in
this respect, tell me why Paul employed it, and also explain to us why he
cried out so loftily, “O man, who are you?” For if matters stood as you say,
why would anyone complain where the vessels of mercy are concerned? And if,
on the other side, corruption is the highest level of causes, why would the
apostle labor over this point too? Not even the slightest appearance of a
complaint would remain to the reprobate if making vessels of wrath were the
same as leaving in corruption those who would be born such by their own
nature. And if any explanation of this divine judgment had to be offered,
only this one would have been needed: God owes grace to no one. Why, then,
would the apostle cry out here and command man silently to adore the mysteries
of divine majesty?

Some are accustomed to assert that in that passage Paul is not discussing why
some are reprobated, but why these rather than those are reprobated, since
equal things are owed to equals. Although this is perhaps not stated with
sufficient precision, it nevertheless tells strongly against you too. For
what will be owed to both groups except a just punishment, if—as you
maintain—their foreseen corruption precedes God's decree? If this seems too
obscure to you, come, let us produce a popular analogy to oblige you. If a
creditor has two people equally indebted to him, forgives the debt of one, and
demands it from the other, who would not call the latter insane if he said an
injustice had been done to him because he was treated more severely than the
other? If a king has two people equally bound by precisely the same crime,
shows favor to one, and sends the other to the cross, will there therefore be
any appearance of injustice in the judge because he punishes the latter?
Indeed, if any injustice existed here, it would not lie in his punishing that
man but rather in his failing to punish the other, who was equally criminal.
Thus, if this had been the purpose set before the apostle, he would not have
needed to assert God's justice in the destruction of the reprobate, but rather
in the salvation of the elect.

Since these things are so, you see that, wherever you turn, you cannot escape
being compelled to confess that God is compared to a potter who had clay
before him and from it once made both vessels of honor and vessels of
dishonor, because it so pleased him—and nothing is more just. Tell me, then,
how did he make them? Surely in Adam, to whose very creation the apostle
without doubt looked when he mentioned potter and clay. Further, how were the
vessels destined in Adam, some for mercy and honor and others for wrath and
dishonor? Surely only through the intervention of Adam's voluntary fall. For
unless Adam had fallen of his own accord—and although not apart from God's
ordination, nevertheless without any fault in God—no place would have remained
for either mercy or severity, and consequently God's counsel would have been
frustrated. Confess, then, from this passage of the apostle that it is God who
made the vessels of wrath and mercy and therefore justly ordered the causes
necessary for that end—unless perhaps you prefer either to accuse God of
imprudence and rashness, or to imagine the potter depending upon the clay
itself, instead of the potter shaping the clay as he pleased so that it would
attain the destined end.

Moreover, when Paul says that God shut everyone under disobedience so that he
might have mercy on all—that is, all who would believe, as he explains
elsewhere, and consequently all who were elected—does he not seem to you to
ascend all the way to the very source of disobedience, that is, to Adam's
fall, when he has reached the point

[Printed p. 361; PDF p. 26; logical scan 385; OCR object 0394]

of crying, “O the depth of God's judgments”? But these things apparently do
not seem sufficiently explicit to you. You want a passage produced in which
it is stated expressly and in so many words that God decreed and willed Adam
to fall. In return I ask where you find in the divine books, expressly
condemned in the way you demand, Noah's and Lot's drunkenness, Lot's incest as
well, Isaac's lie in Gerar, Judah's malice and fornication, the polygamy of
Abraham and very many others, or that perverse and utterly unjust judgment of
David by which he deprived Mephibosheth, the son of his dearest friend, of
half his goods but gave magnificent rewards to Ziba, the criminal betrayer of
his master, as though for a deed well done. I ask where you find these and
almost innumerable other things condemned in express and proper words. I fear
you will find this nowhere.

Will you therefore judge that these things ought not to be condemned? Not at
all, I suppose, unless perhaps you think polygamy can be disputed on either
side, so that you may retain the Academic manner here as well. What, then?
Will it be lawful to add anything to or subtract anything from divine
doctrine? Certainly not. But doctrine must be distinguished from examples,
which are like appendices to doctrine. In doctrine itself this must be
observed with the utmost reverence: we must depend entirely upon God's written
Word. But in examples, if we wish to know what is set before us for approval
or rejection, general theses must be compared with the matter at hand, even
if Scripture does not always express this in the narrative of the examples
itself. For instance, although the deeds we mentioned are not expressly
condemned in their narratives, God's Word manifestly forbids drunkenness,
incest, lies, murders, fornication, fraud, unjust judgments, and finally
polygamy, since the Lord said of the two from the beginning, “And they shall
be one flesh.” We will therefore not hesitate to censure all those things as
sins to which even the most excellent men were subject.

Since you cannot deny this, how is it that, when we come to the history of
Adam in which his fall is described, you refuse to follow this reasoning but
demand an express statement from God? “Because,” you will say, “it seems
foreign to God's nature for him to decree what is evil, indeed the very thing
that is the source of all evils.” But we have already shown that God justly
ordains what human beings do unjustly; God, I say, makes good use of a sinful
instrument. For the Lord looks to one thing, namely, his glory, for which he
even made the wicked man, as Solomon says. But the perverse will of Satan and
wicked human beings proposes something else to itself. Thus in the same work
God is just, while Satan and wicked people are unjust.

To understand this, let us set the example of Adam itself before us. God,
intending to make man in order to declare the glory of both his mercy and his
severity—as the event shows, and as the apostle sets before us under the
example of the potter—made Adam in his own image, that is, holy and innocent,
as the same apostle explains. Why so? Because, since God is good, nothing
defective can be created by him; yet those upon whom he has determined to
have mercy and those whom he has justly decreed to condemn for his glory must
become defective. Further, even if he could have created something defective,
had he done so he could not justly have condemned his own work. Adam therefore
had to be made holy and innocent, but in such a way that, having become
defective of his own accord and without any fault in God, he would open the
way for God's eternal counsels—that is, for the manifestation of both his
mercy and his severity. God therefore also had to open this one way for
himself, that is, to ordain Adam's fall, but toward the end I have described.
Since that end is most just and most holy, it follows that his ordination is
just as well.

But meanwhile, as Satan unwittingly serves God's counsel, what does he
attempt? Inflamed by hatred of God and boiling with envy, he tries to corrupt
that distinguished work of God. And what of Adam and Eve? They try to cast God
himself down from the very throne of divinity as a liar and an envious being.
The serpent, Adam, and Eve are therefore deservedly punished. They were God's
instruments in such a way that, so far as lay in them, they did not obey him
but rather conspired against him, although they opened the way for his eternal
and just counsel. For the origin of the fault must be sought in the
instruments' spontaneous motion, by which it comes about that God justly
decreed what they did unjustly, as we said earlier from Augustine.

“But,” you will say, “they could not resist God's will, that is, his decree.” I
grant it. But just as they could not, neither did they will to do so. “Yet they
could not will otherwise.” I grant this with regard to the outcome and the
ἐνέργεια (*energeia*), or actuality. Adam's will was nevertheless not
compelled; indeed, it assented to sin by a motion not merely spontaneous but
also free, since its δύναμις (*dynamis*), or capacity, had not yet been
enslaved to the servitude of sin. “But these things,” you will say, “do not yet
satisfy me.” I grant this too. But who are you to acknowledge no justice and
wisdom in God unless you can grasp the reason

[Printed p. 362; PDF p. 27; logical scan 386; OCR object 0395]

for it? Why do you not instead consider who you are and marvel at God's
goodness toward fallen and corrupted Adam? Adam fell voluntarily; what God
decrees is just. If these two things do not satisfy you, so that you locate all
the fault of the fall in Adam, the apostle declares you unworthy of any
further disputation, because you know neither who God is nor who you are.

But come, let us at last draw in the sails. I have shown what method we must
follow in evaluating examples; I have also shown that there is no blasphemy
here for you to fear. It remains for me to demonstrate from what heads of
Christian doctrine the truth of what we affirm can be clearly apparent:
namely, that Adam did not fall by chance, or with God ignorant, or with God
idly permitting it so to happen, and much less against God's will. I therefore
say that this is confirmed by all those passages of Scripture which affirm
that rashness has no place in God; that God is omnipotent; that God not only
made but also governs all things; that God cares and has always cared for
humanity; that nothing is hidden from God; and that God does not change or
repent, although Scripture sometimes speaks this way as though lisping to us.
All these things, I say, must either be refuted by you as false, or you must
confess what we affirm and have now repeated a thousand times: Adam fell not
only with God foreknowing but also with God justly ordaining and decreeing.
For if, before making man, God did not deliberate about the end for which he
would make him, he acted rashly and imprudently. But if he decreed anything
other than what occurred, then either he was not omnipotent, since Satan broke
his counsels, or he was inconstant and changed his counsel.

There, then, you have testimonies of Scripture clearer, I hope, and far more
numerous than you expected. But this is almost incredible: while you consider
so anxiously how to support God's justice, as though it were tottering and
wavering, it never even occurred to you how weak the position on which you
rely would be, even if we granted it to you. For I ask—if you please to measure
that divine wisdom by the rule of human judgment—how will God be free from
fault if man was so abandoned to his own choice and exposed to Satan's deceit
that his Maker idly watched him fall from heaven, although he could have
supported the falling man by a mere nod and had no cause to hate him? You see,
I think, into what precipices all must fall who, after the manner of the
giants, prefer to assault heaven rather than rest in God's simple Word. From
that Word we understand that God is in heaven, that he does whatever he wills,
and that he does everything rightly even if our flesh judges otherwise. For
because he is God, he cannot be unjust; and he is so just that his justice
surpasses all our understanding.

## The Third Calumny

The sins which are committed are committed not only by God's permission but
also by his will. For it is frivolous to assign a distinction between God's
permission and will where sin is concerned. Those who make this distinction
seek to win God's favor by flattery and adulation.

### Reply

Now at last we have come to the point over which you are accustomed to
triumph, as though we make God the author of sin. Indeed, of the twelve
articles that remain, I see that ten relate to this one end: even in a matter
however absurd and almost incredible in itself, you wish to appear to have
invented nothing false—at least to people who lack sufficient leisure or
desire to read our writings. But I trust I shall answer in such a way that no
other one of your charges will make our innocence clearer to everyone than
this one.

For there are two kinds of people who brand this stain upon us. One group
admits that we do not actually say this but alleges that we nevertheless lay
down premises from which it necessarily follows. The other is not ashamed to
say and write that we expressly affirm this very thing. It will be sufficient
to teach the former what they do not know; but the latter's obstinate
shamelessness is surely worthy of more severe restraint. Nevertheless, I shall
take pains that everyone understand I have regarded what becomes us rather
than what becomes you. Since, then, there are exactly two things from which
people can be evaluated by others—namely, deeds and words, under which I also
include writings—show, I ask, if you can, that either Calvin's character or
that of any one of us is such that even the slightest suspicion of this sort
of blasphemy falls upon us. For someone must be closely connected not with a
light vice or crime,

[Printed p. 363; PDF p. 28; logical scan 387; OCR object 0396]

who abolishes every distinction between good and evil, shameful and
honorable. Rather, he must be devoted wholesale to every crime and, having
cast off all shame, be remarkably practiced in every kind of wickedness, so
that the διάθεσις (*diathesis*), as the logicians say, has passed into a fixed
ἕξις (*hexis*) or habit. We, for our part, freely and willingly confess that
we are separated by an almost infinite distance from that perfection of which
you dream. Nevertheless, by God's benefit we have lived in the church of God
up to this day in such a way that—I wish this said without any appearance of
boasting—no one has yet been found who thought our life deserving of censure
from whom all good people would not rather be censured than praised.

Nor, I hope, will you persuade any sane person that, if we held the opinion
that we could sin with impunity with God as author, it would be possible for
us voluntarily to leave our homeland, despise the only things most people
admire, and neglect all the aids sought by those who have resolved to wallow
in the mud of pleasures, preferring instead to endure every misery in the
sight of almost the whole world. Among these miseries I count this as one of
the chief: that we have continual contests with monsters like you. I say that
we would hardly have preferred to suffer all these afflictions for so many
years and provoke every person's savagery against us instead of yielding to
those things toward which not only nature itself but our own will and judgment
would have dragged us.

As for Calvin's writings—for you judged him above all others to be gnawed
upon—I fear, good man, that by this deed of yours you confirm for us very well
the very doctrine you oppose. For what sane person do you suppose, after
merely reading the titles of Calvin's books and learning that you have lodged
this calumny against him, would not immediately judge that by God's just
judgment you have been deprived of all memory and reason? For what madness is
this: to attribute to Calvin a heresy which, when the Libertines revived it
almost within our own memory, he himself refuted so gravely and fully? Nor
were you unaware of it; indeed, you yourself, Castellio, were then living in
the same city and perhaps even in the same building.

Therefore, so that your audacity may become clear even to everyone who is
blind; so that human beings and angels may know the injury you do not so much
to Calvin or our churches as to God's truth itself; and finally so that
everyone may understand once for all what our judgment is concerning these
things—come, let us hear with what firm, true, and solid arguments eleven
years ago he refuted in a written and published book this blasphemy of the
Libertines and the very charges which you are not ashamed to lodge against a
faithful servant of God. Hear, then, how true it is that Calvin makes God the
author of sin.

#### On the Opinion of the Libertines, by Which They Hold That the One
#### Immortal Spirit Does All Things; and What Plague Lies Hidden beneath This
#### Opinion

##### Chapter XIII

“After they have fashioned the one Spirit according to their own judgment,
destroying and abolishing the natures of angels, devils, and even souls, they
say that this Spirit alone does everything. They do not mean what Scripture
teaches when it speaks of him: that as all creatures subsist in him, so they
are governed by him, are subject to his providence, and each in its own order
serves his will. Rather, they maintain that whatever occurs in the world must
be considered directly his work. Thus they attribute no will to man, as if he
were a stone, and abolish every distinction between good and evil, since in
their judgment nothing can be done wrongly when God is author of everything.

“To make the matter clearer, I shall give an example of their conversation.
Once that pig Quintin happened to come to a place where a murdered man lay,
and a godly man was present who cried out in horror, ‘Alas, who committed this
crime?’ Quintin immediately answered in his Picard speech, ‘If you wish to
know, I committed it.’ Then the other, as though stunned, said, ‘Are you, then,
so criminal?’ Quintin replied, ‘It was not I, but God.’ ‘What?’ said the other.
‘Are crimes which he orders to be punished to be imputed to God?’ Then that
foul man began to vomit his poison still more, saying, ‘So the matter stands:
you yourself committed it, I committed it, and God committed it. For what I or
you do, God performs. What God does, we ourselves also do, for he is in us.’
If this is granted, sin must be imputed to God, or it must be concluded that
no sin occurs in the world, since nothing exists which is not done by God.

“Thus every distinction between good and evil is abolished. It will follow
that we are not permitted to condemn anything as evil, since all things are
God's works, and people will be permitted to carry out whatever comes to mind
as though the reins were loosed—not only because we are beyond danger of
sinning, but also because restraining any desire would be to hinder God. For
example, has someone fornicated? He must not be censured,

[Printed p. 364; PDF p. 29; logical scan 388; OCR object 0397]

for that would inflict blasphemy upon God. Has someone desired his neighbor's
wife? Let him possess her if he can, for he knows with certainty that he is
doing nothing foreign to God's will. As for property, let anyone who can seize
it, whether by force or some subtle method, do so boldly, for he will undertake
nothing that is not approved by God. Let one who has stolen everything he
could not trouble himself greatly about restoring it, for it is not fitting
that God should be corrected.

“Some then turn those splendid speculations to their own advantage. Whatever
happens, they do not wish it to touch themselves, nor do they wish the God
they imagine to do anything that could harm them. But if someone else has
suffered injury or the loss of goods, they laugh and say that all these things
are very good; they also say that those who accuse the authors of a crime are
contending with God. But if even their smallest finger is touched, they forget
those fine reasonings, immediately burst into rage, and no one is more
vehemently indignant.

“I shall tell an amusing story here to show how these ravings help them when
they are needed most. In Paris a certain cobbler had been so imbued with the
poison of this sect that he judged nothing to be evil any longer. It happened
that he once visited Stephen de la Forge, with whom he had some acquaintance,
and found him grieved because a servant had abandoned him and taken some money
away. The chief cause of his grief was his fear that the servant might
somewhere abuse his name among the merchants. The cobbler asked what was
troubling him. Stephen, being a man of few words, answered in three. The
cobbler immediately raised himself up as though he would fly above the clouds
on outspread wings, and reproached Stephen as though he were blaspheming God
by calling God's work wickedness. Since God did everything, he said, nothing
was to be judged evil. Stephen, knowing that he would accomplish nothing by
reasoning with him, cut off his speech with a single word.

“A few days later, by God's will, it happened that a servant committed a
theft against this philosophizing cobbler. As though seized with madness, he
ran here and there, urgently seeking some report of the servant. When he could
not find the man, he went to Stephen's house to relieve his mind by complaint
and lamentation. He had begun to heap piles of abuse upon the thief and was
about to go further when Stephen interrupted his speech: ‘What? Is this how
one should blaspheme? Can we accuse God? If he himself did this, he should be
praised instead.’ Conquered by his own words, which he had used before, that
fanatic went away covered with shame, yet did not therefore come to his
senses.

“This example teaches us how the Lord confounds those madmen by experience
itself. Yet even this does not profit them so that they cease clinging
obstinately to their madness. They are possessed by the devil, who does not
allow them to see the things placed before their eyes. It must be noted that
three exceedingly execrable consequences follow from this article. The first
is that, if it is granted, there will be no distinction between God and the
devil; indeed, the God they fashion for us is in reality an idol worse than
the devils themselves. The second is that people will no longer be moved by
conscience to avoid evil, but like beasts, making no distinction among things,
will follow their appetite. The third is that no one will now dare to judge
anything, but everything will have to be approved: fornications, murders,
thefts, and the most shameful of all crimes will have to be regarded as deeds
worthy of praise. To handle this article better, these three consequences I
have shown to follow must be treated in order and refuted by demolishing the
foundations they misuse.”

#### How the Providence of God, Which Effects All Things, Must Be Considered;
#### How the Libertines, in Speaking of It, Confound Everything—the First
#### Consequence of the Preceding Article

##### Chapter XIV

“We ourselves do not deny that all things occur by God's will. Indeed, when
we explain why he is called Almighty, we attribute to him a power effective
in all creatures. We teach that just as he once created the whole universe,
so he also governs it and always keeps his hand applied to the work, so as to
preserve everything in its condition and dispose it according to his own
judgment. To express more easily what this means, I say that we must consider
God as working in three ways in the administration of the universe. First is
the universal operation by which he directs all creatures according to the
condition and property which he gave each when he formed them. This governance
is nothing other than what we call the order of nature. Since unbelievers
recognize nothing in the ordering of the world except what they see with their
eyes, and therefore establish nature like a goddess which presides and rules
over everything, this praise must be attributed to God's will: it alone
governs and rules all things. Therefore, when

[Printed p. 365; PDF p. 30; logical scan 389; OCR object 0398]

we see the sun, moon, and stars completing their course, let us understand
that they obey God and carry out his commands; and not only this, but that
they are governed by God's hand and that everything occurs by his power. Thus,
when we perceive the course of earthly things, everything must be attributed
to God, while creatures must be regarded as instruments in his hand which he
applies to the work in whatever manner he pleases. Scripture often touches
upon this universal providence so that we may learn to attribute glory to God
in all his works. God especially commends this power of his to us so that we
may acknowledge it in ourselves and be stripped of the arrogance which
usually rises in us as soon as we forget that we are in his hand. To this
belongs what Paul said at Athens: “He is the one in whom we live and move and
have our being.” By these words he warns that unless God sustains us by his
hand we cannot stand for even the smallest moment, since we subsist in him;
and that, just as the soul moves the members by pouring its power through the
whole body, so we are given life by God, from whom we obtain whatever capacity
or strength is in us. Yet this universal operation of God does not prevent
each creature, in heaven and on earth, from retaining its own quality and
nature and following its proper inclination.

“The second way in which God works in his creatures is by binding them to the
service of his goodness, justice, and judgment, according as he now wills to
help his servants, now to punish the guilty, now to test the patience of the
faithful, or to chastise them with fatherly affection. Thus, when he wills to
bestow an abundance of crops upon us, he gives rain in its season, sends heat
through his sun, and gives bright and clear days; he also uses other natural
means as instruments of his kindness. But when he draws his hand back to
himself, heaven becomes like bronze and earth like iron. Thus he is the one
who causes thunder, frost, hail, and storms and is the cause of barrenness.
Therefore, whatever pagans and the ignorant attribute to fortune, we must
assign to divine providence—not only to that universal power of which we have
spoken, but to the special ordination by which he governs everything as seems
most expedient and useful to him. He teaches this when he says through the
prophets that he creates darkness and light, sends life and death, and that
nothing good or evil occurs except from his hand. He even says that he governs
lots and other things which appear fortuitous. If anyone happens to be killed
unintentionally, God says that he is the cause of that death and so ordained
it, so that we may understand that nothing occurs by chance but everything by
the determination of his counsel. And he is greatly indignant when we suppose
anything to proceed from elsewhere and do not look to him, so as to
acknowledge him not only as the chief cause of all things but also as the one
who brings them about, determining everything in this way or that by his
counsel.

“Let us therefore conclude that both prosperous and adverse things—rain,
winds, hail, frost, clear weather, abundance, famine, war, and peace—are God's
works. Creatures, which are lower causes, are only instruments he uses to
execute his will. Thus he uses them at his discretion, turns them wherever he
pleases, and brings them to the point that what he has determined occurs
completely. It must further be noted that he uses not only creatures without
sense in this manner, working through them and executing his will, but human
beings themselves and even devils, so that Satan and the wicked too are
executors of the divine will. Thus he used the Egyptians to afflict his
people; somewhat later he raised up the Assyrians and others like them to
avenge its sins. We see that he used the devil to torment Saul, to deceive
Ahab, and, whenever necessary, to exercise his judgments against all the
wicked; or, conversely, to test the steadfastness of his own, of which Job is
an example for us.

“When the Libertines hear these things, they rush ahead rashly and
thoughtlessly, look no further, and conclude that creatures no longer do
anything. Thus they mix everything together horribly. They confound not only
heaven with earth, but God with the devil. This happens because they entirely
fail to observe two very necessary qualifications. The first is that Satan
and the wicked are not instruments of God in such a way that they do not also
play their own parts. For we must not imagine that God works through a wicked
person as through a stone or log, but as through a creature who participates
in reason, according to the quality of the nature he gave it. Therefore, when
we say that God works through creatures, this does not prevent the wicked
also from acting on their own part. Scripture shows this most openly. Just as
it declares that God will whistle and sound a trumpet, as it were, to lead
unbelievers out to war, or that he will harden or inflame their minds, so it
never ceases narrating their own counsel and what their wills were, and it
ascribes to them the work which they carried out by God's decree. The second
qualification, of which these wretched people take no account,

[Printed p. 366; PDF p. 31; logical scan 390; OCR object 0399]

is that there is a great difference between God's work and the wicked
person's work when God uses him as an instrument. The wicked person is driven
to his crime by his avarice or ambition, envy, or cruelty, and he looks to no
other end. The work therefore takes its quality from that root—that is, from
the disposition of his mind and the end he seeks—and is rightly judged evil.
But God's purpose is altogether contrary: namely, to exercise his justice in
preserving the good, to employ his grace and goodness toward the faithful,
and to punish those who have deserved evil. See how God and human beings must
be distinguished, so that in one and the same work we contemplate, on the one
side, his justice, goodness, and judgment, and, on the other, the malice of the
devil and unbelievers. Let us now take a splendid and perfectly clear mirror
in which to behold these things.

“When Job receives the news of so great a loss of goods, of the death of his
children, and of all the calamities that befell him, he acknowledges that God
is visiting him and says, ‘The Lord gave me all these things, and he himself
has taken them away.’ And this was indeed how matters stood. Yet did Job not
know that the devil had devised that calamity for him? Did he not understand
that the Chaldeans had driven off his herds and flocks? Did he praise those
plunderers and robbers? Did he excuse the devil because that affliction arose
from God? By no means. He understood that there was a great difference
between these things. Thus, while condemning the evil, he said, ‘Blessed be
the name of the Lord.’ Similarly, when Shimei persecuted David, David says
that he accepts it from God and sees that wicked man as the rod with which God
chastises him. Yet, while praising God, he nonetheless later condemns Shimei.
This matter will have to be treated elsewhere. For now, it is enough to hold
that God works through his creatures and so uses them in his providence that
the instrument through which he acts does not cease to be evil. Although he
turns the malice of Satan and wicked people to good, they are not thereby
excused or absolved so as to cease sinning, nor do their works cease to be
wicked and condemnable; for all works take their quality from the purpose and
will of their author.

“Those, therefore, who do not make these distinctions overturn everything
like pigs with their snouts and throw things established in the most
beautiful order into horrible confusion. Such are the Libertines. They not
only join the devil to God in partnership but even transform him into God
himself, judging his works praiseworthy on the pretext that he does nothing
unless it has been ordained by God. But this heresy, if we trust the ancient
teachers, took its origin from Simon Magus. We, by contrast, must observe that
creatures at their own level accomplish their own works on earth, which must
be judged good or evil according as they have been done for this end or that.
Meanwhile, God governs and directs everything, guiding it to a right end. He
turns evil to good, or at least, acting from the goodness of his nature, draws
something good out of that which is evil. He so uses the devil that he does
not mingle himself with him, have anything in common with him, become
entangled with him in a fellowship of evil, or destroy the nature of evil by
his justice. For just as the sun, sending its rays into a corpse and producing
a certain decay in it, draws nothing corrupt or impure to itself, and by its
purity does not prevent the corpse from being putrid and foul-smelling, so God
works through the wicked in such a way that the justice which is in him does
not justify them, nor is he himself contaminated by their wickedness and
corruption.

“The third kind of divine operation consists in the government of the
faithful, in whom he lives and reigns through his Spirit. Insofar as we are
corrupted by original sin, we are very like dry and barren ground and produce
no good fruit whatever. For our judgment is corrupt, our will is rebellious
against God and prone and inclined to evil; in short, our entire nature is
vicious. Since this is so, we are not only unable to apply our minds to good
things, but, what is more, are not even fit to conceive a single good thought.
Rather, as Paul says, if we are fit for anything, it must proceed from God
himself. He is therefore the one who works in us both to will and to
accomplish. He illuminates us so as to bring us to knowledge of himself; he
draws us to himself and forms new hearts in us, softening our hardness. He
himself also inspires in us the desire to pray, imparts strength and power to
resist all Satan's temptations, and causes us to walk in his commandments.

“Yet it must be noted that by nature we possess choice and will. But because
both have been corrupted by sin, the Lord reforms them and makes them good
from evil. That we are fit to decide, that we will, and that we do this or
that—this is a natural gift. But that we can choose, desire, or do nothing but
evil comes from the corruption of sin. That we desire to act well and are
endowed with the power to carry it out proceeds from supernatural grace, by
which we have been regenerated into a better and altogether divine life. See,
then, what God in

[Printed p. 367; PDF p. 32; logical scan 391; OCR object 0400]

his children effects: destroying their perverse nature, he directs them by his
Spirit into obedience to his will. But these drunken people, loudly insisting
that everything is done by God, make him the author of evil. Then, as though
the nature of evil were changed because it is covered beneath this veil of
God's name, they affirm that it is good. In this they treat God with a more
atrocious and wicked insult than if they transferred his power or justice to
another. For since nothing is more proper to God than his goodness, he would
have to disown himself and be transformed into the devil in order to effect
the evil which they attribute to him. Indeed, their God is an idol which ought
to be more execrable to us than all the idols of the nations. Yet they think
their hands duly washed, as the saying goes, when they reply that they affirm
all things to be good because they are done by God—as though it were in their
power to change black into white. They have discharged their duty splendidly,
no doubt, if, after calling God a robber, thief, and fornicator, they add that
there is no evil whatever in these things. But who condemned murder,
fornication, robberies, and thefts, if not God? We would certainly have to
charge him with falsehood in order to excuse the works they attribute to him.

“‘But Scripture,’ they say, ‘declares universally that God effects all things
in all.’ I reply that they wrongly and falsely adapt Scripture to this end.
For when Paul uses this statement, he speaks only of the graces of the Holy
Spirit. The passage itself sufficiently demonstrates this, for in it he
admonishes the Corinthians to use them well, since they are gifts of God;
because, as he says, no one can utter even a single word in praise of Christ
unless speaking through the Holy Spirit, from whom all good things proceed.
And certainly, as for whatever pertains to good, we will most gladly allow it
all to be attributed to God. But who will show that evil is born from God?
Where will they demonstrate that God steals in the thief, or kills innocent
people in the robber and assassin? Thus these coverings not only fail to
purge their blasphemies but greatly increase them and make them twice as
grievous.

On the second consequence which follows from “God effects all things”:
namely, that no one should any longer be troubled in conscience about
anything.

CHAPTER XV

“We said above that if everything is attributed to God, as the Libertines
wish, and a human being is said to do nothing, the result will be that we are
moved in conscience by nothing. For avoiding sin would then not be avoiding
anything but rather reversing God's work. I do not merely say that this can
