be deduced from their preceding discourse, lest we labor over debating
whether or not this is their meaning; I say that this very thing is what they
themselves confess. Indeed, this is their chief aim: to lull consciences to
sleep, so that people, free from every concern, may perpetrate whatever
presents itself and whatever they desire—as though God had given his law in
vain, by which good is distinguished from evil.

“I ask them this: Do we not have a declaration of the divine will in the law?
But there God has declared that fornication, theft, murder and, by
consequence, avarice, hatred, envy, ambition, and other things of this kind
displease him. Now, if these things are said to please him, is he not charged
with falsehood? Moses testifies that the law was given so that we might learn
to serve God, cleave to him, and obey his will, lest by our crimes we provoke
his wrath against us. These wretched people endeavor to strike such thoughts
out of us. And so that we can perceive nothing, they bind our eyes with this
bandage: namely, that everything happens by God's will, and that therefore
nothing displeases him—as though God were changeable and contradicted
himself, or were a dissembler, affirming that he hates and detests what he
nevertheless desires and approves.

“‘Because of these things,’ says Paul, ‘the wrath of God comes upon
unbelievers.’ ‘You grieve the Spirit of God,’ says Isaiah. ‘You have been
burdensome to me,’ says the Lord in another place. Again: ‘The Lord was
greatly angered; his wrath was kindled against Israel.’ I know, indeed, that
God is by no means subject to human passions. But all these expressions mean
that he disapproves and condemns evil, and thus that sin is the cause of
enmity between him and us. As long as we do evil we cannot be reconciled with
him; rather, punishment and vengeance must be expected, because he is a just
judge by whom no wickedness can be tolerated. Throughout nearly all Scripture
we read nothing but such exhortations: ‘Fear the Lord. See that you do not
offend him. Keep yourself from evil.’ But these madmen shout the contrary:
that it is foolish to fear offending him, since we do nothing good or evil,
but he effects everything in us. Paul warns that even the nations, though
lacking doctrine and the Scriptures, have a law

[Printed p. 368; PDF p. 33; logical scan 392; OCR object 0401]

impressed upon their hearts—namely, conscience, by which they either accuse
or defend themselves before God. Those deranged people desire to erase it,
denying that there is anything of which we should accuse ourselves since
everything is done by God. Why would they be ashamed to overturn the
Scriptures, when they dare so much that they do not hesitate to uproot the
conviction naturally fixed in our minds and engraved there by God? If we wish
to plead ignorance in order to excuse ourselves, God sends us to our own
conscience, which can testify abundantly against us. But these madmen,
suppressing this testimony, say that God must first accuse himself if he
wishes to bring an accusation against us, since he effects all things in us.

“Moses calls this ‘a root bearing gall and wormwood,’ when by flatteries we
endeavor to lull every moral consciousness to sleep and so deceive ourselves
that we think evil is a game. Indeed, what deadlier or more pestilential poison
could there be in the entire world? He also calls it adding drunkenness to
thirst—and rightly so. For our natural disposition is a disordered appetite
and, as it were, a thirst for doing evil. Therefore, when we bless ourselves,
as he says in that passage, and persuade ourselves that we will possess peace
as long as we live wickedly and ruinously, it is as though someone burning
with vehement thirst made himself drunk with wine so that no sensation would
touch him any longer, when he ought rather to have checked and quenched that
thirst by sobriety and abstinence. This is indeed an excellent passage for
admonishing us, so that we may understand what bitterness lies beneath the
honey which those ill-omened people offer us to drink.

“Nevertheless, they perpetually take refuge in this hiding place: that
nothing at all occurs apart from the will of God. But I reply that, with
respect to the works we do, we must consider God's will as he himself declares
it. For example, because he commands that each person's own property be
preserved and that loss or injury be inflicted upon no one, his will is clear
there. What pleases him therefore need not be investigated any further. We
know that if we do this we will obey his will, but if not, we will be wholly
unacceptable to him. Thus, if someone steals and then says that he has done
nothing apart from God's will, he lies shamelessly, since he has transgressed
God's commandment, by which he was taught about that very will.

“Someone will ask whether anything can happen against God's will. I admit
that it cannot. But we must take every care not to inquire into his
providence, which is hidden from us, when we know what he requires from us and
what he approves or condemns. Not without reason does Solomon affirm that the
one who searches into majesty will be overwhelmed by glory. Indeed, this must
happen, and the arrogance of the proud must be punished in this way. We
experience this in those people who desire to ascend above the clouds in
order to investigate God's will, but refuse to rest content with the
revelation of it contained in the Scriptures. They plunge into delusions so
absurd and beastly that they are horrible to speak or hear.

On the third consequence which the Libertines draw from the proposition “God
does all things”: namely, that it is not lawful to condemn anything.

CHAPTER XVI

“After thus loosening the reins for everyone, so that they allow each person
to live according to his own choice under the pretext that they permit
themselves to be governed by God, they infer from the same principle that it
is wrong to pass judgment about anything. They could have no teaching more
suited to opening a path for their abominations. For they have achieved much
when they have so obstructed—or rather pierced—the eyes of their hearers that
no one any longer dares to judge whether their words and deeds are good or
evil. But if we grant them this, what will become of God's sentence by which
he curses all who say that evil is good? See, God announces every dire and
unhappy thing against us if we regard as just what he judges to be evil. They,
by contrast, permit absolutely nothing to be condemned.

“If they were superior to God himself and able to remit the penalty he
pronounces, perhaps there would be room for deliberation. But who will wish to
be condemned by God in order to be absolved by Quintin? When they wish to put
this article forward, they use these words: ‘A Christian must turn everything
to his own advantage.’ For, as we said at the beginning, they never speak
simply and openly so as to express clearly what they have conceived in their
minds, but entangle the matter in oblique turns. I freely grant them that a
Christian ought to count anything as gain, as that man says, but in the sense
taught by Scripture: namely, that amid adversities he should reflect upon
what Paul says, that the faithful are afflicted so that they may be conformed
to the image of Christ, and that their afflictions may thus assist them in
attaining salvation. He should sustain himself with that consolation. If he
sees his brother slip

[Printed p. 369; PDF p. 34; logical scan 393; OCR object 0402]

or err, let this serve him as a mirror in which, recognizing the weakness of
human nature, he may walk in greater fear. If he himself should happen to
fall, let it remind him of his weakness, so that he distrusts his own powers,
becomes humbler, and lays aside all arrogance. Let it be like a goad urging him
to call upon God with more ardent prayers. See how Christians turn everything
to their advantage: not so that they may take pleasure in their vices or shut
their eyes lest they notice what is evil, but rather so that, grieved and sad
that they see God offended both by themselves and by others, they may be
trained in repentance, humility, and the fear of God, and may be solicitous to
guard themselves in the future.

“In this way David gathered benefit from the sin he had committed. He did not
justify himself in the least; rather, after hearing his condemnation, he
confirmed himself more and more in God's goodness and took care that he should
not afterward fall again into so great a calamity. In the same way Paul warns
us to turn to our advantage the sins which the children of Israel committed
against God: namely, that when we consider the punishments which followed, we
may be instructed by their example. See, finally, why Scripture recounts the
sins both of the faithful and of those who despise God: not to render us blind,
so that we can define or pronounce nothing with certainty and doubt whether
what is evil might be good, but so that, having recognized evil, we may avoid
it. Scripture itself also openly declares what judgment we must make about
each work.

“But they allege that it is written that we must not judge. The Lord indeed
uses these words: ‘Do not judge.’ But to what end? To rebuke the rashness of
those who usurp God's authority by judging matters unknown to them. Paul treats
the same subject in Romans 14, where he reproves those who condemn their
brothers over an indifferent action. We all have one judge, he says; to him we
must render an account; it belongs to him to define matters of that kind. Let
us therefore not subject our neighbors to our opinion. This is indeed an
excellent and most useful doctrine: just as there is one spiritual Lawgiver,
namely God, so he alone is the competent judge of our souls. Meanwhile, the
judgment he has already rendered is no less valid on that account. Thus when we
judge fornication, theft, blasphemy, drunkenness, and ambition to be shameful
vices, we do not pronounce judgment from ourselves; we approve the judgment
God has already rendered.

“These shameless dogs, however, while pretending that they do not wish to
judge, set themselves up as judges superior to God. I ask: after the supreme
judge had rendered sentence, if some private person said that the matter must
be left in doubt, as though nothing whatever had been decided, and that a
thing condemned by judgment should not be regarded as evil, would he not
rightly be thought worthy of severe punishment as a partisan or instigator of
sedition, since he wished to overthrow the whole order of law? Yet under the
pretext that judgment must not be made, these impure dogs desire the supreme
judgments of the highest God, which he has published to the whole world, to be
void, and whatever he has pronounced to be a mere frustration. We therefore
see that, just as we must not dare to render judgments according to the whim
of our own mind, so everything God has judged must be approved and regarded as
ratified, as it were, by our assent. Therefore, to testify that he is a good
and equitable judge, let us condemn wicked works with him and, as he warns,
judge a good or evil tree by its fruits.

“They next allege this saying: ‘Hypocrite, first take the beam out of your own
eye, and then you will remove the speck from your brother's eye.’ But what do
they wish to conclude from it? This rebuke pertains to hypocrites who conceal
their own vices and observe only those of others. Similarly, when he said to
those accusing the adulteress, ‘Let the one among you who is without sin be
the first to cast a stone at her,’ he did not thereby forbid the punishment of
criminals. He only warns that when we reprove others we should begin with
ourselves, and that when we correct those who have sinned we should be free of
the crimes for which we punish them. Thus we may show that we are led by true
zeal for justice and hate evil both in ourselves and in others, without any
partiality. But there is a very great difference between judging nothing
hypocritically and not judging at all. The Lord commands us to judge according
to truth, lest we be more severe against our neighbors than against ourselves.
These dogs, however, use this pretext to exclude and abolish every judgment,
however consonant with reason. And even if a hypocrite who flatters himself
reproves another, it does not follow that the offender is therefore excused,
for evil must always be condemned. The hypocrite's fault is that he does not
measure himself by the same rule and, readily pardoning himself, uses the
strictest law against others. But, as I said at the beginning, these parasites
do not strive for this without a purpose. For they would open a splendid
entrance for themselves if they persuaded the world that absolutely nothing
must be condemned. Moreover,

[Printed p. 370; PDF p. 35; logical scan 394; OCR object 0403]

it is best to observe Satan's cunning here and the end toward which it aims.
Besides the fact that our neighbors' offenses and the chastisements with which
God punishes them ought sufficiently to warn us not to provoke his wrath
against ourselves, something else is also required of us: our minds ought to
be affected with grief because God is offended and because the sinner's soul
rushes into destruction. We ought to burn with such zeal for God's honor that,
when we see him reviled, we are pressed by the anguish of grief with which our
mind is scorched. We ought to pursue our neighbors with such ready affection
of mind that, when we see destruction threatening them, and especially their
souls, we are moved with mercy.

“But through these scoundrels Satan would make people senseless, so that no
matter what confusion we see we are touched by no concern at all, nor affected
in any way if we behold God's name torn by blasphemies, his sacrosanct commands
violated, souls destroyed, and every crime and vice running rampant. And in
fact these mockers laugh at whatever happens and are touched by no concern,
lest perhaps melancholy kill them—except when something less pleasing to them
is done. Then, having forgotten all these charming speculations about not
judging, they become far sharper and harsher than everyone else. They even
secretly mock those by whom they are fed and stick out their tongues behind
their backs. For all their pleasure and study consists in this: that at ease
and free from care they may indulge themselves pleasantly and cheerfully. They
have, of course, heard this saying of Solomon: ‘By a sorrowful spirit the bones
are dried up.’ So that they may not waste away from thinness, they have found
this method of luxuriating sweetly and calmly after shaking off all anxiety:
namely, by approving everything by which the sons of God must be distressed
and tormented in mind, so that they may no longer have any cause of sadness.
See how, delighting equally in evil things and good, they turn everything to
their advantage. Paul, however, says that this is the culmination of
wickedness: when someone not only commits evils but also gives delighted assent
to them. Therefore, if Paul is to be believed, let us regard these men as the
most deplorable of all people. Not content to sleep in their own evils, they
burst forth to such a degree of wickedness that they even applaud the vices of
others.”

Do these things still not satisfy you? Will you appeal to Calvin's other
books? Hear, then, what he taught long ago about the same matters in
*Institutes of the Christian Religion* 2.4.3: “The manner of the divine action
is far different. To make it more certainly evident to us, let the calamity
inflicted by the Chaldeans upon holy Job serve as an example. After killing his
shepherds, the Chaldeans plunder his flock like enemies. Their wicked deed is
now plainly evident. Nor is Satan inactive in that work, for the history
relates that the whole thing proceeds from him. But Job himself recognizes in
it the Lord's work, saying that the Lord took from him what was carried off by
the Chaldeans. How can we refer the same work to God, to Satan, and to man as
authors without either excusing Satan through his fellowship with God or
proclaiming God the author of evil? Easily, if we first consider the end of the
action and then its manner. The Lord's purpose is to exercise his servant's
patience by calamity; Satan endeavors to drive him to despair; the Chaldeans
seek to gain from another's property contrary to right and justice. So great a
diversity of purposes greatly distinguishes the work. There is no less a
difference in the manner.

“The Lord permits Satan to afflict his servant. He permits and hands over to
Satan the Chaldeans, whom he chooses as ministers for executing this, to be
driven by him. Satan incites the otherwise wicked minds of the Chaldeans with
his poisonous goads to perpetrate this outrage. They rush furiously into
injustice and bind and contaminate all their members with crime. Satan is
therefore properly said to act in the reprobate, in whom he exercises his
kingdom, that is, the kingdom of wickedness. God too is said to act in his own
manner, because Satan himself, since he is an instrument of God's wrath, turns
himself this way and that at God's will and command to execute his just
judgments. I omit here God's universal motion, from which all creatures derive
the efficacy to do anything, just as they derive their sustenance. I speak
only of that special action which appears in each deed. We therefore see that
it is not absurd for the same deed to be assigned to God, Satan, and man; but
the difference of end and manner causes God's blameless justice to shine forth
there, while the wickedness of Satan and man exposes itself with its own
disgrace.”

What more? Let us also hear what he writes in the book *On the Eternal
Predestination of God* against Pighius, whose shamelessness you have proposed
for yourself to imitate: “Pighius falsely objects that, if man's apostasy is
God's work, what Scripture teaches—that all things God made are good—is false.
But I can solemnly testify and candidly profess that this invention never came
into my mind. I everywhere assert that human nature was first created upright
precisely so that the depravity which man contracted for himself by revolt
cannot be ascribed to God; and that death, to which the man who formerly
possessed life enslaved himself, proceeded in such a way from his own fault
that God is not regarded as its author.”

[Printed p. 371; PDF p. 36; logical scan 395; OCR object 0404]

The same author in the same book writes: “Let people impute to themselves
whatever they sin. If anyone tries to evade this, I say that he is more tightly
bound by the chains of conscience than to extricate himself from just
condemnation. Let Adam allege as he pleases that he was deceived by the charms
of the wife God gave him. Yet within he will find the deadly poison of
unbelief; within, ambition, the worst counselor; within, the diabolical fan of
audacity. Much less excusable, then, will be those who try to draw the cause of
their evils from the deep sanctuaries of God, a cause which thrusts itself
forth from their own vicious heart. Let each person accuse his own sins,
condemn himself, and, having confessed his fault from the heart, diligently
entreat his judge. If anyone protests, the answer is ready: ‘Your destruction
is from yourself, Israel.’”

In the same book, addressing you yourself, Castellio, he writes: “Those who
claim that we make God the author of sin if his will is the cause of all things
that happen burden us with a foul and servile slander. For what a person
perpetrates unjustly, stirred by ambition, avarice, lust, or any wicked
affection, cannot receive the name *sin* in God when God performs it through
that person's hand by a just, though hidden, judgment. Treachery, cruelty,
pride, intemperance, envy, blind self-love, and some wicked desire make sin in
a person. You will find nothing of this kind in God.” And a little later he
says: “Such is God's manner of acting in human sins that, when one comes to
him, by his purity he entirely wipes away every stain.”

What, then? Are these things not sufficient to explain Calvin's view and prove
your falsehood? Let us therefore add what he wrote on the passage where Peter
reproaches the Jews for crucifying Jesus, delivered up by the definite counsel
and foreknowledge of God, through wicked hands: “Because Peter seems to imply
that the wicked obeyed God, one of two absurdities follows: either that God is
the author of evils, or that people do not sin when perpetrating any crime. I
answer that, however much the wicked execute what God has decreed within
himself, nothing is further from them than obeying God. For obedience arises
from a willing affection. But we know that the wicked have a far different
purpose. Moreover, no one obeys God unless his will is known to him. Obedience
therefore depends upon knowledge of the divine will. God has made his will
known to us in the Law. Thus only those obey God who measure their works by the
rule of the Law and then willingly submit themselves to his command. We see
nothing of this sort in all the wicked, whom God drives unwittingly this way or
that. Therefore, no one will say that they are excusable under the pretext that
they obey God, since God's will must be sought by us in the Law and they, so
far as lies in them, desire to resist God.

“As for the other point, I deny that God is the author of evil, because this
term contains a definite connotation of a wicked affection. For an evil deed is
evaluated from the end toward which each person tends. When people perpetrate
theft or murder, they sin because they are thieves or murderers. In theft and
murder there is a criminal purpose. God, who uses their wickedness, must be
placed on a higher plane. For he aims at something far different, since he
wills to chastise one person and exercise the patience of another, and thus
never turns aside from his nature, that is, from perfect rectitude. Thus that
Christ was delivered through wicked hands and was crucified happened by God's
direction and ordination. But the betrayal, which in itself is abominable, and
the killing, which contains so great a crime in itself, must not be regarded as
God's work.”

What more need be said? The day would certainly fail me if I recounted
testimonies of this kind. Thus even if I had produced only one of them, I think
there would be no one to whom your supreme and unheard-of shamelessness would
not be sufficiently evident. Nevertheless, I shall apply still others in their
proper places. And lest you complain that I obscure an obscure matter rather
than explain it, I shall set forth briefly our view concerning God's ordination
and action, even in those things which deserve the name *sin* not insofar as
they are performed by God, but insofar as they are performed by instruments;
likewise, what distinction between permission and will we teach from God's
Word. Then I shall answer your arguments one by one. I have decided to explain
these matters in brief and plain aphorisms so that you cannot complain of
obscurity.

I. God *energei*—that is, he efficaciously acts or effects all things according
to the counsel of his will.

II. God indeed executes this counsel at definite moments in time, but the
counsel itself is eternal and precedes all things not only in time, since it is
before all time, but also in order. Otherwise God's will would not be the
primary rule of God's counsel,

[Printed p. 372; PDF p. 37; logical scan 396; OCR object 0405]

but rather the foreseen or foreknown qualities of things, which impelled God
to adopt this or that counsel, would prescribe a rule to God's own will.

III. This counsel cannot be separated from God's will without despoiling God
of his Divinity.

IV. This counsel is not situated merely in the moderation and direction of the
outcome, as Pallas is represented by the Poet turning Pandarus's weapon away
from Menelaus's breast toward the lower parts protected by his belt. Rather, it
has an active and effecting power in all things, which Paul expressed by the
verb *energein*.

V. This power and efficacy are attributed to God as agent; they are not said
merely to be *from God*. Thus the term does not signify a natural capacity
implanted in certain things by God the Creator so that they may effect this or
that, but God's own power which he possesses in himself to effect all things.

VI. The universal particle in this saying of Paul cannot be restricted by the
least exception whatever without making God idle in that respect according to
Epicurus's dogma. But if we say that anything is done against his will, he will
be despoiled of his infinite power.

VII. The conclusion therefore stands: just as God himself decrees from
eternity all future things according as it pleased him, so at their proper
time he performs them by his own efficacy, just as he wills.

VIII. Nevertheless, none of these blasphemies follows: either that God is the
author of sin, delights in wickedness, or even wills wickedness; or that Satan
or people, by acting wickedly, obey God, or insofar as they act wickedly do
what God wills, and are therefore free from blame. Far indeed from not only our
tongues but also our thoughts be all blasphemies of this kind.

IX. That these inferences are invalid can be demonstrated as follows. God
executes the counsels of his will through secondary causes and intermediate
instruments—not as though bound to them, as the Stoics thought, but freely and
powerfully fashioning, moving, and directing them as it pleased him.

X. There are two principal kinds of these instruments. Some are animate and
are moved by an internal motion of their own; others are inanimate and are
moved only by an external impulse. Animate instruments, in turn, fall into two
classes. Some are endowed with reason and judgment; others lack reason and are
carried by the blind impulse of nature.

XI. Inanimate things, and indeed things which are animate but lack reason, are
said to act neither well nor badly, because they are acted upon rather than
acting. But those who use these instruments are said to act either well or
badly.

XII. Animate beings endowed with reason and judgment are either angels or
humans, and these in turn are of two kinds. Some angels are good and others
evil. All humans, however, are evil by nature, but by grace they are so
distinguished that some are altogether evil and others good to some degree,
namely insofar as God's Spirit has sanctified them.

XIII. When beings of this kind are moved in some action by that internal
motion of their own, they are rightly said to act. Thus the distinction between
a good and evil action applies only to this kind of instrument. Indeed, in this
respect they cannot properly be called instruments, but rather efficient
causes.

XIV. I call an action evil when it does not have the revealed will of God as
its goal; a good action, by contrast, when it regards that will.

XV. These same beings, although causes insofar as they act by an internal
motion of their own, are nevertheless called instruments in another respect
whenever and insofar as they are acted upon by another. Thus an executioner
kills a person by the magistrate's command; some harm others under Satan's
impulse; or at someone's command and in his name we do good or evil to another.

XVI. In this species of actions everyone sees that one and the same work is
attributed to two parties: to one as impelling and acting through the other as
an instrument, and to the other as himself acting. For he is an instrument in
such a way that he also acts by his own internal motion, not simply as a hammer
or axe in a craftsman's hand.

XVII. Indeed, because of this twofold relation, the work too sometimes appears
twofold, even to such a degree that one is praiseworthy and the other vicious.
For if a magistrate hands a guilty person over to the executioner, everyone
rightly praises that work; but if the executioner, from hatred or avarice,

[Printed p. 373; PDF p. 38; logical scan 397; OCR object 0406]

or driven by some other wicked desire, kills that man with regard to his own
passion rather than the judge's command, he certainly cannot escape the guilt
of murder before God.

XVIII. Let us now apply these things to God himself, whose efficacy we proved
above to intervene without any exception in everything that happens, and in
such a way that through the things he created, as through instruments, he
executes in its time whatever he decreed from eternity.

XIX. Whatever God does is good, since no evil can proceed from the highest
good. But he effects all things. All things, therefore, are good insofar as
they are effected by God. The distinction between good and evil has a place
only in the instruments, and specifically those of which we spoke in
proposition 15.

XX. For if these instruments are good and regard God's revealed will, they act
well, and God too acts well through them. The work is therefore good in every
respect, as when the good angels execute what God commands and holy people
follow God when he calls.

XXI. Evil instruments—evil, I say, not from creation but from corruption—always
act badly insofar as they act and therefore justly incur God's wrath. But
insofar as God acts through them, they serve God's good work either unwillingly
or in ignorance. For through whatever instruments God acts, he always acts
well.

XXII. He acts through those instruments in such a way that he not only permits
them to act and not only governs the outcome, but also arouses, impels, moves,
and rules them, and indeed—greatest of all—even creates them so that through
them he may do what he has determined. God does all these things rightly and
without any injustice.

XXIII. For whenever an evil person sins against himself or against another
person who is likewise evil, God without any sin causes either the evil person
to avenge himself or evil people to inflict deserved punishments upon other
evil people. Both are most just works of God, and by these examples of his
judgments God raises up and consoles his own.

XXIV. Whenever the evil harm the good, they themselves indeed sin and finally
pay deserved penalties. Nevertheless, through them the Lord chastises,
instructs, and strengthens his own, and plainly renders his Church glorious
through the Church's own enemies.

XXV. Yet these evil instruments cannot be said to obey God. Although God
effects his own work through them, they themselves, so far as lies in them and
with respect to their purpose and will, do not perform God's work but their
own, for which they are deservedly punished. For although whatever God does
through the evil is good, whatever the evil do is evil.

XXVI. Nor is this inference valid: God effects all things; therefore he also
commits sins. For the name *sin* applies only to a vicious quality, which lies
entirely in the acting instrument.

XXVII. By reason of this vicious quality, however, the work, which in itself is
one, becomes in a certain manner twofold and double, so much so that one work
is diametrically opposed to the other—that is, God's just work to man's unjust
work.

XXVIII. Nevertheless, God acts differently through good instruments than
through evil ones. Besides the fact that he does his work through good
instruments, the good instruments also do their own work by the power and
efficacy which the Lord supplies to them. In short, the Lord does his own work
through them and also works in them both to will and to accomplish. But through
the evil—such as Satan, or humans insofar as they are not regenerate—whenever
the Lord executes the just counsels and decrees of his eternal will, he exerts
his power and efficacy in his own work through them, whether ignorant or
unwilling. Yet insofar as they perform their own work, the Lord does not work
in them at all, but loosens Satan's reins. By his just judgment he also hands
the ungodly over to Satan to be moved and aroused, so that they are carried
according to their own and his will.

XXIX. We therefore do not reject the term *permission* or *concession*, nor did
it ever enter our mind to say that God acts in the evil just as he does in the
good. But because the Sophists corrupted the distinction between will and
permission—which Augustine doubtless took from the Greeks and they drew from
Augustine—we utterly reject that distinction as they employ it.

XXX. For the Sophists oppose will to permission, from which it follows that God
permits what he permits either unwillingly, or at least idly and without caring
about it. We, by contrast, lest we either deprive God of his infinite and
immeasurable power or agree with the Epicureans, state the fact: God does
nothing except willingly, whether acting through instruments

[Printed p. 374; PDF p. 39; logical scan 398; OCR object 0407]

or permitting instruments to act; yet he most justly does whatever he does and
most justly permits whatever he permits.

XXXI. He acts with respect to his own work, but permits with respect to the
work which the evil instruments themselves perform spontaneously—that is,
insofar as they are active, not passive instruments, to retain the terms
customary in the schools. Yet he justly permits what they do unjustly, because
sins, insofar as they are permitted by a willing God, are not sins but the
punishments of sins. For before God it is just to avenge sins even by sins. But
these same actions, insofar as they proceed from Satan and from evil people
solicited by Satan and their own desire, are at that point sins, which the Lord
justly avenges in his own time. For the Lord never permits sins insofar as they
are sins; rather, he always prohibits and forbids them.

XXXII. Nor is this inference valid: God wills all things; therefore he approves
all things. He wills, and therefore permits, many things not because he
approves them simply, but *in a certain respect*. He approves them insofar as
he permits them, because to that extent they are not sins, as we just said; but
he disapproves and avenges them insofar as he regards the vicious instruments
whose actions they are.

XXXIII. These are Augustine's words in the *Enchiridion to Laurentius*, chapter
100: “Great are the Lord's works, sought out according to all his wills, so
that in a wonderful and ineffable manner what is done against his will is not
done apart from his will; for it would not be done if he did not permit it, and
he does not permit it unwillingly, but willingly.” The same author in book 5
*Against Julian*, chapter 3, where he expressly disputed against those who
posit an idle foreknowledge or permission, finally bursts into these words:
“Certainly, if we permit those over whom we have power to perpetrate crimes
before our eyes, we will share their guilt. Yet how innumerable are the things
he permits to be done before his eyes, things which he would by no means permit
if he did not will them—and nevertheless he is just and good.”

XXXIV. All Scripture testifies, and common sense itself dictates, that nothing
happens without God's will—not even the things that appear most fortuitous.
Thus in Genesis 27:20 God is said immediately to have delivered the prey into
Jacob's hands. And in Exodus 21:13, whenever an unpremeditated killing occurs,
Moses says, “The Lord caused him to fall into your hands.” The same is taught
of the outcome of lots in Proverbs 16:33; of all human counsels in Daniel 4:32;
of a sparrow's fall in Matthew 10:29; and finally of all things without
exception in Ephesians 1:11.

XXXV. That God's will—and indeed his most efficacious will—also intervenes when
he acts through the evil appears clearly from almost every page of Scripture.
Thus God himself is said to have sent Joseph into Egypt, Genesis 45:8. Thus he
raised up Pharaoh to make his power manifest in him, Exodus 4:21. Thus he hands
David's wives over to his son Absalom, 2 Samuel 12:11. Thus he incites David's
heart to number the people, 2 Samuel 24:1. Thus he commands Shimei to curse
David, 2 Samuel 16:10. Thus David calls his adversaries the Lord's sword and
hand, Psalm 17:13–14. Thus the Lord calls the Medes and Persians his consecrated
ones and instruments of his wrath, Isaiah 10:5 and 13:6. Thus he calls the
revolt of the ten tribes his work, 2 Chronicles 11:4. Thus Job says, “The Lord
gave; the Lord has taken away,” Job 1:21. Thus the king of the Babylonians is
compared to an axe and saw, because through him, though he thought nothing of
the kind, the Lord executed his work, Isaiah 10:15. Thus the godly are afflicted
by God's will and predestination, Romans 8:29 and 1 Peter 3:17 and 4:19. Thus
there is no evil in a city which God has not done, Amos 3:6. And Jeremiah says
in Lamentations 3:37–38: “Who is this who spoke and it happened, though God did
not command it? Therefore do not evils and good things proceed from the Lord's
mouth?”

XXXVI. Come, then, let the work at once most excellent and most criminal of all
be chosen as an example: most excellent if we consider either the Father's
infinite justice and mercy or the Son's infinite obedience and love; most
criminal if we consider the instruments themselves—Satan, Judas, the Jews,
Pilate, and Herod. This work is the death of the Son of God, full of cruelty
and disgrace. If we deny that God's eternal counsel intervened in this deed,
countless testimonies of Scripture will convict us. For it is certain that we
were chosen in Christ—who was destined to die and no other—before the
foundations of the world were laid, Ephesians 1:4 and 1 Peter 1:20. Hence the
Lamb is also said to have been slain from the origin of the world, Revelation
13:8—not merely by foreknowledge, but above all by God's definite counsel, so
much so that Herod and Pilate, although thinking nothing of the kind, assembled
in order to execute the things which the Lord's hand and counsel had determined
should happen, Acts 4:28. Therefore he could not

[Printed p. 375; PDF p. 40; logical scan 399; OCR object 0408]

be seized except at his own hour, John 7:30; 8:19; and 12:27. For he was
delivered up by God's definite counsel and preceding decree, Acts 2:23, and was
wounded by God for our iniquities, Isaiah 53:5. For it is God who did not spare
his own Son but delivered him up for us all, Romans 8:32. Thus if only this one
example of God's eternal and never idle providence existed, it would be amply
sufficient to convict all those who falsely cry out that we make God the
author of sin when we say that nothing occurs except by God's just will.

XXXVII. For on this account we do not excuse but rather most vehemently accuse
Satan, who works in the children of disobedience, Ephesians 2:2, even when the
Lord performs his work most efficaciously and justly both through Satan and
through Satan's captives, 2 Timothy 2:26. We therefore acknowledge and venerate
God's goodness and judgments everywhere, even if their reason is often not
known to us. But we condemn both the instruments themselves which are evil and
their wicked and criminal actions: all Satan's counsels and stratagems;
Joseph's brothers' envy and fratricide; Pharaoh's impiety and hardness;
Absalom's parricidal mind and execrable incest; David's own thoughtlessness;
Shimei's wickedness; the malice and treachery of David's adversaries; the
sacrilegious revolt of Jeroboam and the ten tribes; the Chaldeans' rapacity;
the Babylonians' insatiable avarice, unbelievable lust, and intolerable
arrogance; and finally all the ungodly's wicked counsels and monstrous cruelty
against the Church.

XXXVIII. That God also avenges sins by sins, and that by a permission which is
not idle but truly efficacious and active, yet nevertheless most just, is clear
from many exceedingly transparent testimonies of Scripture. It is he who gives
kings in his wrath, Nehemiah 9:37 and Job 34:30. It is he who causes people to
err, Isaiah 63:17, because he mixes a spirit of error, Isaiah 19:14. It is he
who hardens and turns hearts, blinds eyes, and makes people drunk with the wine
of dizziness, Exodus 4:21; 7:4; 9:12; 10:1, 20; 14:4; Deuteronomy 2:30; Joshua
11:20; 1 Samuel 2:25; 2 Chronicles 22:7; Psalm 105:25. It is he who avenges
contempt for himself by handing people over to a reprobate mind, Romans 1:28,
and sending an efficacy of error so that they believe the lie, 2 Thessalonians
2:11. It is he who deceives prophets, Ezekiel 14:9. Finally, it is he who sends
even evil spirits, adding a command to harm and granting efficacy to deceive,
as in 1 Kings 22:22–23; 2 Chronicles 18:21; and Job 1 and 2. Go now and, if you
please, cry yourselves hoarse that we make God the author of sin.

But come, let us hear how powerful are the arguments with which you attack us.

#### Castellio's first argument concerning the third calumny

“Against the third point, concerning the difference between will and
permission, they say this: Calvin says that he is a prophet of God; we say that
Calvin is a prophet of the devil. But one party must speak falsely. For if he
is a prophet of God, we lie; but if he is a prophet of the devil, he himself
lies when he says that he is a prophet of God. What if both happen because God
wills it—that is, if God wills Calvin to say that he is a prophet of God and
us to say that he is a prophet of the devil? Then he wills contradictory
things, which is impossible. For if God wills falsehood, he does not will
truth; or if he wills truth, he does not will falsehood. It follows that if he
wills one party to speak truth, he does not will the other to lie. Yet one
party certainly lies. Therefore they lie with God's permission, not by his
will. Thus even in God there is a difference between permission and will.”

#### Refutation

Your argument is of this kind: God cannot will contraries; falsehood and truth
are contraries; therefore he cannot will both. But he permits falsehood and
wills truth; therefore there is a difference between will and permission. Now
hear my answer. I say that there is an obvious paralogism in the term *will*.
Sometimes will is said only of the things which we approve simply and in
themselves. Sometimes it extends more widely and includes things which, though
we do not at all approve them in themselves, we nevertheless embrace for the
sake of other things, even to the extent that we commend them and, if need be,
execute them to the best of our ability. Let this serve as an example. No one
but a wholly bloodthirsty person simply delights in war, since it brings
countless evils with it. Nevertheless, a prince will be an enemy of his
country if, whenever he cannot protect his people in another way, he does not
prefer to wage a legitimate war rather than abandon them to the enemies' lust.
Therefore a good

[Printed p. 376; PDF p. 41; logical scan 400; OCR object 0409]

and humane prince does not simply will war, if you consider war in itself. Yet
he wills war for just causes and does not permit it to his subjects while
remaining idle; rather, willingly and of his own accord, even at risk to his
life, he conducts it. And who but a wholly monstrous person would think that a
magistrate delights in any citizen's pain, much less his death? Nevertheless,
although grieved and in no way approving the killing insofar as it is a
killing, the same magistrate will inquire into the criminal citizen—not idly
or unwillingly, but with the greatest diligence and will—and, once he is
convicted, hand him to the executioner, adding a command that he be subjected
to this or that punishment. Yet war and peace, preservation and killing, are
contraries. Who is so foolish as therefore to deny that a prince wills both at
one and the same time? “But in what way?” you will ask. Because war does not
oppose peace insofar as the prince wills it; rather, it serves peace. Nor does
killing a citizen oppose the citizens' safety insofar as a good prince kills
an evil citizen, since he rightfully kills him in order to preserve the good.
Do you finally see that the things you call contradictory agree perfectly with
one another?

If you are compelled to recognize this distinction in human actions
themselves, will you—who do not hesitate to measure all God's works by common
sense—not permit your rule to hold here? But come, let us measure these things
by the prescription and exact standard of the Divine Word itself. We confess
that what David says, “You are not a God who wills wickedness,” is perpetually
true, and we regard anyone who thinks otherwise as ungodly and blasphemous. But
you infer that when we say, without exception, that nothing is thought, said,
or done in which God's will does not intervene, we also mean that God wills it
as the author of every crime and outrage. We say that this is nothing but a
most obvious slander. We affirm that the distinction of good and evil cannot
apply to God, even though, insofar as he is God, he is in his own peculiar and
proper manner the cause of every single thing. For insofar as all things occur
by the decree and will of God as agent, as the Apostle says—whether he executes
his work through good or evil instruments—they are always just, good, and holy.
But insofar as the instruments act, their actions are sometimes good and
therefore pleasing to God, and sometimes evil and therefore displeasing to God
and liable to his just judgment. For although God wills and does everything
that happens, he does not will or do everything in the same manner that the
instruments themselves will or do it. The fault is in the instruments, not in
God.

We must now see which works of the instruments are good and which evil. To
explain this conveniently, we say that *God's will* is used in two ways.
Sometimes it embraces only the things which the Lord simply approves, because
their quality is good in itself. Thus David says, “Teach me to do your will,”
and we ask the Father that his will be done on earth as in heaven—that is, that
our whole life be conformed to his will. In these passages, I say, and
countless others, *God's will* signifies nothing but what the Lord has revealed
to us in his Word as pleasing and delightful to himself. Nor do we say that
Satan or people, insofar as they act badly, do God's will; rather, they resist
God's will and therefore finally pay the just penalties for their crimes. We
accordingly define as evil those things whose quality is such that they
displease God in themselves, and as good those which God approves in themselves
and simply.

But sometimes God's will also embraces things which he does not simply approve
but indeed rejects and avenges, yet which are decreed by him and please him
*in a certain respect* and with a definite regard. Because you do not
understand this, you foolishly criticize it. What, then? Will you deny that
those who afflict the Church sin very gravely? Yet if perhaps you deny that the
saints are afflicted by God's will, Peter himself will refute you in two
passages. “It is better,” he says, “if God's will so wills, that you suffer
evils for doing well than for doing evil.” And in the same Epistle: “Let those
who suffer evils according to God's will entrust their souls to the faithful
Creator by doing good.” Do you hear the word *his*? What does Paul say when he
treats this very subject? “Those whom he foreknew,” he says, “he predestined to
be conformed to the image of his Son.” We now understand, then, that God's
predestination and will intervene in the saints' afflictions. Elsewhere Peter,
discussing Christ's cross in the passage we have already cited often, mentions
the foreknowledge and counsel of God; and elsewhere the whole Jerusalem Church
commemorates God's hand and counsel. But does God approve the wickedness of
those who persecute the Church? Certainly not. The ungodly persecute the Church
and finally pay deserved penalties; but precisely by rightfully avenging
himself upon his enemies, God tests his Church and daily perfects her, and in
this respect

[Printed p. 377; PDF p. 42; logical scan 401; OCR object 0410]

the will, counsel, fixed and definite decree, and finally the very action of
the most just and wise God intervene no less in those actions than when he
employs chosen and holy instruments for things that are in themselves best and
most excellent. And certainly, if this consolation is taken from us—as it
surely is if we believe you, dreaming up I know not what idle permission outside
God's will and decree in matters of this kind—by what means shall we at last
remain firm and steadfast? For the man who supposes that only the event and
outcome of all such things is governed by God has not said everything. Rather,
we must above all be persuaded of this: Satan or Satan's instruments cannot
contrive even the smallest thing against us which that good Father of ours has
not decreed and purposed from eternity for our benefit. Only so can our
consolation be full and solid.

But perhaps, in this species of evil by which the godly are afflicted, you will
deny that Peter, Paul, and the other holy writers, when they mention God's will,
counsel, decree, and hand, have in view those who inflict these evils on the
Church, and will say that they have in view instead those who endure them. Yet
if I answer this objection, I fear someone may think that I wrong you by
supposing that questions so inept could be proposed by you. Nevertheless I
shall untie even this knot, since I see that nothing is so clear in itself and
free of all difficulty that you do not eagerly seize upon it in order to
entangle both yourselves and others.

First of all, these two things—the action of those who persecute the saints,
and the suffering of the saints afflicted by them—are so bound together by
mutual relation that anyone who says God wills the latter but only permits the
former may deservedly be thought devoid even of common sense. But lest I seem
to contend with you by arguments drawn solely from common sense—though you
yourself appear to have prescribed this rule of combat for us—come, let us hear
the Spirit of God himself speaking. If only at last you might prefer his
authority to your empty and futile inventions!

Job was delivered by the Lord into Satan's hand, not on account of any sin of
his own, but in order to confirm both him and us by a most beautiful example.
“The Lord,” he says, “gave; the Lord has taken away.” What would you answer
here? Does the verb *take away* apply to Job himself, from whom the goods were
snatched, or rather to the one who snatched them? Yet Job neither cries out
that an injury is being done to him nor excuses the robbers; instead, in both
giving and taking away he acknowledges and adores the just will of God—the
*will* of God, I say, is what he acknowledges. For who does not see that by the
verb *take away*, just as by *give*, Job signified not only the will and counsel
to give and take away, but also the very giving and taking? For both in giving
and in taking away the Lord employs whatever instruments he pleases, and does
so by no means under compulsion but willingly. Thus David, in a similar case,
as we explained above, calls his adversaries, however criminal, nevertheless
God's sword and hand. And will you go on to deny that not merely God's counsel
or will but also his efficacy and hand intervene in the saints' afflictions,
even with respect to the persecutors themselves? At least hear what Paul says
about the most bitter death of the Son of God: “He did not spare his own Son,”
he says, “but delivered him up for us all.” And will you acknowledge here no
decree, no will of God, our best and most benevolent Father?

Let us now proceed to that species of evils by which the Lord justly chastises
his saints, although employing the hands and agency of criminals; and here too
let us demonstrate that God's counsel, decree, will, and finally action
intervene. What examples of savagery, cruelty, and impiety did the Chaldeans not
display in Jerusalem and throughout Judea? Who was it that exerted his hand,
struck Judah, raised the banner and summoned the nations to arms, aroused the
most distant peoples with a whistle, and brought the greatest and most violent
waters against Judah? Let us hear the Lord himself answer in Isaiah: “O Asshur,
the rod of my fury, and the staff in their hand is my indignation! I shall send
him against a hypocritical nation, and against the people of my indignation I
shall command him to seize spoil, to plunder booty, and to make it a trampling
place like the mud of the streets.”

And what does the Lord say in Jeremiah? “I shall send,” he says, “and take all
the nations of the North and Nebuchadnezzar my servant, the king of Babylon, and
I shall bring them upon this land, against its inhabitants and against all
these nations round about, and I shall lay them waste.” And in Ezekiel: “Woe to
the city of blood,” he says; “I too shall increase the burning; I shall
multiply the burning by kindling the fire, boiling the flesh, and sprinkling in
spices, so that even the bones may be burned up.” What more? “Thus says the
Lord God: Behold, I am profaning my sanctuary, the magnificence of your
strength, the desire of your eyes, and the object of your soul's compassion.”
Tell me, then: does the one who himself rouses the enemy, assembles the forces,
leads the army and brings it into the city itself, commands that no one be
spared, lights the fire and feeds it in every way, and finally exposes the
Temple itself to everyone's greed and lust—does he do nothing, but merely
abandon,

[Printed p. 378; PDF p. 43; logical scan 402; OCR object 0411]

or idly allow something to happen, or only govern the outcome of events? If you
deny that this is God's work—God's work, I say, not as one idly watching but as
one effectively doing this very thing through Nebuchadnezzar—then believe the
Lord himself rather than yourself. For when the Lord discusses this very
matter, that is, the city's devastation, he says: “After the Lord has completed
all his work on Mount Zion and Jerusalem, I shall visit the fruit of the
haughtiness of the king of Asshur's heart.” Why, then, do you flee to the
refuge of that empty and idle permission? Surely this amounts to saying that
God works idly and unwillingly—unless perhaps you dare accuse the Prophets
themselves, indeed the Lord himself, of blasphemy or at least impudence.

And what of the time when David was assailed with Shimei's curses? “Thus,” says
David, “he curses me, because the Lord commanded him to curse David.” Again,
when the ten tribes revolted not only from Rehoboam but also from the Lord
himself—a revolt for which they afterwards paid the gravest penalties—does not
Scripture expressly testify that it was brought about by the Lord that the king
did not listen to the people? I certainly invent nothing here, since in
narrating that history the Holy Spirit uses the words נסבה and נהיה, than
which I do not see how anything could be said more actively
(*energetikōteron*). Therefore the Lord himself, speaking about this revolt and
addressing Rehoboam's army through the prophet Shemaiah, says: “Do not go up,
nor fight against your brothers, the children of Israel. Let each man return to
his house, for this thing has been done by me.”

Moreover, when the Lord threatened to avenge David's adultery by the penalty of
a similar and indeed greater crime—namely incest—come, what words does he use?
“I shall take,” he says, “your wives before your eyes and give them to your
closest friend, who will sleep with your wives in the sight of this sun.”
Behold the Lord's own words, good sir. Go now, then, and accuse God himself of
blasphemy. Why should you not dare this, when with brazen and shameless face you
dare denounce his servants as ungodly and blasphemous for affirming these same
things? “But these things,” you will say, “require some interpretation.” We
shall see about that later.

Let us now come to the third species of evils: those through which the Lord
either avenges the wicked by means of the good, or sets the wicked against one
another, so that, as the hammer wears down the anvil, they in turn wear down
and destroy each other. Here we affirm that not that empty, fictitious
permission or abandonment of which you dream, but God's counsel, decree, will,
and finally action truly intervene; and these are most just and holy, even when
evil instruments do wickedly what God most justly grants to them and then
executes through them.

What, then? Will you deny that good men are stirred by God to destroy the
wicked? I think not. As for wicked men set against one another, even when they
wrong each other, what we must think is apparent even from that passage of
Isaiah in which the Lord calls the Persians and Medes, whom he was going to
rouse against the Babylonians, his consecrated ones and the instruments of his
wrath. And elsewhere, where the kings who had opposed Joshua are discussed,
the Holy Spirit says: “This was from the Lord, who had hardened their heart so
that they would resist Israel in war, and so that he might deliver them to
destruction and no mercy be shown them, but they be cut off, just as the Lord
had commanded Moses.” We shall treat hardening in its proper place.

Now answer what it means to deceive a false prophet. I know what you will
answer: the usual chatter about abandonment. On this matter, if you please, let
us hear Augustine's words against Julian, as he examines the question and
expressly refutes that separation of permission from will which you teach.
“What is this,” Augustine says to Julian, “that you say: ‘Those who are said to
be handed over to their own desires must be understood to have been abandoned
through divine patience, not driven into sins by power’? As though the same
Apostle had not placed these two together, both patience and power, when he
said: ‘But if God, willing to show his wrath and demonstrate his power, bore
with great patience vessels of wrath fitted for destruction.’ Yet which of
these do you say is meant by what is written: ‘And if the prophet has erred and
spoken, I the Lord have deceived that prophet’? Is it patience or power?
Whichever you choose, or if you acknowledge both, you nevertheless see that
the act of prophesying falsely is both a sin and the punishment of sin. Or will
you say here too that the Lord's statement, ‘I have deceived that prophet,’ is
to be understood as, ‘I abandoned him, so that, deceived by his own deserts, he
might err’? Have it as you wish; nevertheless he was punished for sin in such
a way that he sinned by prophesying falsely. But consider what the prophet
Micaiah saw: the Lord sitting on his throne, and all the host standing around

[Printed p. 379; PDF p. 44; logical scan 403; OCR object 0412]

him on his right and his left. And the Lord said: ‘Who will deceive Ahab king
of Israel, so that he will go up and fall at Ramoth-gilead?’ And one spoke in
one way and another in another. Then a spirit came forth in the Lord's presence
and said, ‘I shall deceive him.’ And the Lord said to him, ‘By what means?’ He
said, ‘I shall go out and be a lying spirit in the mouth of all his prophets.’
And he said, ‘You will deceive him and prevail; go out and do so.’ What will
you say to these things? The king himself, of course, sinned by believing false
prophets. Yet this very thing was also the punishment of sin, with God judging
and God sending an evil angel, so that we might understand more plainly how it
is said in the Psalm that he sent the wrath of his indignation through evil
angels. But did he do so by erring, or by judging or doing anything unjustly or
rashly? Far be it! It was not in vain that it was said to him: ‘Your judgments
are like the great deep.’ Not in vain does the Apostle cry out, ‘O the depth!’”

The same Augustine in the same place says: “Who could be so foolish that, on
hearing what is sung in the Psalm, ‘Do not hand me over, Lord, to my sinful
desire,’ he would say that the man prayed for God not to be patient toward him,
if God hands people over for evils to be done only by showing patient goodness
while they are done? And what is the meaning of what we say daily, ‘Do not lead
us into temptation,’ except that we not be handed over to our lusts? For each
person is tempted when drawn away and enticed by his own lust. Or perhaps we
ask this of God, that his goodness not be patient toward us? Then we do not
invoke his mercy, but rather provoke his anger. Who sane—indeed, who even
insane—would say this? God therefore hands people over to shameful passions so
that they do what is improper; but he himself hands them over properly, and the
same deeds are both sins, punishments of former sins, and grounds for future
punishments. Thus he handed Ahab over to the falsehood of the false prophets,
and Rehoboam to false counsel. The one who knows how to work his just judgments
not only in people's bodies but in their very hearts does these things in
wonderful and ineffable ways. He does not make evil wills, but uses them as he
wills, since he cannot will anything unjustly.”

So much for Augustine against your error. But come, let us at last draw the
conclusion. From the things we have explained in so many words and confirmed by
so many testimonies of Scripture, we say it is sufficiently clear that the
term *will* also extends to things which, though evil either by their own nature
or because of the instruments' evil will, are nevertheless good in a certain
respect, insofar as God through them tests or chastises the good and avenges
himself upon the wicked. Thus God is not contrary to himself whenever he wills
and does not will one and the same work, since this happens in different
respects; nor does he will incompatible things whenever he appears to will
contraries, because things which by their own nature conflict with each other
can nevertheless agree with each other in a certain respect.

And I should like to explain this to you also in that same hypothesis which you
yourself proposed to us. That Calvin is a faithful and true prophet of God is
apparent from every argument by which the integrity of human beings can be
approved before human beings. The Lord therefore wills, and it is pleasing to
him, that Calvin be regarded as what he is, namely an eminent instrument of the
Church; and there is no doubt that those who disparage a good man, as you do,
perform a work displeasing to God. For he said: “He who receives you receives
me, and he who despises you despises me.” Yet because Christ has also been set
for the fall of many—who accordingly love darkness more than light and are
therefore worthy to perish—the Lord in his anger against the world has raised
up you, a false prophet, and others like you everywhere, to whom he has given
an efficacy of error, so that through you those who rejected the truth may
believe the lie. Thus, as formerly in Shimei's case, so now he has commanded
you not to cease barking at and biting the man who has served the Church of God
so very well.

And yet you, so far as it lies in you, do not obey God's will. Instead,
struggling against God and your own conscience, you slander a thoroughly
upright servant of God in the most wicked manner. To this extent, you perform
your own work and that of the spirit which has long possessed and twisted you.
God, however, nonetheless executes a most just work through that barking of
yours. What is done most wickedly by you, and what he least of all wills or
approves insofar as it proceeds from you, he most justly wills and approves in
an entirely contrary respect: insofar, that is, as by this means he tests his
Church; daily keeps his servant more and more within modesty and humility by
buffeting him, or confirms him in perseverance; avenges himself upon an
ungrateful world; and finally brings you and yours to his just judgment—unless
perhaps you should prefer at last to come to your senses.

If you compare these things with what we said is pleasing to God in itself,
namely that his servant be regarded by good men as the man he truly is, you
will find that they no more conflict with each other than when the Lord wills
and commands, at one and the same time, that the South Wind gather clouds which
the North Wind scatters, or that a rock in the sea be battered by contrary
winds. Behold, then,

[Printed p. 380; PDF p. 45; logical scan 404; OCR object 0413]

my very dear sir, what you may answer those from whom you took this futile
argument. Let us now proceed to the others.

II. CASTELLIO'S ARGUMENT FOR THE THIRD CALUMNY

“They also adduce very many examples concerning the distinction between
permission and will. In one, after God had reproached his people in many words
with what he willed, he finally concludes thus: ‘Go, let each serve his idols’;
and, ‘Go, let each serve the dung-god, because you do not obey me’—as if he
said, ‘I permit you to your own lust, since you refuse to obey my commands.’”

REFUTATION

See, you stumble against the same stone once again. For because we say what is
the case—namely, that God is neither unwilling nor idly disdainful of human
affairs, but willingly and always justly and wisely decrees to do what he does,
to command what he commands, and to permit what he permits, since no contagion
from his instruments can in any way infect God's justice and wisdom—you at once
infer from this that we mean whatever God willingly permits he also approves
simply and in itself. But since you dream this while wide awake, as you do many
other things, go ahead, if you please, and fight against your own dreams,
provided everyone understands that this fiction is not ours.

For we affirm that God never wills iniquity, not even when, for reasons hidden
but always just, he loosens the reins of the ungodly or gives them over as
slaves to Satan. What is iniquity with respect to the instruments is judgment
with respect to God; and that which in another respect he condemns, hates in
the instruments, and deservedly avenges is approved by him not simply, but
*kata ti*—in a certain respect—and accordingly is permitted. For it is just
before God that he punish sins by sins, as we demonstrated most fully a little
earlier from God's Word and also from Augustine. Therefore, even if that
passage in Ezekiel were to be understood in precisely the way you suppose, it
would nevertheless not make against us. For we do not say that idolatry is
pleasing to God, although by his just judgment he wills and even brings it
about through Satan and whatever instruments he pleases that those who rejected
the truth embrace a lie and at last fall headlong into all the vilest things.

But none of this pertains to the present passage. For one who speaks as the
Lord does here neither commands nor concedes anything; rather, by the bitterest
irony he reproaches their treachery and ungrateful disposition, so that, if
possible, those who are rebuked may return to the way at least out of shame.
The Lord used a similar manner of speaking when, as Judas was already
approaching, his disciples were drowsing and had so often been admonished in
vain to watch and pray: “Sleep,” he says, “and take your rest.” By this saying
he was so far from either encouraging them to sleep or giving them permission
to sleep that, on the contrary, with the present danger before their eyes—the
danger about which he had warned them in vain—he rebuked them most gravely and
roused them by the sharpest reprimand. And so, when I weighed this argument of
yours, I was truly amazed that a man so untaught could set out to teach others.

III. CASTELLIO'S ARGUMENT FOR THE SECOND CALUMNY

“This seems to be the same as what he had said a little earlier in the same
chapter: ‘Because they rejected my ordinances, I handed them over to
commandments that were not good.’ Yet God did not hand the Israelites over to
commandments that were not good, for all God's commandments are good. But
because they rejected God's good commandments, he abandoned them; and,
abandoned by God, they fell headlong into evil commandments, just as that
prodigal son, abandoned by his father—or rather, having abandoned his
father—fell headlong into dissipation; and just as Paul teaches, because people
did not love the truth, God sent them a spirit of error so that they would
believe a lie.”

REFUTATION

What, I ask, does it mean to send a spirit of error? Do you think this declares
nothing other than to regard someone as abandoned? Or does one who sends
something for some definite purpose either send unwillingly or decree nothing?
What sane person will you persuade of that? But we shall discuss this in the
refutation of the next argument. As for your trifling about the prodigal son's
abandonment, it is nothing. What then? Do you think he was abandoned by an
unwilling father, or by one who cared nothing for his son? If neither is true,
then the father certainly abandoned him willingly; nor did he cast off all
concern for him, but willed that he should at last learn wisdom at his own
expense, as they say, and was thinking most of him precisely when he seemed to
have abandoned him. We shall discuss this in the rebuttal of the sixth
argument. Thus not even this makes against us, but against you, who separate
permission from will. But once again your shameful ignorance

[Printed p. 381; PDF p. 46; logical scan 405; OCR object 0414]

is revealed in your explanation of this passage of Ezekiel. For *handing over
commandments that are not good* does not mean abandoning people so that they
fall headlong into evil commandments. Rather, by *commandments that are not
good* the Lord means tributes, burdens, laws—in short, whatever the victorious
Chaldeans were going to impose upon the conquered. It is as though the Lord
said: “You refused to obey my most holy and equitable commands; but I shall
avenge this treachery of yours most severely, for I shall hand you over to be
ruled by others, who will command you things most unjust and harsh—indeed,
everything most evil—and force you to perform them.” If you could not learn
this exposition from the Latin interpreters, you certainly could have learned
it from the Hebrew ones, had you not been accustomed to despise and disdain
every writer. But does that act of handing over declare your inert
abandonment? You will certainly persuade no one who has so much as greeted the
Prophets from their threshold. Nor is this any less absurd than if you were to
say that criminal men, once convicted and condemned, are not handed over to an
executioner by a willing magistrate but are merely abandoned.

IV. CASTELLIO'S ARGUMENT FOR THE THIRD CALUMNY

“The saying in Amos seems also to be of this kind: ‘Go to Bethel and sin, since
you love to do so.’ In the same way today, because people refused to obey God,
who says that he does not will sin, God permitted spirits of error to arise who
teach that God wills sin, so that those who refused to obey the truth may obey
a lie.”

REFUTATION

Once again I say that you act ineptly when you take the bitterest rebuke and
reproach as permission; and I send you back to the grammarians, so that there
you may learn the force of irony and how many species of it are numbered. But
who can bear calmly that you never cease trifling about a spirit of error? “If
a prophet has been deceived and has spoken a word, I the Lord have deceived
that prophet,” says the Lord in Ezekiel. And elsewhere, what does the Lord say
to the lying spirit? “You will persuade him,” he says, “and prevail; go out,
therefore, and do so.” What does Paul testify? “Because,” he says, “they did
not receive the love of the truth so that they might be saved, for this reason
God will send them an efficacy of error so that they may believe a lie.”

Indeed, consider very carefully, over and over again, whether a man who
dismisses an executioner with definite instructions to inflict just
punishments upon criminals who have been convicted and condemned can be said
merely to abandon them, rather than himself willingly and deliberately arming
the executioner's hand for their just punishment. For if you do not know it,
all these things, although by their own nature they are sins which God hates
and avenges, nevertheless, insofar as they are decreed by God, are nothing but
the punishments of sins. If you do not believe Augustine, who treats this very
argument fully and lucidly against Julian, at least believe Paul himself, who
expressly testifies to it in three passages of the Epistle to the Romans. As
for the sidelong thrust you make against us when you say that spirits of error
arise who teach that God wills sin, we pay it little heed, since by God's
kindness you can easily be convicted both from our writings and our conduct.
But see, on the other hand, how you can clear yourself of manifest slander,
since you suppose that sins are permitted by God insofar as they are sins. And
when I say this, I do not trade insults; I answer slander with a true
accusation.

V. CASTELLIO'S ARGUMENT FOR THE THIRD CALUMNY

“They adduce besides a passage from Zechariah, where God says that he is angry
with the nations at ease because, when he himself was only mildly angry with
the Israelites, they aggravated the evil—that is, they afflicted the
Israelites more severely than God's anger would bear. Therefore it happened
with God's permission, not his will. He adduces a similar example from the
prophet Obed, who rebukes the Edomites because they afflicted the Jews more
severely than the Lord's wrath required.”

REFUTATION

I suspect that this “Obed” of yours is the man whom the Hebrews call Obadiah
and the Latins Abdias. But I cannot excuse your citing from this Prophet things
which are nowhere found in him. For I read in Abdias that the Edomites were to
be severely punished because they had raged most cruelly against their kinsmen,
who were already being afflicted in every way; but what you write is nowhere
found in him. The passage of Zechariah therefore remains. In explaining it,
notice the snares in which you entangle yourself and from which you cannot
extricate yourself. For I ask at what point, as it were, you make that
permission of yours, severed from will, begin. Surely it begins when those
nations crossed the bounds prescribed by God? Let us suppose they had been
more moderate; it certainly follows that

[Printed p. 382; PDF p. 47; logical scan 406; OCR object 0415]

up to that point the Jews were oppressed by them because God so willed. Up to
that point, therefore, they did not sin—at least if we acquiesce in those
principles of yours. For you deny that God's will intervenes in any sins in any
way. But who will grant you that ungodly and criminal men do not sin when
afflicting God's people unless this exception be added: *if they cross the
limit prescribed by God*? Are you so ignorant of what obedience is? Surely it
is rather the force of truth which compels you, against your will, either to
agree openly with us or to disagree with yourself.

But so that you may know: Satan or any ungodly man cannot move so much as a
foot or a finger in order to harm either the good or the wicked, whether he
knows the Lord's decree or not, without sinning most grievously. For a bad tree
always produces bad fruit, even if the best physician has sown and cultivated
it and uses its poisons as certain remedies.

Let this serve as an example of the matter. Suppose some criminal citizen has
been judged an enemy of his country and proscribed on that charge. He encounters
another man equally criminal or even more guilty, who, ignorant of the edict of
proscription, murders the man as a robber, by the very kind of death with which
he would have had to die if the commonwealth had held him in its power. Will
the man who killed the proscribed person seem to you to have obeyed the
commonwealth, or rather to deserve punishment as a robber? How can one who was
ignorant of the edict of proscription seem to have obeyed it?

We must judge the same concerning the Chaldeans and the other enemies of God's
people. Although they afflicted the Jews with deserved penalties and carried
out God's judgments, they cannot nevertheless seem to have obeyed God, whose
very name they did not revere, much less think of complying with his commands.
There is, however, this difference in the example: the commonwealth did not
prepare that robber to kill the man, whereas the Lord called the Chaldeans,
though ignorant, to execute his judgments, as he himself testifies in
countless passages and especially in Ezekiel 25—a passage which, I think,
cannot possibly be read without horror at the divine judgment. The Chaldeans,
therefore, did not properly perform the Lord's work; rather, through those who
knew nothing and contemplated no such thing, the Lord executed his just work,
and nevertheless afterwards avenged himself upon them most justly, as the
Prophets had testified.

For when the Lord had said in Isaiah, “O Asshur, the rod of my fury, and the
staff in their hand is my indignation. I shall send him against a hypocritical
nation, and against the people of my indignation I shall command him to seize
spoil, plunder booty, and make it a trampling place like the mud of the
streets,” he then adds: “Yet Asshur himself will not judge it so, nor will his
heart so conceive it, because in his heart will be the purpose to extirpate and
cut down not a few nations.” And shortly afterward: “But it will come to pass,
when the Lord has completed all his work on Mount Zion and Jerusalem, that I
shall visit the fruit of the magnificence of the king of Asshur and the glory
of the haughtiness of his eyes.” The same Prophet then treats these matters
more fully in chapter thirteen.

But let us also suppose that the ungodly themselves understand what God's
command is. Even so, I say that they can do nothing but sin in carrying out his
command. Let this be an example. A magistrate hands over to an executioner a
criminal who has been lawfully convicted and condemned. The executioner
inflicts upon the man, who happened already to be his enemy, precisely the kind
of punishment prescribed for him; but he is driven by hatred or greed, and
does not set before himself the judge's command but this one purpose instead:
to gratify his own disposition. Before the Lord, who sees the thoughts of the
heart, will that man seem to you innocent, rather than guilty of homicide and
indeed of sacrilege, since he abused the magistrate's holy judgment to satisfy
his own desire? Of this kind was Satan's crime when he was sent to afflict Job
and likewise to deceive Ahab—not by God's bare permission, as you would have
it, but by his express command. It is also likely that the Edomites rebuked by
Obadiah acted in no other way, since it is probable that they were by no means
ignorant of what the Prophets had proclaimed against the Jews.

Therefore, to take in the sails at last: you are right to think that the
ungodly are borne, by God's will, against those upon whom the Lord wills to
avenge himself through them; but here you err in two ways, and with impious
error. First, you suppose they do not sin unless perhaps they exceed the limit;
we have proved this false. Second, you think they can pass beyond the limits
and bounds prescribed by God. It remains now for me to refute that error.

I say, then, that whenever the Lord decrees anything—and foremost among these
are the punishments by which he chastises human sins—he customarily
circumscribes everything by fixed and definite limits, as it were, of places,
times, and persons. I say also that whatever God has decreed is so firm and
stable that, since he himself never changes, all the things he determined must
also attain their outcome, and precisely the outcome he determined. I am not
now discussing secondary causes, lest you accuse me in your usual fashion of
defending Stoic fate. I am discussing the keeping of the boundaries which God
himself defined and circumscribed. These limits
