[Printed p. 383; PDF p. 48; logical scan 407; OCR object 0416]

you think cannot be moved without impious crime; but I judge you convicted of
two blasphemies when you say this. For I admit that, if they could be moved,
the man who did so would deservedly be regarded as sacrilegious and guilty of
injuring the Divine majesty. But I deny that God permits anything to happen
contrary to what he has decreed, for then he would disagree with himself.
Further, I deny that it lies within anyone's power to cross bounds once
established and circumscribed by God's secret decree. For what sort of God
shall we suppose him to be if his sentence is not settled and firm, or if his
decree and nod depend upon secondary causes?

You think you can escape these difficulties by saying that the limits
prescribed by God cannot be crossed unless he permits it but does not will it,
so that his will, of course, may not be found inconstant; but you say
absolutely nothing. For if he permitted it unwillingly, it follows that
something happened which God did not will to happen. Where, then, is his
omnipotence? If you answer that he indeed did not will it to happen, but did
not prevent it, and consequently that his omnipotence is not endangered,
because he could have opposed it but did not will to do so—not even this will
support you, but you will rush from Scylla into Charybdis. For then *to permit*
will mean the same as *not to prevent*, as if someone should allow a torrent
which he could restrain to rush headlong. But if God does not prevent
something, he does not prevent it either because he wills it to happen, or, on
the contrary, because he does not will it to happen, or finally because he does
not care whether the thing happens or not. Choose from these whichever you
like best.

If you say that God wills the things he permits to happen, you agree with
us—or rather with the Lord himself, who testifies that not even sparrows fall
to the earth apart from the heavenly Father's will. Nor on this account is
there a danger that you make God the author of sin, as we explained fully
above. But if you suppose he permits something precisely because he does not
will it to happen, who would not believe you devoid of common sense? For he
would forbid it rather than permit it. Common sense itself surely dictates
that, just as God's single will is sufficient for something to happen, so for
something not to happen it is sufficient that God not will it to happen. The
third option remains: that he permits something to occur which he neither
wills nor does not will to occur, since he does not care whether it happens or
not. But who does not know that this teaching is Epicurean?

Besides, who does not know that God's omnipotence consists not merely in his
ability to do everything he wills, but also in the fact that all the force and
power of every lower cause depends upon him? From where, then, comes this power
except from a willing God? For it cannot be extorted from one unwilling, nor
can it flow and slip, as it were, from the hands of an idle God, so that lower
causes are borne along not by his decision but solely by their own. I do not,
indeed, deny that instruments endowed with will are borne by their own
decision, but in such a way that they are borne toward the end established by
God. For as Augustine wrote most beautifully and truly, God's will is the
necessity of things—not that Stoic necessity which you falsely reproach us
with, but one which does not abolish the spontaneous motion of the will, as
the same writer beautifully explained in the books entitled *The City of God*.

Thus, when you teach that the limits established by God can be crossed when he
does not will but permits it, you either strip him of his omnipotence, or make
him fickle and inconstant, and finally at odds with himself. Even all the
mute elements rebuke this blasphemy in a clear voice, for they have remained
in their station through so many ages and confess that they cannot cross even
a transverse line unless he expressly wills and commands it. “The Strength of
Israel will not deceive,” says Samuel, “nor be moved by repentance, since he
is not a man that he should repent.” And Paul says, “It cannot happen that the
word of God should fall away.” Yet it must fall away in some respect if even
the least thing happens outside his decree, including among the things which
he does not simply approve.

But why do I adduce these witnesses? Even Balaam, that hireling false prophet,
refutes you. “God is not a man that he should lie,” he says, “or a son of man
that he should repent. Does he say and not do? or speak and not confirm it?”
Indeed Satan himself, that lying and unclean spirit, refutes this doctrine.
Although he possesses a thousand arts of doing harm and has such wickedness in
him as we cannot even conceive, you nevertheless see that so hard a bridle has
been put upon him that he cannot even open his mouth unless the Lord rouses him
with his voice, and such tight fetters have been fastened upon him that he
cannot move even a finger except by the Lord's prescription. For he recognizes
that without first seeking power from the Lord he cannot harm anyone even in
the smallest degree. And whenever it is granted him to harm—the Lord grants
him this power according to his good will, either to punish the ungodly or to
draw, recall, test, and chastise his own, as we showed above—he knows himself
compelled to remain within the circumscribed limits, as is clear from the
histories of Job and Ahab and from other testimonies. And so, to come at last
to the passage of Zechariah: although the enemies of the Jews

[Printed p. 384; PDF p. 49; logical scan 408; OCR object 0417]

afflicted them more bitterly than God's wrath would bear, they were
nevertheless not permitted to cross God's decree by even the breadth of a
fingernail. For God's decree extends farther than his wrath. By these words of
the Prophet it is signified that the people ought not measure God's wrath from
the magnitude of the calamities by which they had been afflicted. For the Lord
had sent a large part of those calamities upon them, not to destroy them as one
angry, but, on the contrary, to chastise them as a benevolent father, so that
they might return to good conduct. Thus the Lord, in his customary manner,
lisping with his children like some most merciful father, testifies that he is
offended by the insolence and cruelty of those to whom he had entrusted his
sons to be corrected, not destroyed. For those executioners had dealt with
them more savagely than his wrath would bear, since, so far as it lay in them,
they had cut off the people whom the Lord nevertheless willed only to
chastise—which is also what happened. All these things, however, must be
explained by *anthropopathy*, just as when we read that God was angry or
repented. I shall explain this more fully presently.

For somewhere you trifle that many things happen contrary to God's
destination, as though the outcome of God's counsel depended upon an added
condition, and one of such a kind that it lay within our power. The Basel
Church long ago deservedly execrated all this from God's Word as sacrilegious
blasphemy. And since we have entered upon this discussion, I should like to set
down your words from that impure gathering of four leaves containing your
annotations on Paul's Epistle to the Romans. Although it was condemned with
the fullest justice by the Basel Church, your followers have not ceased to
scatter it through all the churches. I should like, I say, to recite and also
refute those words, because they pertain to this passage.

“It is easy to show,” you say, “that many things happen contrary to a
destination which is joined with a condition. A potter destined clay for a
cooking vessel, but the vessel cracks in the furnace contrary to its
destination. God had destined to bring into Canaan all the Israelites led out
of Egypt, as he showed Moses himself; and for this reason all were washed into
Moses in the cloud. But because they refused to attack the Canaanites, they
perished in the deserts contrary to the destination, just as did the scribes,
who rescinded God's counsel in themselves (Luke 7). God had destined King
Hezekiah for death, but by prayers and piety Hezekiah obtained fifteen years
contrary to the destination. Six hundred such things could be adduced.”

You recognize your words, than which I think scarcely anything more impure
could be said. For who is our God, if his counsel depends upon us? if something
can happen contrary to his will and destination, as you openly say in the same
place? if he changes in the manner of human beings? if human beings can rescind
his counsel? if tomorrow he will not be ashamed to revoke what today he
decreed would happen? I appeal to you, most distinguished senators of a most
renowned city, and to you also, most illustrious and learned governors of the
Basel Academy: how long will you endure this disgrace in your bosom?

But to the matter. You corrupt three testimonies in order to establish this
doctrine of yours. It is true that those Israelites were barred from entering
Canaan only by their own fault; but from where did you learn that the Lord had
ever adopted a different counsel about them? From the fact that all were
baptized in the cloud? As though the Lord had destined for salvation all those
born from Abraham according to the flesh and circumcised, or everyone baptized
in the Christian Church! As for what the Lord says in Moses—“If you enter the
land concerning which I lifted up my hand (that is, swore) to settle you in
it”—if, I say, you think this supports you, you are greatly mistaken and can
easily be refuted from what was written a little earlier. For what does the
Lord answer Moses as he intercedes for the people's safety? “I have forgiven,”
he says, “this people according to your word.” Yet it immediately follows that,
with two exceptions, no one over twenty years old would enter Canaan. What,
then? Clearly the Lord says he spared the people whom he had threatened with
destruction; this must not be understood of each individual person.

The Lord, therefore, had sworn to the people that he would settle them in
Canaan, and this he also did, although those who rebelled did not enter. On the
one hand, he so avenged himself upon the rebels that he nevertheless showed
mercy to their descendants and stood by his promises; on the other, he so
showed himself merciful to those rebels that he afflicted them with just
penalties and nevertheless kept his pledge, as is shown in Ezekiel chapter
twenty. Meanwhile no room is left either for a change of counsel or for
repentance. For everyone understands that the occasions on which the Lord is
said to have repented are figurative expressions.

In the second passage you cite, from Luke, you display the same character. Yet
you yourself were a little more modest when you perverted rather than
translated this passage into Latin. For you rendered it thus: “When they heard
this, the people and the publicans praised God, having been washed with John's
washing. But the Pharisees and experts in the law rescinded God's counsel (so
far as it lay in them) in themselves.” Tell me, then, since the qualification

[Printed p. 385; PDF p. 50; logical scan 409; OCR object 0418]

“so far as it lay in them” had to be added by you, did your conscience not warn
you that the sentence could not stand without this qualification? How does it
happen, then, that after omitting it you now dare twist this testimony? I shall
say nothing here about your “washing”; but who taught you to interpret εἰς
ἑαυτούς as “so far as it lay in them”? You ought rather to have said “the
phrase τὸ εἰς ἑαυτούς.” And since τὸ ἀθετεῖν has various meanings, why did you
choose precisely the one which was the least suitable of all? For who does not
see that the publicans, who embraced God's doctrine, are set in direct
opposition to those who had disdained it and repudiated it from themselves?
The verb ἀθετεῖν very often means precisely this, as in John 12:48 and 1
Thessalonians 4:8, where you yourself used the verb *repudiate*. You see,
therefore, that this testimony is very far from confirming that doctrine of
yours.

There remains what you adduce concerning Hezekiah, examples of which kind you
testify could have been produced by you in great number. But I choose to refute
you with Calvin's own words, because in my judgment nothing can be said on this
matter more briefly, truly, or plainly. Thus he speaks in that golden work
whose true title is *The Institutes of the Whole Christian Religion*, book 1,
chapter 17, sections 13 and 14:

“Just as, when we hear that God is angry, we ought not to imagine some
commotion in him, but rather to reckon that this form of speech is taken from
our way of perceiving, because God presents the appearance of one who is
inflamed and angry whenever he executes judgment; so under the word
*repentance* we ought to conceive nothing other than a change of deeds. Human
beings, by changing what they have done, are accustomed to testify that those
deeds displease them. Since, therefore, every change among human beings is the
correction of something displeasing, and correction proceeds from repentance,
the name *repentance* signifies that God changes something in his works.
Meanwhile neither his counsel nor his will is reversed, nor is his affection
changed; rather, he pursues in unbroken course what he had foreseen, approved,
and decreed from eternity, however sudden a change may appear to human eyes.

“Nor does Sacred History show that God's decrees were abrogated when it
relates that the destruction already proclaimed to the Ninevites was remitted,
and that Hezekiah's life was prolonged after his death was announced. Those
who think so are mistaken about announcements. Although they affirm simply,
from their very purpose it will nevertheless be understood that they contain
an implicit condition. For why did the Lord send Jonah to the Ninevites to
foretell the city's ruin? Why did he announce Hezekiah's death through Isaiah?
He could have destroyed both them and him without tidings of disaster. He
therefore intended something other than that, foreknowing their own death,
they should see it coming from a distance. Clearly he did not will them
destroyed, but corrected so that they might not be destroyed. Thus his
prophecy that Nineveh would fall after forty days was made so that it should
not fall. Hezekiah's hope of a longer life was cut off so that he might obtain
a longer life. Who now does not see that by threats of this kind the Lord
willed to awaken to repentance those whom he terrified, so that they might
escape the judgment which they had deserved by their sins?

“If this is granted, the nature of things leads us to understand an implicit
condition in a simple announcement; this is also confirmed by similar
examples. When the Lord rebuked King Abimelech because he had taken Abraham's
wife, he used these words: ‘Behold, you will die because of the woman whom you
have taken, for she is joined to a husband.’ But after he had made his excuse,
the Lord spoke thus: ‘Restore the wife to the man, for he is a Prophet and will
pray for you, that you may live. If not, know that you will surely die, you and
all that you have.’ You see how by the first edict he shakes his mind more
violently, so as to make him intent upon giving satisfaction, but by the second
plainly explains his will. Since the reasoning of the other passages is
similar, do not infer from them that anything was taken away from the Lord's
former counsel because he made void what he had proclaimed. Rather, the Lord
lays down a road for his eternal ordinance when, by announcing a punishment,
he moves to repentance those whom he wills to spare, instead of changing
anything in his will—or even in his speech, except that he does not express
syllable by syllable what is nevertheless readily understood. For Isaiah's
saying must remain true: ‘The Lord of hosts has determined, and who can annul
it? His hand is outstretched, and who will turn it back?’”

Thus Calvin. Even if these words do not satisfy you, I am nevertheless
confident that they are of such a kind as will satisfy every moderate mind.
For his remarks about an implicit condition do not support you. You will have
a condition attached not, as he does, to the announcement of the decree, but
to the decree itself—as though the things the Lord has decreed were doubtful
and uncertain, or as though the Lord's will were turned now in this direction,
now in that, according to the varying outcome of a condition lodged in human
beings. That opinion is so absurd and so foreign to God's nature that I wonder
it could ever have pleased anyone.

[Printed p. 386; PDF p. 51; logical scan 410; OCR object 0419]

VI. CASTELLIO'S ARGUMENT FOR THE THIRD CALUMNY

“They also adduce the example of the prodigal son whom I have already
mentioned. If you say that he went off to live in dissipation with his father's
will, it will be utterly absurd; therefore he went with his father's
permission. So too, they say, guilty men are God's prodigal sons and sin with
God permitting, not willing.”

REFUTATION

Because in explaining parables some room exists for conjectures, curious and
fanatical minds nowhere exult more freely than in parables. I, however, think
that the purpose of parables should rather be considered, and that their
individual parts ought not to be pursued too subtly unless the interpretation
itself happens to depend upon explaining them; otherwise we shall necessarily
fall into the most absurd ideas. In this similitude I certainly acknowledge
that the Lord represents his goodness to us under the image of some most
merciful father; likewise, that in the person of the prodigal son he presents
sinners who for a time withdrew themselves, as it were, from God's sight in
order to indulge their vices, but whom afterwards the best of fathers, once
subdued by just miseries and returning to good conduct, receives back into his
favor. I do not touch upon the other part of the parable, because it does not
pertain to our purpose.

But let us see how aptly you reason from this passage. You deny that the son
went off to live dissolutely with his father's will. I acknowledge that the
son's dissipation was not approved by the father, and that when the father
sent the son away, he did not aim at the son's living dissolutely. But you are
a remarkably acute man, to conceive nothing about God except what fits human
beings, because in this similitude God proposed himself to us under the image
of a mortal father! As though God thereby signified that in every matter he is
accustomed to deal with his own in no other way than human beings do, rather
than setting before us in this passage only this one thing: his immense
goodness even toward those who have provoked him to anger by every kind of
wickedness. Once you pass beyond these limits, I say you wander from the
purpose.

But let us grant you even this. Suppose a man considers that by sending away a
stubborn son whom he has been unable to correct by any means, he will bring the
son into some great danger from which he can nevertheless rescue him whenever
he pleases; and suppose he drives him to that danger because he knows that the
son can be recalled to the way by no better means. Who would think it absurd
if someone said that the son fell into that danger because his father willed
it? Or who would not praise the father as prudent on that account rather than
denounce him as savage and cruel? For in this plan he did not set his son's
danger before himself as his purpose and end, but rather his amendment and
safety. All the elect sons must think this very thing concerning the heavenly
Father, although in this particular matter not everyone has the same
condition.

For since all things without exception work for the benefit of those who love
God, there can be no doubt that whenever God withdraws, as it were, from his
elect the grace by which they were supported, so that they fall even into the
most shameful sins, he willingly and by a definite counsel permits it. He does
this by no means as though he approved their sins, but because he wills that,
warned of their weakness also by this means, they should either arrogate
nothing to themselves, guard themselves more diligently against the adversary
than ever before, return to the way if they have already wandered, or even
provide a warning for posterity. For this reason he willed there to be some
blemishes in Abraham, Moses, and Aaron. For this reason it was only willingly
that he suffered David to fall into the greatest crimes. When David afterwards
recognized this counsel of God, he said: “Against you I have sinned and done
evil before you, so that you may be justified and prevail when you are judged.”
For the more unworthy David was to whom the Lord granted pardon, the more
brightly God's faithfulness and constancy in keeping his promises shone when
he pardoned David.

Elsewhere the same man, acknowledging how much he had profited from that
trial, says: “Before I fell into miseries, I went astray; but now I keep your
word.” And again: “It was most beneficial for me that I was cast into misery,
so that I might learn your statutes.” Without doubt, in the same way he willed
to train Peter in modesty by that threefold denial; and for this reason he
willed Paul also to be beaten by Satan's angel, lest he be lifted up too
proudly. Away, then, with your abandonment or unwilling permission, as though
the father regarded the prodigal son as forsaken even for the shortest time,
when, on the contrary, he is most solicitous for his son precisely when he
seems most angry with him.

VII. CASTELLIO'S ARGUMENT FOR THE THIRD CALUMNY

“They also adduce that saying of Christ: ‘Do you also wish to go away?’ He
certainly did not will them to go away, but he permitted it.”

[Printed p. 387; PDF p. 52; logical scan 411; OCR object 0420]

REFUTATION

On the contrary, he was so far from commanding them by this saying to go away,
or giving them power to leave—which, if he had done it, he certainly would
have done willingly—that instead by these words he kept them with him. He set
the treacherous defection of the others before them and tacitly recalled to
their memory all the benefits they had received from him. The man who cannot
explain forms of speech of this kind, drawn from common usage, must truly be
supremely ignorant.

VIII. CASTELLIO'S ARGUMENT FOR THE THIRD CALUMNY

“Finally, they adduce common sense, which says that there is a distinction
between will and permission. Christ was accustomed to teach divine things
according to this common sense; and if you take it away, all Christ's
similitudes, which we judge by common sense, will perish.”

REFUTATION

I acknowledge that to prescribe or command is one thing, and to permit this or
that to happen is another. But I say that common sense dictates to everyone
that will is joined by an indivisible bond both with prescription and with
permission, even if people, sometimes overcome by necessity or importunity,
command or permit what they would otherwise will not to happen. But since you
ask for similitudes, come, let us make these matters clear by presenting
examples.

Those who conduct their business through others must give them the power to do
whatever pertains to conducting that business, so that the agent may act by
commission. But to whom except you alone has it occurred to say that this
permission is opposed to will, when the whole permission proceeds rather from
sheer will, and indeed from choice? Yet the Lord's mode of acting is no
different in all the things performed from day to day. First, he implanted in
each thing—not only in each species but also in each individual—that universal
force and capacity which we may observe in its manifold forms. Then at every
moment he adapts that same capacity to singular and particular actions, in
accordance with his eternal and unchangeable counsel, by which he not only
foreknows and governs all things but also wills and decrees them.

He employs both good and evil instruments, as we said before. That distinction
did not begin with creation, but with corruption. Yet he employs them so that,
if there is any fault in the instruments, that contagion does not reach him.
For he rightly decrees, wills, and permits not only what voluntary instruments
rightly will and do, but also what evil instruments wickedly will and do, as we
showed fully above. Yet all these instruments do what they do only by the
permission of a willing God. For what in heaven or on earth possesses of itself
either this power or the exercise of this power, whether it uses it rightly or
not? I therefore confess that I cannot see what common sense has taught you
that permission can be separated from will, or that God can permit something
unwillingly.

I nevertheless acknowledge that there is otherwise a very great difference
between God and human beings who employ the agency of other human beings. To
say nothing of people's wicked designs, what people often do when driven by
necessity—because they cannot accomplish both their own and others'
business—God does by a most free will, since he is otherwise omnipotent in
himself and altogether self-sufficient (*autarkēs*). People, often compelled
by force, fear, or some other cause rather than merely moved, permit many
things which they would wish undone. But God alone, decreeing by a most free
will, permits what he permits.

Because Apollo in the poets swears rashly, he is in a certain way compelled by
the sanctity of his oath to grant the chariot to Phaethon, who will perish. Yet
he grants the chariot willingly, because with him the sanctity of the oath has
more weight than his son's certain danger. In Homer, Jupiter laments with tears
that he cannot rescue his son Sarpedon from present death though he wishes to
do so. When Herod, who had sworn rashly, hears that the head of John the
Baptist is requested, he sorrowfully deliberates whether he would prefer to
refuse the girl and incur the charge of perjury, or cruelly kill a completely
innocent man without trial. In the end, driven not so much, in my judgment, by
religious scruple—which did not exist—as by pride and ambition, lest he seem
either to have sworn imprudently or to be inconstant in fulfilling his oath,
he not only permits but even commands that John's severed head be given into
the girl's hands, as Mark expressly says. A sick man who fears for his life
offers himself to be cut by a surgeon—certainly not unwillingly, yet not by an
entirely free will. We must judge the same concerning a merchant who in the
greatest tempest not only allows his goods to be thrown overboard, but even

[Printed p. 388; PDF p. 53; logical scan 412; OCR object 0421]

casts them away with his own hands. Behold many examples of permissions in all
of which, nevertheless, will cannot be separated from permission. But far be
it from us to conceive anything of this kind concerning God, since neither
thoughtlessness, ignorance, nor repentance befalls him, and he is subject to
no necessity. For, as Augustine says truly and learnedly: “We do not place
God's life and God's foreknowledge under necessity if we say, ‘God must always
live and foreknow all things,’ just as his power is not diminished when he is
said to be unable to die and be deceived. For he is unable to do this in such
a way that, if he could, he would surely possess less power. He is rightly
called Omnipotent, though he cannot die or be deceived. For he is called
Omnipotent by doing what he wills, not by suffering what he does not will.”

Therefore, to settle the matter once for all, be off to Anticyra with that
common sense of yours. There, if you can, swallow a gallon of hellebore and at
last learn wisdom, and learn to dispute about common sense in such a way that
we may understand you to be endowed with some sense. For you say that Christ
was accustomed to teach divine things according to common sense; but who will
grant you that except someone wholly devoid of the Christian religion? I
acknowledge that Christ employs forms of speech drawn from ordinary usage.
But from where did you learn that divine mysteries agree entirely with our
common sense and can therefore be taught according to common sense?

Paul cries out that the *psychikos*, the natural man, does not receive the
things of God. He also testifies that *to tēs sarkos phronēma*, the mind of the
flesh, is hostility against God. He professes that he announces a doctrine
regarded as foolishness by the Gentiles. He cries out, “O man, who are you?”
and, “O the depth of God's judgments!” Christ too, praising Peter's confession,
says, “Flesh and blood did not reveal these things to you.” But you, on the
contrary, appeal to common sense as though to the supreme judge of God's
mysteries.

Explain to us, then, most acute man, why the Lord delayed the calling of the
Gentiles for so long; how the Word could be made flesh; how Christ was born of
a Virgin; how he who is in heaven feeds us on earth with his life-giving flesh;
why the Lord wills what you foolishly denied, that some are born into misery so
that he may be glorified in them, as we taught from the example of the man born
blind and from Christ's own words; why the calling of many is deferred to the
very end of life; how it happens that the greatest part of humanity hears
absolutely nothing about Christ; why, although the Lord could save everyone by
a mere nod, he nevertheless saves few; and why, from two people equal to each
other, he saves this one rather than that one. Finally—to remain with the
example proposed, though there are countless things of this kind—I ask you to
untie for us from your common sense this double knot tied by Augustine:

“Great are the works of the Lord, for in a wonderful and ineffable manner what
is done against his will is not done apart from his will; because it would not
be done if he did not allow it, and surely he allows it not unwillingly but
willingly.” Again: “If we permit those over whom we have power to commit crimes
before our eyes, we shall share their guilt. But how innumerable are the things
he permits to be done before his eyes, which, if he had not willed them, he
would by no means permit! And yet he is just and good.”

There remains what you say about similitudes, as though Christ employed them
for the sake of clarity, as we commonly do in teaching. But I say there is a
great difference between similitudes which teachers commonly use to explain a
matter and those which are called parables, which you would indeed call
allegories or riddles more correctly than similitudes.

THE FOURTH CALUMNY

Every crime committed by any human being is a good and just work of God.

CASTELLIO'S ARGUMENTS FOR THE FOURTH CALUMNY

“Against the fourth they cry out with that saying of Isaiah, ‘Woe to those who
call good evil and evil good.’ If sin is a good and just work of God, it
follows that righteousness is evil and an unjust work of God, for righteousness
is wholly contrary to sin. If sin is just, it follows that injustice is just,
for sin is injustice. If sin is God's work, it follows that God commits sin;
and if he commits sin, he is the servant of sin, according to Christ's saying.
If sin is God's work, and Christ came to abolish sin, then he came to abolish
God's work. But if Christ came to abolish the work of the devil, as Peter
testifies, what then are the works of the devil? If sin is a just work of God,
God hates and punishes his own just work; therefore he is unjust. But if it is
objected to them that sin is not sin to God, he responds: ‘Then whose fault is
sin? Or why does God hate it? Or why is it sin, unless because it is against
the law not of human beings but of God?’ If sin is God's work, God

[Printed p. 389; PDF p. 54; logical scan 413; OCR object 0422]

commits sin; and if God commits sin, he sins, just as one who does
righteousness is righteous. But if God sins, why does he forbid others to sin?
Why does he not rather command them to sin, so that human beings may be his
imitators? For offspring ought to follow their father. ‘Be holy,’ he says,
‘because I am holy.’ By the same reasoning, therefore, it will have to be said:
‘Practice sin, because I practice sin.’”

REFUTATION

What is it for the devil to act, if this is not? These are the words of Calvin
which you have distorted: “We must consider,” he says, “how God's will is the
cause of all things which are done in the world, and yet God is not the author
of evils. I shall not say with Augustine—although I embrace it as truly said by
him—that in sin or evil there is nothing positive; for it is a subtlety which
would not satisfy many. Instead I take another principle: the very things
which are done wrongly and unjustly by human beings are right and just works
of God. If at first sight this appears paradoxical to some, at least let them
not be so fastidious that they refuse to bear with me for a little while as we
inquire from God's Word what ought to be thought.”

Thus Calvin. He then explains from the Scriptures how God directs by his
counsel the things which most appear to happen by chance, what Stoic necessity
is, and to what extent contingency is not abolished. Afterwards he descends to
those actions in which human plans and endeavors intervene, and teaches that
God so acts that, through even the ungodly themselves, although ignorant and
unwilling, what had been divinely ordained is accomplished. In sum, Calvin
teaches that God acts well even through evil instruments whenever he pleases,
and yet that on this account neither is God himself connected with any fault,
nor do the evil instruments fail to sin. If a man who speaks thus speaks either
impiously, improperly, or too obscurely, let the Holy Spirit be accused of all
those faults.

For in Isaiah the Lord does not hesitate to call his own work that miserable
devastation of Jerusalem, filled with every calamity and indeed every crime,
but he does so in an entirely contrary respect. Likewise Peter does not
hesitate to say that Christ was delivered up by God's definite counsel and
foreknowledge. The Jerusalem Church agrees with Peter and speaks even more
harshly, if there is any harshness in these forms of speech; for it speaks of
Pontius Pilate and Herod as nothing other than administrators of God's
counsels. Paul also openly says that the Father did not spare his Son. David
says that Joseph was sent ahead into Egypt by the Lord. He also calls his
adversaries the Lord's hand and sword. The Lord himself calls Nebuchadnezzar
his servant. Is this, then, to make God the author of sin along with the
Libertines, or to mingle the crimes of the ungodly with God's holy works? But
these things were treated most fully in the rebuttal of the preceding calumny.

You could not have cited any passage more suitably than this one from Isaiah,
since it fits you and your faction exactly. For what else is it to call good
evil than to do what you do—that is, to overturn with the vilest slanders what
has been written in holiness and piety? And so, see how candidly and plainly I
deal with you. I agree that whatever you infer by these arguments is true of
you; but I grant you a little more than perhaps you wish, namely, that you are
fighting against yourself.

Nevertheless, I shall answer briefly the objection which you undeservedly
ascribe to us. For we do not say that sin is not sin to God. Rather, when we
distinguish between the work of God and the work of Satan or the wicked, we
commonly say this: with respect to God acting, works are good and just which,
if we look at the evil instruments insofar as they too act, are evil and unjust
works and are accordingly punished by God with just penalties. This
distinction is not ours, since the Lord in Isaiah manifestly establishes it,
as do Peter and the entire Jerusalem Church, as we taught shortly before.
Augustine likewise says: “Since the Father delivered up the Son, the Lord his
own body, and Judas the Lord, why in this act of delivery is God just and man
guilty, unless because, though the deed they did was one, the reason for which
they did it was not one?”

And if you would also like to read what the same Augustine wrote about these
things, both soundly and acutely in his customary manner, in chapter 100 of the
*Enchiridion to Laurence*, you will understand many things which you ought to
have known before criticizing ours. It is this distinction, therefore, which
you must either grant or oppose, not that Chimera which you fashioned for
yourself, since the latter has nothing to do with us, and since no one refuted
that Libertine heresy more gravely or fully than the very Calvin whose
testimony we produced above.

[Printed p. 390; PDF p. 55; logical scan 414; OCR object 0423]

THE FIFTH CALUMNY

No adultery, theft, or homicide is committed without God's will intervening.
*Institutes*, book 1, chapter 17, section 5.

RESPONSE

If you were to add that in this passage Calvin expressly treats against the
very heresy which you fasten upon us, then at last it would be clear that the
statement you slanderously criticize was spoken truly and piously. For we do
not say without qualification that God's will intervenes in things which are
evil; we add two qualifications. First, God's will intervenes even in these
matters in such a way that he by no means wills or does them insofar as they
are evil, but insofar as they are punishments of other evils, or insofar as
through them the Lord executes his judgments, sometimes hidden but always
just. Second, whenever the Lord pleases he uses even evil instruments to carry
out his just works in such a way that the instruments themselves meanwhile by
no means obey God's will, but indeed oppose it diametrically. We have already
said far too much about both points. Nevertheless, I shall repeat a few things
briefly, so as at last to satisfy you.

When the Lord was about to avenge David's adultery, he said: “Behold, I shall
raise up evil against you from your own house, and take your wives before your
eyes and hand them over to your neighbor, and he will sleep with your wives in
the sight of this sun. For you did it secretly, but I shall do this thing
before all Israel and before the sun itself.” I ask, then: does the one who
does something—the Lord so decrees these things that he is by no means the
author of incest, but justly punishes adultery and homicide—does he seem to you
not to interpose his will? “When you cease plundering,” says the Lord, “you
will be plundered.” Does it not seem to you that God willingly delivered
robbers into the hands of other robbers? If you dare deny this, the Lord will
refute you in the same Prophet Isaiah, addressing with these words the Medes
and Persians through whom he had decreed that the Babylonians, plunderers of
the Jews, should themselves be plundered: “I have commanded my consecrated
ones, and moreover have called my mighty men for my wrath.” Do you really
think these words, צוה (*commanded*) and קראתי (*I have called*), fit someone
who does not interpose his will?

What of the time when the Lord said to Satan, “Behold, whatever Job has is in
your power”? Was he decreeing nothing? And shortly afterward was Job a
blasphemer when, passing over the robbery of Satan and the Chaldeans, he said,
“The Lord gave, the Lord has taken away”? And when the Lord says that whoever
strikes with the sword will perish by the sword, does he not seem to you to be
speaking of judgments which he willingly executes by the hands of others, even
when one robber kills another? God's will, therefore—and indeed his just and
holy will—intervenes also when human beings act wickedly and accordingly pay
deserved penalties.

But I wish to cite Calvin's own words, not mutilated as you made them, but
whole, so as to set both your shamelessness and his integrity before everyone
to inspect. “The same men,” he says, “wrongly and thoughtlessly drag past
events to God's bare providence. For because whatever happens depends upon it,
‘Therefore,’ they say, ‘neither thefts, adulteries, nor homicides are committed
without God's will intervening. Why, then,’ they say, ‘will the thief be
punished who robbed the man whom the Lord willed to chastise with poverty? Why
will the murderer be punished who killed the man whose life the Lord had
ended? If all such people serve God's will, why will they be punished?’”

Thus far Calvin has spoken in the person of the Libertines. Here you display
unheard-of wickedness, for you ascribe to Calvin himself what he said in the
person of the Libertines and immediately refutes in the same place with these
words. For he adds: “But I deny that they serve God's will. For we do not say
that a man who is borne by an evil disposition renders service to God who
commands, when he obeys only his wicked desire. He obeys God who, instructed
concerning his will, strives toward the place to which he is called by it. But
from where are we instructed, except from his Word? Therefore, in the things
we do, we must look to that will of his which he declares in his Word. God
requires of us only what he commands. If we undertake anything against the
command, it is not obedience but rebellion and transgression.”

Calvin then adds other objections of the Libertines, which elsewhere I shall
set against your arguments. Are you not now ashamed of yourself, since you
object against Calvin the very things he resolves? You act just as if some
Epicurean today should wish to enlist David on his side because it is written
in him: “There is no God.”

[Printed p. 391; PDF p. 56; logical scan 415; OCR object 0424]

THE SIXTH CALUMNY

Scripture openly testifies that evil deeds are committed not only with God
willing, but with God as author.

RESPONSE

How many times have I already shown, by a thousand testimonies taken from
Calvin himself, that he opposes this very thing which you object against him? I
shall therefore not repeat what I have already said a thousand times. Yet it is
worthwhile to see what occasion you seized for stitching together this
calumny. Calvin wrote thus against Pighius and Siculus, from whose cesspools
you drew your unclean filth:

“Augustine's judgment must be maintained,” he says: “When God wills something
to be done which must be done only by willing human beings, he at the same time
inclines their hearts so that they will it; and he acts in hearts not only by
aiding, but also by judging, so that people who had proposed no such thing may
fulfill what his hand and counsel decreed.” And shortly afterwards: “From this
it is certainly easy now to infer how empty and feeble is that defense of
divine justice which says that evil things are done not by his will but merely
by his permission. Indeed, I acknowledge that insofar as the things which
human beings commit with an evil disposition are evil—as I shall presently
explain more fully—they by no means please God. But the fiction that he idly
permits things which Scripture declares are done not only with him willing but
with him as author is an exceedingly frivolous escape.”

Thus far Calvin. When you read this, of course, you imagined that you held him
entangled in your nets; but I shall shortly show how foolishly. For I wish to
gratify you and produce another similar passage from Calvin's little book
against the Libertines, chapter 14. “The Lord is fiercely indignant,” he says,
“when we suppose that anything proceeds from elsewhere and do not look to him,
so as to acknowledge him not only as the principal cause of all things but
also as author, establishing all things in this or that manner by his counsel.
Let us therefore conclude that both prosperity and adversity—rains, winds,
hailstorms, frost, fair weather, abundance, famine, war, and peace—are works of
God; but that creatures, which are lower causes, are only instruments which he
has in use to execute his will, and therefore that he uses them at his
discretion, turns them wherever he pleases, and brings them to the point that
what he determined happens altogether.”

These are Calvin's words. If you understand them as though he says that God is
the author of evil deeds or wills sin, you certainly surpass every degree of
shamelessness, since in those same passages Calvin refutes that very
blasphemy. But because some, in order to resolve the Libertines' argument, fall
into another error scarcely less reprehensible, substituting God's idle
permission for his action in most matters, he therefore judged that this error
must be refuted first of all. Thus he teaches that God carries out his work in
a different manner through evil instruments than through good, yet no less
through the former; and consequently, since he always acts as sovereign
(*autokratōr*), he intervenes not as an idle spectator, nor even merely as
governor of the outcome alone, but truly as author. As author, I say, not of
the work which the evil instruments perform wickedly—for you added this from
your own store, Castellio—but of that work which God also most justly performs
through them precisely when they act most wickedly.

Calvin therefore does not teach that evil deeds are perpetrated either with
God willing them or with God as their author. Rather, he teaches what is most
true: although the instrument sins because it acts with a corrupt will, God is
nevertheless very far from sinning when he acts well through evil, and is the
willing author of this most holy work of his. Thus through Satan and the
ungodly he daily exercises and confirms his Church. Thus, whenever he pleases,
he avenges himself upon the ungodly by the hands of the ungodly. Thus he
chastised his people through the Assyrians. Thus he willed to punish David's
adultery through Absalom while nevertheless preserving David himself. Thus he
prepared a lodging for his Church through Joseph's brothers. Thus he redeemed
the world through Herod and Pilate. Yet he deservedly avenges himself upon all
of these as people acting impiously. If you deny these things, I appeal to the
testimonies of Scripture—almost countless ones—which we cited in rebutting the
third Calumny, to pass over those things which we have already transcribed
word for word so often from Augustine and Calvin to the same effect.

CASTELLIO'S ARGUMENTS FOR THE FIFTH AND SIXTH CALUMNIES

“Against the fifth and sixth they say many other things, and chiefly these. If
God wills sin, he is the author of sin; God himself must be punished, for
punishment for sin must certainly be visited upon its author. If God wills
sin, the devil does not will sin, for the devil is contrary to God in every
respect. If God wills sin, he loves sin, and if he loves sin,

[Printed p. 392; PDF p. 57; logical scan 416; OCR object 0425]

he hates righteousness. If God wills sin, he is worse than many human beings,
for many people do not will sin; indeed, the nearer anyone approaches God's
nature, the less he wills sin. Why, then, does Paul say, ‘The good which I will
I do not do, but the evil which I do not will, this I do’? Why does Paul not
will what God wills, or why does Paul will what God does not will? Finally,
they ask what Scripture testifies that evil deeds are committed not only with
God willing, but with God as author.”

RESPONSE

Why should I contend with you at greater length, Castellio? You are raving
when you think us so foolish that it could even enter our minds that God wills
or loves sin, or that evil deeds are committed with God as author. But whatever
you have ineptly directed against us in this passage I have already shown to be
drawn from the very man you attack—much less do we deny those things or do
they make against us.

THE SEVENTH CALUMNY

They say that what human beings do while they sin is from God's will, since
God's will very often conflicts with his command.

RESPONSE

Even though Satan and unbelievers cannot do the smallest thing against either
the godly or ungodly unless God decrees and wills it, and therefore permits it,
it nevertheless does not follow that what they do as sin they do from God's
will. For although God wills what Satan and unbelievers will, he does not will
it in the same manner or respect in which Satan and unbelievers will it. God
justly wills and justly does through them what they most wickedly will and do,
because they are acted upon by God in such a way that they also play their own
parts, namely with corrupt counsel and will. There is therefore sometimes
great disagreement where there appears to be the greatest agreement, and on
the contrary great agreement where there appears to be great discord.

On this subject I should like to append a most elegant passage of Augustine,
so that your slander may appear more clearly. Thus he writes to Laurence in a
passage which Calvin suitably cited and illustrated with the most beautiful
examples: “Sometimes a man with a good will wills something which God does not
will, though God himself has a good will far more fully and certainly—for
example, if a good son wills his father to live when God with a good will wills
him to die. And conversely, it can happen that a man with an evil will wills
what God wills with a good will—for example, if an evil son wills his father to
die, and God wills it too. Clearly the former wills what God does not will,
while the latter wills what God too wills. Yet the former's piety, though he
wills something else, accords with God's good will more than the latter's
impiety does, though he wills the same thing. So greatly does it matter what it
is fitting for a human being and for God to will, and to what end each directs
his will, so that it may be either approved or disapproved.

“For God fulfills some of his wills, which are surely good, through the evil
wills of evil human beings. Thus by the malevolent Jews and by the Father's
good will Christ was killed for us. This was so great a good that when the
Apostle Peter did not will it to happen, he was called Satan by the very one
who had willed to be killed. How good appeared the wills of the godly faithful
who did not want the Apostle Paul to go to Jerusalem, lest he suffer there the
evils which the Prophet Agabus had foretold! And yet God willed him to suffer
these things for the proclamation of Christ's faith, training a martyr of
Christ. He did not fulfill this good will of his through the good wills of the
Christians, but through the evil wills of the Jews. And those who did not will
what he willed belonged to him more than did those through whom, willingly,
what he willed was done; because both did the very same thing, but God through
them with a good will, while they did it with an evil will.

“But whatever the wills of angels or human beings may be, whether good or
evil, whether willing the same thing as God or something else than God, the
will of Almighty God is always unconquered. It can never be evil, because even
when it inflicts evils it is just; and what is just is surely not evil.”

Behold the first part of your calumny explained fully, truly, and plainly. I
now come to the second, in which you invent that Calvin teaches that God's
will very often conflicts with his command. I shall speak first about the
matter itself, and then produce Calvin's words, so that your boldness in
corrupting them may be evident to anyone. We acknowledge that in the
Decalogue

[Printed p. 393; PDF p. 58; logical scan 417; OCR object 0426]

and its explanation the Lord fully and completely disclosed his will to us, so
far as concerns passing life piously and innocently. I say, however, that you
are raving if you suppose that the whole will of God is comprehended in his
Law. What then? Does gratuitous redemption through Christ not pertain to his
will? Yet there is no mention of this benefit in the Law. The declaration of
this will pertains to the other part of the divine Word, which is called the
Gospel.

But when we speak thus, do we ascribe two wills to God, and ones diametrically
opposed? For these two statements are contrary: “Unless you do all these
things, you will die,” and, “Even if you have not done them, nevertheless, if
you believe, you will live.” Far be it from us, however, to ascribe two wills
to God on that account, much less contrary ones, since God's will is one, most
simple, and always like itself. For the Lord never gave the Law with the will
to save us through it, but rather to convict us of unrighteousness through it
and lead us to Christ. Nor does the Gospel abolish righteousness from the Law;
rather, it teaches that the righteousness which we do not find in ourselves is
freely imputed to us in Christ through faith. You therefore see that there are
not immediately two wills wherever two appear, nor conflict where there seemed
to be the greatest conflict.

I shall go farther. If you think everything God wills has been disclosed to
you, and that no hidden will of God remains, tell me, please, what by the Lord's
will is going to happen tomorrow to either you or me. Prophesy how long the
Lord will suffer you to devastate God's Church. Or, if you do not wish to say
that, prophesy from those Sibyls of yours—which I hear you are accustomed to
prefer as more open and clear even to the most holy Prophets themselves—which
of the two kings, Henry or Philip, will be the victor. For if you perform this,
as you will easily if no hidden will of God remains for you, you will free many
miserable mortals from great care and anxiety and immediately acquire the
great name which you have long been wretchedly seeking in vain.

It rained here this morning, but the rest of the day was fairly clear. Let us
suppose that at the same moments, conversely, the sky was clear in Paris when
it rained here, and that it rained when it was clear here. All these things
happened by God's decree and will. Are there, therefore, simultaneously two
wills in him, and contrary ones at that? Will you at last acknowledge how
inept, foolish, and ridiculous these notions of yours are? For when we say
that one part of God's will has been disclosed to us and another is hidden,
you grave gentlemen immediately ask with loud laughter whether there are two
wills in God, and how we have come to know that hidden will.

As though, when we speak thus, it were not sufficiently clear that we say
nothing other than this: God's will, that is, his eternal counsels, has in part
been disclosed to us insofar as our salvation requires it—just as Paul
testifies that he announced the whole counsel of God to the Ephesians, to close
off every road to false doctrine. But in part it still remains hidden, insofar
as in his infinite wisdom and power he decreed and daily executes many things
which surpass all comprehension and understanding even of the angels
themselves, much more of wretched little men, and which therefore ought rather
to be religiously adored than curiously investigated. What does a man know if
he does not acknowledge this to be true?

Indeed, even in the very doctrine he disclosed to us, how many things are
there about which we must not doubt at all, yet whose rationale, if you try to
trace it, will make you no less insane than if you wished to comprehend God
himself? This splendor dazzles the sharpness of our eyes in contemplating many
matters, but especially in the argument we are treating. For since it is
certain that God hardens whom he wills and this will cannot be resisted, if
you ask why God is not therefore at fault, you will indeed adduce the true
answer, that human beings are sufficiently convicted by the testimony of
their own conscience. But since that argument cannot satisfy human judgment,
you must say with Paul: “O man, who are you to answer back to God?” And when
you have discussed these mysteries of God at length, you must cry out with
Paul, “O the depth of the riches both of the wisdom and of the knowledge of
God!” and with David, “Your judgments, Lord, are a great abyss,” and silently
adore with Isaiah his infinite omnipotence.

All your sophistries, or rather futile trifles, therefore collapse, as though
we make God contrary to himself. Although the human mind cannot comprehend how
not even what is against God's will can happen apart from his will, as
Augustine says, it does not follow that this is not true, since there exist
very many and most manifest testimonies of the Word which confirm that it is
so, as we showed above. For that idle permission in the action of evil
instruments, that mere governance of the outcome, and that mere conversion of
good into evil, besides being refuted by the clearest passages of sacred
Scripture, utterly

[Printed p. 394; PDF p. 59; logical scan 418; OCR object 0427]

conflict also with God's nature, for then he would have no action in avenging
crimes. Augustine, however, provides an argument which has always satisfied
every moderate mind, as we showed above in rebutting the third Calumny. For
whenever the Lord acts through evil and therefore sinful instruments, the
actions themselves, although sins by their own nature, are nevertheless not
sins with respect to God acting, but punishments of sins, by which the Lord
either avenges former sins, exercises even his own people in patience, or does
both at once.

Thus not even when God seems to will sins which he nevertheless prohibited in
the Law can he appear unlike himself. Indeed, he shows himself to be precisely
as the Law sets him before us, namely a severe vindicator and avenger of
crimes. In my judgment no one explained this more plainly than Calvin, and in
the very passage which you thought should be censured. “There is no reason,”
he says, “for anyone to drag God into partnership in crime whenever some
similarity appears between his secret counsel and the openly corrupt desires
of human beings.”

And shortly afterwards: “Certainly,” he says, “when Paul cries, ‘O the depth!’
he openly teaches that God's judgment concerning the Jews was something other
than what is expressed by Christ's words: ‘Jerusalem, how often I willed to
gather your children!’ And because he did not will Eli's sons to be obedient
to their father, that will differed from the command of the Law by which he
commands a son to obey his parents.”

This is the passage from which you patched together your calumny. For in place
of “differed from the command of the Law,” you substituted “conflicted with
the command of the Law.” But things which differ from one another do not
necessarily conflict. Thus in this case disobedience does not conflict with
obedience insofar as God wills the former. For God does not will Eli's sons to
be rebels against their father as though viewing and approving that rebellion
in itself, but rather as by this means opening the way for his just judgments.
Do these things conflict with one another, or do they not rather agree
perfectly: to command that parents be obeyed, and to avenge the rebels against
this command by another rebellion, so that at last they may pay deserved
penalties?

Further, lest I seem to flee to subtle arguments, let us hear what
interpretation Calvin adds to this statement, so that you cannot complain of
obscurity or ambiguity. For what is more equitable than that he interpret his
own mind? “Many are mistaken in this,” he says, “because they do not consider
that God justly wills the things human beings do in sinning. ‘How so?’ you will
ask. He abominates adulteries and incestuous couplings. When Absalom violates
his father's concubines before the people, does this happen with God willing?
Yet God himself had foretold through his servant Nathan that he would do this:
‘I shall take your wives before your eyes,’ he says, ‘and give them to your
neighbor, who will sleep with them in the sight of this sun. You did it
secretly; but I shall do it before all the people and before the sun.’

“Scripture is filled with such examples. Shall we therefore either impute the
guilt of sin to God, or fashion two wills in him so that he is at odds with
himself? By no means. Rather, just as I have already shown that he wills the
same thing as criminals and the wicked, but in a different manner, so now we
must hold that he wills in the same manner things which are outwardly
different, so that in that variety which strikes our minds there is a
beautifully tempered harmony. Insofar as Absalom's crime is a monstrous
impiety against his father, a treacherous violation of marriage, and also a
shameful profanation of the order of nature, it certainly displeases God, who
is pleased by honor, chastity, faithfulness, and modesty, and who wills the
lawful order he prescribed to be honored and preserved inviolate among human
beings. Yet because it pleases him to avenge David's adultery in this manner,
he wills in the same manner the things which seem different to us. For the
will by which he commands what ought to be done and by which he avenges
transgressions of his Law is one and simple.

“It was said elsewhere that sins are punishments which God repays for prior
sins. In such lessons we must consider two things: God's just judgment, by
which he testifies that he hates the sin upon which he inflicts punishment;
and human malice, which is openly opposed to God's will. But if so immense a
splendor dazzles the sharpness of our mind, what wonder is it? Bodily eyes are
not sufficient to endure looking at the sun; is our intelligence possessed of
greater sight, or God's majesty inferior to the sun's brilliance? It is
therefore not fitting for us to be too acute, provided we do not meanwhile
either deny to be true what Scripture clearly teaches and experience
confirms, or dare censure it as less consistent with God.”

Thus far Calvin. At last, then, be ashamed to affirm that Calvin says God's
will very often conflicts with his command—that is, that God is at odds with
himself—which no one but the most abandoned person would say.

I. CASTELLIO'S ARGUMENT FOR THE SEVENTH CALUMNY

“Under the seventh they ask: If God's will often conflicts with his command,
how can it be known when he wills or does not will what he commands?”

[Printed p. 395; PDF p. 60; logical scan 419; OCR object 0428]

REFUTATION

It is your invention that God's will conflicts with his command. We say that
what the Lord commands pleases him, and that whatever he forbids displeases
him. But if sometimes he seems to will what he prohibited in the Law, there is
nevertheless no contradiction in him, because the Lord can will in the same
manner and for the same end things which conflict by nature, as we showed a
little earlier from manifest testimonies of Scripture.

II. CASTELLIO'S ARGUMENT FOR THE SEVENTH CALUMNY

“If Calvin says that what God commands must always be done whether he wills it
or does not will it, it follows that he wills sin to be committed so that his
will may sometimes be resisted. For if he commands me not to commit adultery,
and yet wills me to commit adultery, while I nevertheless ought not to commit
adultery, then I ought to act contrary to his will.”

REFUTATION

We say and affirm that God's will must be sought nowhere but in God's Word.
But your addition “whether he wills it or does not will it” is from your own
store, as slanderers are accustomed to add. Sometimes *to will* is said only
of the things which we approve in themselves and simply—if we are good,
because they are good; but if evil, because they are evil, for good people
delight in good things and evil people in evil things. At other times we are
also said to will something which we condemn in itself but nevertheless seek
for the sake of a definite end. Yet on this account we neither disagree with
ourselves nor will our own will to be resisted, because things which are by
their own nature contrary nevertheless agree perfectly with each other
insofar as we will them.

Good princes pursue peace and will the lives not only of all citizens
collectively but even of every citizen individually to be preserved. Yet those
same princes command criminals to be killed and decree wars carried on with
citizens' blood. What, indeed, is more mutually opposed than to preserve alive
and to kill, peace and war? Yet with one simple will, and indeed looking to the
same end, good princes sometimes will both.

The Lord condemns robbery; nevertheless, he also wills to test and exercise
Job by robbery. There is no contradiction here in God's will. For to forbid
robbery and to confirm the patience of his servant, in whatever manner it may
be done, certainly differ but are not contrary. The opposition is only between
Satan's will and God's, because Satan wickedly wills what God well wills. The
Lord condemns adulterers and deservedly subjects the incestuous to severe
penalties; yet it pleases him to avenge David's adultery by Absalom's incest.
Here too there is no contradiction, but the greatest agreement. For the Lord
does not will Absalom's incest as incest, but as the punishment of adultery.
Thus where the Lord might seem to violate his own Law, he is so far from
violating it that he even avenges its transgression.

But conversely, Absalom's evil will conflicts with God's good will, although
both appear to will the same thing in outward form. For God wills to punish
David's adultery and also Absalom's own arrogance and treachery. Absalom
himself knowingly and deliberately does what he knows is forbidden by divine
and human laws. He therefore sins against God, nature, his father, and himself.
Do you see your sophistries collapse of themselves? Even if these things do
not satisfy you, Augustine's statement nevertheless stands firm: “In a
wonderful and ineffable manner, what is done against God's will is not done
apart from his will.” “I do not understand,” you will say. Yet the sun shines,
even if moles see nothing.

III. CASTELLIO'S ARGUMENT FOR THE SEVENTH CALUMNY

“Now when he commands the Israelites universally, ‘Do not commit adultery,’
does he will that none commit adultery, or that one part commit adultery and
another not? Here they ask for some definite response, Calvin. If you say he
wills that one part commit adultery and another not, then God will be contrary
to himself in the same command.”

REFUTATION

Where is that common sense of yours? If the command not to commit adultery
pertained only to one part of the Israelites and the other part were commanded
to commit adultery, why would God be contrary to himself if he commanded
different or even contrary things to different people? He wills the Levites to
carry the Ark; he forbids the others to touch it, or even to steady it when it
is about to fall. Is God therefore contrary to himself because he approves in
some what he condemns in others? But hear a plain and clear answer. God wills
no one to commit adultery. “Therefore,” you will say, “it is false that
adulteries are committed with God willing, or that God's will intervenes in
adulteries.” I grant this insofar as adultery is adultery; I deny it insofar as
it is the punishment of adultery or of some other crime, as appears in

[Printed p. 396; PDF p. 61; logical scan 420; OCR object 0429]

Absalom. For this *antapodosis*, this repayment, is just before God, as Paul
says.

IV. CASTELLIO'S ARGUMENT FOR THE SEVENTH CALUMNY

“They also say that God is a hypocrite if he commands one thing and wills
another, and that he has honey in his mouth but gall in his heart.”

REFUTATION

But you, of course, are no hypocrite, since you speak so openly and in such
impure language. What God commands he always commands sincerely; what he
forbids he sincerely forbids. “But often,” you will say, “he requires from
someone what he nevertheless does not will to be done by him, if we are to
believe you.” Give an example. He requires Pharaoh to let the Israelites go,
and yet does not will Pharaoh to let them go, since he willingly hardens him.
He sends Isaiah to require amendment of life from the people, and yet blinds
the people so that, seeing, they do not see. Every day he calls many whom he
nevertheless does not draw to himself. “This,” you say, “is your doctrine.” I
grant it. For so we learned to speak from God's Word, but we deny that God is
therefore a dissembler. For to command and not to will are not contraries;
these, rather, are contraries: to command and to forbid, to will and not to
will.

God would therefore be a dissembler if he commanded Pharaoh to let the
Israelites go and prevented a Pharaoh willing to obey from being able to obey;
or if he said to unbelievers, “I will you to repent,” and meanwhile, by not
drawing them, commanded them to be unconcerned about repentance. Although it
is true that God wills—that is, approves and regards as pleasing—whatever he
commands, and sincerely discloses his nature both in commanding and
forbidding, it nevertheless does not follow that he always grants those whom
he commands or forbids something this grace, that they can do what he commands
or abstain from what he forbids.

Thus the Lord says to Pharaoh through Moses, “Let my people go,” but does not
bend his mind; rather, he hardens it. Through Isaiah he says to the Israelites,
“Repent”; he himself says to the Pharisees, “Repent”; and through Paul,
“Believe the Gospel”; and yet he does not draw most of them. I deny,
nevertheless, that there is any appearance of dissimulation here. For the Lord
seriously requires what he requires and seriously forbids what he forbids.

But if there is anything here at which human reason protests, it is rather
this: the Lord seems unjust either to command what he knows cannot be
performed, or to forbid what he fully understands cannot fail to happen. But
far be it from any injustice to befall God. Indeed, he is most wise: by such
fearful judgments he illumines his glory; discloses to us the blindness and
malice of the human mind and how miserable the human condition is apart from
his singular grace; avenges himself upon the ungodly; and teaches his own to
work out their salvation with fear and trembling and to revere God. He cannot
be unjust, even if he requires what cannot be performed and forbids what
cannot be avoided. For he is indebted to no one and demands nothing except
what is owed to him by the fullest right. But if a human being is insolvent,
let him accuse himself, for he prodigally squandered the abundant capacities
he had received from God.

V. CASTELLIO'S ARGUMENT FOR THE SEVENTH CALUMNY

“If it is objected to them that God has two wills contrary to each other—one
open, namely in his commands, and another hidden—they ask who disclosed that
hidden will to Calvin. For if Calvin knows it, by that very fact it is not
hidden; but if he does not know it, why does he affirm something about a matter
unknown?”

REFUTATION

Not even the Manichaeans said that God has two wills, much less contrary ones;
for they preferred to establish two principles rather than disgorge that
blasphemy. No one but a complete madman denies that God willingly does every
day countless things which no one could even have suspected. And among the
things he has disclosed to us, how many have a rationale so hidden in God that
it surpasses even the angels' comprehension? This is the hidden and
incomprehensible will of God which we affirm, convicted partly by experience
and instructed partly by the words of all the Prophets and Apostles, who cry
on nearly every page of their books that God's judgments are incomprehensible
and his ways unsearchable. When we reach this will, we silently adore it and
acknowledge that we can in no way perceive its *to dioti*, its reason why. It
is therefore by no means unknown to us that there is some hidden and
unsearchable will of God; nor does the one who speaks thus search hidden
things. But how could no will of God be hidden from you, who do not even know
yourself?

[Printed p. 397; PDF p. 62; logical scan 421; OCR object 0430]

VI. CASTELLIO'S ARGUMENT FOR THE SEVENTH CALUMNY

“They also say that two contraries cannot exist at once and at the same time
in the same subject. But to will and not will the same thing at the same time
are contraries.”

REFUTATION

We have more than sufficiently refuted this calumny a little earlier.

VII. CASTELLIO'S ARGUMENT FOR THE SEVENTH CALUMNY

“If God has two wills contrary to each other, it is credible that Calvin also,
as God's imitator, has two wills, saying one thing while thinking and willing
another. We therefore refuse to believe him, since he is a two-tongued,
two-hearted, two-willed man.”

REFUTATION

I recognize, Sebastian Castellio, your boldness in coining words; I recognize
your futile buffoonery. But why should I return those insults of yours, which
you could persuade no one to believe except someone like yourself? We have
more than sufficiently answered the matter itself.

VIII. CASTELLIO'S ARGUMENT FOR THE SEVENTH CALUMNY

“Likewise, if God, commanding righteousness, wills injustice, it follows that
the devil, commanding injustice, wills righteousness. And if God does not sin
when he wills one thing and says another, it follows that if a human being
imitates him in this, he does not sin, for it is certainly not wrong to imitate
God. Thus it will be permissible to command human beings: ‘Lie, and say one
thing while harboring another in your breast, so that you may be like your
Father, who says one thing and wills another.’”

REFUTATION

It cannot be said without blasphemy that God wills injustice, and we have
never said it. Indeed, in our age there has been found almost one man alone who
convicted those who say such things, and it is through his side that you stab
the truth. The inference you add is not necessary, even if we were to grant
the antecedent—which God forbid. Moreover, what God says and what he thinks
never conflict, although he does not always explain everything he decrees,
because it is not always expedient. The one who imitates this wisdom of God,
so far as we can attain it, is by no means a hypocrite or double-hearted; much
less may dissemblers cloak themselves with God's example. I have explained all
these things at greater length.

IX. CASTELLIO'S ARGUMENT FOR THE SEVENTH CALUMNY

“They ask with which will the Lord speaks when he commands his people to pray,
‘Your will be done’; and, ‘He who does the will of my Father who is in heaven
is my brother.’ Likewise Paul says, ‘You are called a Jew and rest in the Law,
boast in God, know his will, approve excellent things, and have learned the
Law.’ Here God's will is certainly called what is commanded in the Law. If
this will is good, as it surely is, any will contrary to it must be evil. For
whatever is contrary to good is evil.”

REFUTATION

I answer that all these things are said about the will which most condemns
you. For one who slanders an innocent man against his conscience violates the
whole Law of God and is a devil. As for the matter itself, I am ashamed to
repeat what I have urged so often: God is always like himself and wills in the
same manner things which conflict by their own nature. We have cited more
testimonies from Scripture than you can bear in their multitude, and ones too
plain for you to obscure.

X. CASTELLIO'S ARGUMENT FOR THE SEVENTH CALUMNY

“Likewise that saying of Christ, ‘How often I willed to gather your children,
and you refused.’ Christ certainly speaks of his open will, since he had
opened it in so many ways. But if he had another will contrary to it, his whole
life was mere hypocrisy—something horrible even to contemplate.”

REFUTATION

This saying must indeed be understood of Christ's open will. I grant you even
more than you ask. For I say that in this passage Christ speaks not only of
his own ministry, but also of the ministry of all the Prophets who announced
the Lord's Word in Jerusalem, since all the Prophets were instruments of
Christ, that one Prophet

[Printed p. 398; PDF p. 63; logical scan 422; OCR object 0431]

whom the Lord promises to the Israelites in Moses. But it is worthwhile
to understand to what extent the Lord discloses his will through ministers.
For here we consider Christ as discharging the office of a minister, both
through the mouth of the Prophets and also through himself, when he was a
minister of the Circumcision.

Paul testifies that he announced the whole counsel of God to the Ephesians.
Paul therefore knew the whole counsel of God. For he did not speak like a
madman, or utter like those Sibyls of yours things which he did not understand;
rather, he spoke things which he had learned from God himself and embraced by
faith. Yet when the same Paul wished to announce God's Word in Asia and
Bithynia, he was forbidden by the Holy Spirit. Paul therefore did not know
some counsel of God which, had he known it beforehand, would have prevented
him from attempting to go into Bithynia. Add that, when caught up to the third
heaven, he declares that he saw mysteries which it was not lawful to utter.
How do these two things agree—he concealed nothing, yet learned by revelation
things he was not permitted to teach—unless many things lie hidden with God
whose knowledge is not yet useful or timely for edification in godliness?

Is there really any contradiction here between Paul and the Spirit of God? On
the contrary, beneath the appearance of discord lies the greatest agreement.
For what Paul well wills, the Lord with a better will does not will, although
the Lord's reason is hidden while Paul's will is manifest. Since each of these
wills is good—for God provides for his glory as he knows is expedient, and Paul
pursues God's glory as the apostolic office required—these two wills can
certainly differ but cannot conflict. For good, insofar as it is good, can
differ from some other good, but it cannot oppose the good.

Thus he omitted nothing conducive to the use and salvation of the godly, but
disclosed the whole counsel of God. Yet he did not bring forth those things
which Moses testifies remain hidden with God. What, then? From where comes that
appearance of disagreement? From this: although the Lord discloses the
doctrine of salvation to his ministers, through whom he instructs his Church,
he nevertheless does not disclose to them what he has decreed concerning each
person. He therefore sends them to preach the Gospel of salvation to an entire
nation, while reserving in his mysteries to which people and at what moment he
wills the preaching of the Gospel to be effectual, and conversely whom he has
determined to blind and harden by that very preaching.

What, then, is the ministers' duty? To invite everyone indiscriminately to
salvation, to benefit everyone, and to draw everyone by word and benefits. For
they leave God's secret judgments to God until he discloses them. Hence comes
that appearance of disagreement between God and ministers, since they often
wish to gather those whom they do not know do not belong to the Lord's flock.
Hence those daily laments and remonstrances of ministers when they see people
resisting who ought least to have resisted. Christ's complaint which you
adduce belongs to this class. But we showed above that beneath this appearance
of disagreement there is no contradiction.

But if you prefer to ascribe this saying to Christ as God, not as God's
minister—in which capacity it is certain that some ignorance, though free from
all sin, befell him—do you not know that the Lord, in order to draw his
children by his kindness, sometimes lisps with us by assuming human
affections? He complains that he was disappointed by a vineyard in which he
found wild grapes instead of sweet clusters. Will you infer from this that God
can be deceived? Elsewhere, deliberating about sending his son to the farmers
after the servants were killed, he says: “Perhaps they will reverence my son.”
Will you infer from this that, as commonly happens with human beings, he was of
two minds, and that the son's murder by the farmers happened contrary to his
