expectation, when elsewhere he is said to have delivered his Son to the Jews
to be crucified and not to have spared his Son? Thus when from this passage
too you infer either that God disagrees with himself, that the Jerusalemites
could rescind God's counsel and will, as you dared to say elsewhere, or that
they perished against God's will, what do you reveal except your supreme
ignorance?

XI. CASTELLIO'S ARGUMENT FOR THE SEVENTH CALUMNY

“Finally, he says that if God commands something other than what he wills,
there are not two wills but a lie; for the one who says he wills what he does
not will lies, and merely to command in words is not to will but to lie.”

REFUTATION

Since here too you posit a hypothesis which has never entered our minds, I
answer nothing except that you persevere in your insanity.

THE EIGHTH CALUMNY

Pharaoh's hardening, and therefore his stubborn disposition and rebellion,
was God's work—or so

[Printed p. 399; PDF p. 64; logical scan 423; OCR object 0432]

Moses testifies, as he ascribes Pharaoh's entire rebellion to God.

RESPONSE

Elsewhere you define *to harden* as meaning nothing other than *not to
soften*. I say that definition is false and ridiculous. For since we all bear
stony hearts, as you yourself are compelled to admit although you disagree
with yourself shortly afterwards, *to harden* does not mean *not to soften*,
but to make something already hard still harder. Since this hardness is native
in us, and propagated into us not from creation but from corruption, and God
therefore deservedly exercises and even doubles it by his just judgment, why
do you shudder to acknowledge that hardening is the just work of God as
vindicator and avenger?

You certainly cannot deny that the Holy Spirit speaks in no other way. For in
how many passages is God said to harden, blind, stop the ears, fatten the heart,
hold the heart, turn the heart, hand people over to a reprobate mind, and send
an efficacy of error? And how many similar expressions occur? But in these
matters there is not even the faintest mention of permission in the sacred
books, so far as I can recall, except in one passage alone, where the Lord is
said to have allowed the nations in past ages to walk in their own ways. But
neither this passage nor others like it, if perhaps any occur to you, support
you at all, since will is necessarily included in permission, much less do
they conflict with each other here, as we demonstrated in the proper place.

But it is worthwhile to produce Calvin's own words. These, then, are Calvin's
very words: “Moses says that Pharaoh's heart was hardened by God. It is vain to
flee here to permission, as though God were said to have done what he only
suffered to happen. For Moses clearly affirms that that hardness was God's
work.” Thus Calvin. But you write that according to Calvin's opinion Pharaoh's
whole rebellion is ascribed to God—as though this lie of yours could not be
convicted from the very calumny you immediately appended.

THE NINTH CALUMNY

God's will is the supreme cause of the hardening of human beings.

RESPONSE

“He hardens whom he wills,” says the Apostle. But if you seek something higher
than God's will, by all means wander outside God; with the Apostle, we gladly
rest at this boundary. Moreover, can the man who says that God's will is the
highest or supreme cause of the hardening of human beings ascribe Pharaoh's
whole rebellion to God? For if there is only one cause, why is it called the
supreme cause? A liar must have a good memory. You should therefore have said
not the highest but the only cause, so that you did not disagree with yourself.
But lest I seem to seek evasions, let it indeed be the highest cause; it is
nevertheless just. All the fault lies in the secondary causes, which are moved
justly by the supreme cause as he thus punishes Pharaoh and Egypt, but move
themselves unjustly because they are evil and unjust.

CASTELLIO'S ARGUMENTS FOR THE EIGHTH AND NINTH CALUMNIES

“In the eighth and ninth articles they ask what Moses means when he writes:
‘And Pharaoh hardened his heart.’ Must it be interpreted thus: ‘Pharaoh
hardened his heart,’ that is, ‘God hardened Pharaoh's heart’? That will do more
violence to the law of language than if you say: ‘God hardened Pharaoh's
heart,’ that is, ‘God allowed Pharaoh to remain in his heart's natural
hardness because Pharaoh had refused to obey him.’ They then ask about this
saying: ‘Today, if you hear his voice, do not harden your hearts.’ If you
interpret it, ‘Let God not harden your hearts,’ it will be utterly absurd to
command in human beings what belongs to God. For if hardening hearts is God's
act, people can no more be commanded either to harden or not to harden their
hearts than to add or subtract one cubit from their stature.”

REFUTATION

This is the foundation and basis of that excellent disputation of yours on
Predestination, Castellio, which you inserted into your annotations on the
ninth chapter of the Epistle to the Romans. Let us see how firm it is. Who, I
ask—who has ever yet been so senseless that, once a supreme cause was
established, he thought lower causes were abolished rather than established?
“Make for yourselves a new heart and a new spirit,” says the Lord. Will you
infer from this that God is not

[Printed p. 400; PDF p. 65; logical scan 424; OCR object 0433]

the one who gives a new heart and creates a clean heart, who removes the stony
heart and grants a heart of flesh? “If you do these things,” says Paul writing
to Timothy, “you will save yourself.” Will you infer from this that we are not
saved by God through Christ? Heads of households are said to feed their
families; one who lives by his own labor is said to support himself; bread and
food are also said to nourish us. Does God therefore not feed us? On the
contrary, all these things agree perfectly as joint causes (*synaitia*). For
converted by God, we turn; acted upon by God, we act; and as God blesses us, we
live by our own industry. Do you see that all the things you devised to attack
the truth are inept and ridiculous?

Hardening, therefore, is God's just work, for he hardens whom he pleases, yet
none but those deservedly hated, since they are corrupt. Hardening is also
Satan's work, through whom the Lord, of course, acts in the hearts of
unbelievers, as it is written: “The Spirit of the Lord had departed from Saul,
and an evil spirit from the Lord troubled him.” It is also the work of false
prophets, whom the Lord themselves deceives through Satan, so that they drive
one another to just destruction, as it is written: “If a prophet has been
deceived and has spoken a word, I the Lord have deceived that prophet”; as
also appeared in the Egyptian magicians and the false prophet Zedekiah.
Finally, hardening is the work of the very people who are hardened, because,
driven by native malice, they harden themselves from Satan, the false
prophets, and their own lust. Thus the things which you absurdly thought
conflicted with each other cohere by a fixed and indivisible bond.

I shall append Calvin's own words so that this stupidity of yours may appear
more clearly. “It is no obstacle,” he says, “that elsewhere Pharaoh himself is
said to have burdened his own heart, since we neither imagine human minds to
be driven by an external motion so that they are carried violently, nor
transfer the cause of hardening to God as though people were not spontaneously
hard and unyielding through their own malice and did not sharpen themselves
toward stubbornness. Rather, Scripture teaches that the perverse things human
beings do happen only because God justly orders them, just as elsewhere it is
said that the opposition of everyone except the inhabitants of Gibeon to
Israel was from the Lord, who hardened their heart.” Thus Calvin, in the very
passage which you dared to slander as though he ascribed Pharaoh's whole
rebellion to God.

CALUMNY X

Satan is a liar by God's command.

RESPONSE

These are Calvin's words, which you cited mutilated in your usual fashion in
order to persuade people that Calvin thinks God is the author of falsehood and
even commands a lie. Calvin, on the contrary, says: “God himself now uses the
ungodly like scourges to punish human sins; now, as though dragging them by a
twisted neck, he compels them to be ministers of his beneficence.” Thus
Calvin. After proving both parts by clear testimonies, he finally adds: “The
same thing can be perceived in Satan, the prince of all crimes and head of the
ungodly. God sends him to deceive Ahab with this command: that he be a lying
spirit in the mouth of all the Prophets. Thus the deceiving spirit is a
minister of God's wrath to blind the ungodly who had refused to obey the
truth.”

What you say is therefore true, but you should have added that the lie is not
commanded by God insofar as it is a lie—for to that extent God neither
commands nor approves a lie, but forbids and avenges it—but insofar as it is
the just punishment of those who preferred a lie to the truth, as Paul
explains.

I. CASTELLIO'S ARGUMENT FOR THE TENTH CALUMNY

“Against the tenth article they argue thus: If Satan is a liar by God's
command, lying has been commanded and Satan is just. For if commanding a lie
is just—as it certainly is if Calvin speaks truth—then obeying in the matter
of a lie is also just, for obedience is reckoned righteousness from
commandments of righteousness. And just as obeying an unjust command is
unjust, so obeying a just command is just.”

REFUTATION

Neither a lie nor any sin is commanded by God insofar as it is sin, but
insofar as it is the punishment of sin. It is just before God to avenge sins
by sins, as we demonstrated fully in the proper place. Further, for obedience
to be called just, it is not enough that the command be just; it is also
required that what is justly commanded be done justly. Thus not even when
Satan does what God commands does he obey; rather, he diametrically opposes
God. For in that outward agreement there is the greatest disagreement, since
God justly wills and commands

[Printed p. 401; PDF p. 66; logical scan 425; OCR object 0434]

the punishment of sin, while Satan executes it in such a way that, so
far as it lies in him, he does not avenge sin but commits it.

II. CASTELLIO'S ARGUMENT FOR THE TENTH CALUMNY

“If Calvin says that Satan lies disobediently, that is, not in order to obey
God, we shall answer according to Calvin's opinion that this too happens by
God's command—that he lies disobediently—and that Satan is also obedient in
the very fact that he lies disobediently, because the Lord commanded him to
lie disobediently.”

REFUTATION

Though you twist yourself in every direction like an eel, you are nevertheless
held fast. For even if I grant you that by God's command Satan lies
disobediently, it does not follow with respect to this command that there is
any injustice in God or any obedience in Satan. For God justly inflicted even
this disobedience upon Satan—not insofar as it is disobedience, but insofar as
the Lord, depriving him of the gift of obedience, justly wills the unclean
spirit to become worse than himself day by day until, once the measure is full,
he sentences him to eternal punishments. Does the Lord seem to you either idle
or unwilling, rather than justly willing and decreeing as he performs the
part of a judge even against Satan himself? But even when the Lord, justly
avenging Satan's sins by sins, commands him to lie disobediently, Satan does
not obey even in his disobedience, for he murmurs against God and unwillingly
calls judgment down upon himself.

CALUMNY XI

God gives the will to those who do evil and even suggests corrupt and
dishonorable affections, not only permissively but effectively, and does so
for his own glory.

RESPONSE

Far be it from any of us ever to have said or written, as you write, that an
evil will or any corrupt and shameful affections are given, permitted, or
effected by God, since our very thought utterly abhors blasphemies of this
kind. We do not hesitate to affirm this: God rouses and impels even those who
are evil and act wickedly, but in such a way that all the depravity of the
motion and action inheres in them, and not the least contagion reaches God.

For we say what is most true: God is the first mover of all things in such a
way that he not only implanted in all things that universal influence which he
still supplies, but also, just as he decreed each thing from eternity, bends
and regulates secondary causes as he pleases, so as to move, direct, and bring
each to its appointed end; and no thing, even the least, is exempted from his
effective and governing providence. “For not even a sparrow,” says Christ,
“falls to the earth without the Father's will.” Nor has any sane person ever
supposed either that God resembles an architect who, once he has built a ship,
leaves its governance to others; that, after implanting a certain universal
force of nature in all things, he withdrew to heaven so that the individual
parts of the world are driven by their own character or decision; or that he
governs the world only by a general influence. We have learned from God's Word
to execrate all these opinions, and do not think they need fuller refutation
here.

But we also say this: God is the first mover of all things in such a way that
individual things are also moved from their own principles. If those
principles are pure, pure motions follow; but if impure, impure motions. Since,
therefore, in things endowed not only with appetite but also with intelligence
and will, motions proceed from those principles, it is not surprising if we
judge the motions and actions themselves differently according to the
different principles. Let this be an example of the distinction.

The Lord wills to punish one robber by the hand of another. For he has often
determined to set the wicked against the wicked, as is clear from the example
of the Midianites. He therefore rouses the robber's evil will to kill another;
and not content with this, he also directs the robber's hand and weapon so that
the person destined for just slaughter is killed. Thus, insofar as this
killing arises from this principle—that is, from God, who justly impels the
robber's will, though evil, and directs the robber's hand and weapon—neither the
impulse nor the work is evil; rather, it is committed justly and holily by God,
who afflicted the robber with deserved penalties. For God neither compelled
the robber nor implanted malice in him, but well and justly roused the robber's
will, which was already evil, to a good and holy work.

But insofar as that killing arises from another principle, namely the
robber's will—which is impelled not as a stone or log, but as a will which is
acted upon so as to act, and moved so as to move itself by its own inward
motion—to that extent, I say, both the motion

[Printed p. 402; PDF p. 67; logical scan 426; OCR object 0435]

arising from an evil principle is evil and it ends in an evil action, namely
homicide. This is so far from pleasing God that, on the contrary, he cannot
fail to avenge it. Come, let us also posit an example from which this diversity
of principles can be understood. Suppose a lame donkey is driven to the mill
by a driver. The action, insofar as it comes from the driver, cannot be
censured, provided it is moderate; but the limping comes from the donkey.

Do you wish me to speak more plainly, so that you cannot complain of
obscurity? You quibble voluntarily about nature because either you do not
understand or pretend that you do not understand. Yet this *kakoētheia*, this
malignity, is not so much nature as a corruption of nature. Thus its efficient
cause must not be sought, but rather its deficient cause, as Augustine says;
nor is there reason for us to accuse God, the author of the things themselves
but not of their faults. But because man is corrupt, has the Lord therefore,
so far as man is concerned, abdicated his dominion by which he impels and
directs every single thing without exception toward its appointed end? By no
means. He therefore uses even sinful instruments and brings them to the end he
appointed. And he does so in such a way that he sins neither in moving nor in
directing the instrument. For the action is his; the defect belongs to the
instrument, which itself acts upon itself by an inward motion, and, since it is
corrupt, has corrupt motions.

God, then, has chosen you—through the fault of your nature an envious man and
slanderer—above all others in these regions, partly to exercise his own and
partly to punish those who, after disdaining the offered truth of the Gospel,
pursue lies and slanders. In this corrupt nature of yours, whenever it has
seemed good to him, he gradually rouses those motions, using the ministry of
Satan and of others and also your own wickedness. Those motions then gradually
break out into the blasphemies and insults whose refutation now occupies us.
But when we refuted these in former years, and now refute them again, what else
do we do than daily provoke you more and more to continue slandering and
inventing blasphemies?

Yet far be it from us, when we say that you are moved and impelled by God, to
ascribe to God any blame for your deeds. Far be it also for blame deservedly to
be transferred to us because, provoked by our writings, you grow worse every
day. That your abuse exercises our diligence; that by this means the hypocrisy
of many is daily exposed; that your slanders rouse in us zeal to know and
defend the truth; and that in the efficacy of your lie and error God's just
judgment upon those who preferred a lie to the truth or abused the knowledge
of the Gospel is set before us to contemplate as in the clearest mirror—all
this we confess that we owe to God, who moves and directs your wickedness to
these uses.

Moreover, your frauds are now perceived by many whose simplicity you would
otherwise have deceived; your *philotimia*, your ambition, is now hateful to
many; the savage designs you adopted to suppress God's truth, while claiming
that heretics should be tolerated out of regard for humanity, are now open to
everyone; and all now recognize at least in part how bold you are in
perverting God's Word. Godly people acknowledge that they owe this too to God,
and gratefully profess that our diligence played some part in it. We are so
far from bearing any blame because, offended by our writings, you rush toward
the worse—a fault of yours, not any fault of ours, who would rather wish you
reformed.

That many weak people stumble through you; that the course of the Gospel is
slowed by you; that God's truth is polluted by your lies; that God's name is
blasphemed through you; that the reputation of God's faithful servants is
undeservedly injured; and finally that you bring destruction upon yourself and
others whom you draw to you—all good people ascribe these things at last both
to Satan, by whose furies you are driven, and to your own wickedness and
boldness.

Behold, then, what I think is a sufficiently manifest and plain example from
which you may understand that God so suggests will and affections even to the
wicked themselves that all those motions, insofar as they are suggested by
him, are nevertheless both just and honorable, since they pertain to a good
and just work. For the cause of sin is from us and within us; but the time and
object into which it must break out lie in God's power. He does not infuse new
malice, but, according to his infinite wisdom, moves, governs, bends, rules,
and directs the malice innate in human beings, when and how he pleases, to the
glory of his name—even though the ungodly are ignorant and indeed form a
wholly contrary design within themselves.

This is our position concerning God's providence in the argument we are
treating. It remains for us to confirm it again by the authority of God's Word
and also of the Fathers, because you compel us to do the same thing so often.
To this, then, we say it pertains that the Lord is so often said in the
Scriptures to have hardened, burdened, and held the minds of Pharaoh, Sihon,
the Canaanites, and the Egyptians, and even

[Printed p. 403; PDF p. 68; logical scan 427; OCR object 0436]

to have turned them to hatred of his people. Finally, Scripture manifestly
calls the ungodly a rod, hammer, saw, and axe which is aimed and driven by
God's hand. This is certainly not so that human beings may be exempted from
fault, or as if people had degenerated into logs or stumps, but so that we may
understand that God's providence is by no means idle. Otherwise, why would the
Lord call Nebuchadnezzar his servant and yet chastise him under that very name,
because he called the overthrow of the city of Jerusalem his own work? What
more? When the king's heart is said to be in the Lord's hand so that he may
incline it wherever he pleases, is not what we say manifestly confirmed: God
rouses even corrupt affections—not insofar as they are corrupt, but insofar as
he has determined to use them well—and inclines them as he pleases, so that
nothing happens except what he decreed?

This is also clear from David's example, whom the Lord is expressly said to
have incited to number the people. If this is blasphemy, come, rise up against
the Holy Spirit himself and fasten upon him the charge of blasphemy. “But,” you
will say, “elsewhere this very thing is ascribed to the devil; therefore the
devil's name must be supplied in the other passage where it is not expressed.”
Yet the words of the context read thus: “And the Lord's fury was again angry
against Israel, and he incited David against them to say, Go and number Israel
and Judah.” Who does not see that the Lord's name must be repeated *apo tou
koinou*, from the common clause, thus confirming what Solomon later wrote:
“The king's heart is inclined by the Lord wherever he pleases”? This is by no
means as though he infused new malice, but because he rouses the malice he
finds either to punish sins or to accomplish his other counsels, as a certain
most learned man said with complete correctness.

Even if the same motion is ascribed elsewhere to Satan, does it follow that
the Lord has no part in it, since it is well established that Satan is an
administrator of the Lord's fury? For what is said concerning Saul? “The
Spirit of the Lord deserted Saul, and an evil spirit from the Lord invaded
him.” Tell me, why are the words “from the Lord” added here? To signify idle
permission, or rather so that in this matter we may know that Satan was God's
instrument as he executed his just judgment? And not merely this, as Calvin
wrote rightly and truly, that at God's command Satan solicits minds toward
those desires which are evil both with respect to Satan himself and to the
wicked, but also that Satan effectively draws. For the Lord is not written to
have said to that lying spirit, “I permit you to deceive Ahab if you can,” but,
“You will persuade and prevail; go, therefore, and do so.” Nor does Paul write
that the Lord merely gives someone the power to deceive, but that he gives an
efficacy of error so that the lie is believed.

And lest you suppose that we were the first to teach thus in the Church from
God's Word, hear what Augustine wrote about these things. Julian the Pelagian,
as appears from Augustine, thought precisely what you think: when the Lord is
said to blind, harden, or hand over to a reprobate mind, nothing else is
signified than that the Lord abandons and permits. Augustine, however, shows
that God not only abandons but also declares his wrath and power. Julian says
these expressions are hyperbolic; Augustine insists they are proper. Julian
says, “They labored under their own desires beforehand; what need was there
for them to be handed over to them?” But Augustine says, “Do you think that
having desires and being handed over to them are the same? For they are handed
over to desires not merely to have them, but also to be wholly possessed by
them.”

Thus, just as God acts upon the bodies of the ungodly by afflicting them, so he
also acts upon their souls by impelling them into sins. In that passage
Augustine also treats the history of Shimei. “The Lord,” says David, “commanded
him to curse me.” Augustine says: “God justly inclined a will evil by its own
fault, so that it assailed David with insults.” And the reason is added: “The
Lord will repay me good for this curse.” The same Augustine says elsewhere:
“When God wills something to be done which must be done only by willing human
beings, he at the same time inclines their hearts so that they will it. Nor
does he act in hearts only by aiding, but also by judging, so that those who
had proposed no such thing fulfill what his hand and counsel decreed.”

The same author elsewhere says: “If Scripture is carefully examined, it shows
not only that God himself makes good human wills from evil ones and directs
the wills he has made good toward good acts and eternal life, but also that
the wills which preserve the creaturely order of the age are so within God's
power that he makes them incline whenever and wherever he wills, either to
bestow benefits upon some or to inflict punishments upon others.” And shortly
afterwards: “Who would not tremble at those judgments by which God does
whatever he wills even in the hearts of the wicked, while nevertheless
repaying them according to their deserts?” Again he says: “By the testimonies
of Scripture it is sufficiently manifest that God works in human hearts to
incline their wills wherever he wills—either toward good things according to
his mercy, or toward evil things according to their deserts—by his own
judgment, sometimes hidden, sometimes open, but always just. For it

[Printed p. 404; PDF p. 69; logical scan 428; OCR object 0437]

must be settled in your hearts that there is no injustice with God.”

You now hear how the Lord justly acts even in the hearts of the ungodly—or,
to speak properly in terms of your trifles, through the hearts of the
ungodly. But since after Augustine Calvin explained these things most fully,
what wickedness was it to pass over those distinctions, which rest upon the
manifest Word of God, and weave false inferences devised in your own brain,
making those very men their authors by whom they are so accurately and
diligently refuted? But it is time for us to hear your arguments here too.

I. CASTELLIO'S ARGUMENT FOR THE ELEVENTH CALUMNY

“Calvin ascribes to God what belongs to the devil, as the Scriptures testify
throughout.”

REFUTATION

But you do what belongs to the devil when you falsely accuse Calvin of that
blasphemy which, if any man did, he refuted accurately and diligently. God,
kindled with just fury against the Israelites, stimulates David through
Satan's ministry so that through the census of the people he may open the way
for his just judgments. What do you censure here? Satan incites David to this,
not in order to obey God, but so that God may be offended and, if possible,
David may be ruined with the whole people. David himself, perhaps incited by
pride or ambition, commands the people to be counted contrary to God's
command. Censure the devil and Satan, then. But is this to ascribe to God what
belongs to the devil? Are you not rather the blasphemer, since you abolish
God's providence for the most part?

II AND III. CASTELLIO'S ARGUMENTS FOR THE ELEVENTH CALUMNY

“If God suggests corrupt and dishonorable affections, and yet commands us to
resist corrupt affections, he commands us to resist himself and is contrary to
himself. Every good gift is from above, descending from the Father of lights.
Are corrupt affections also a good gift? Or do darknesses—for corrupt
affections are certainly darknesses—descend from the Father of lights?”

REFUTATION

We deservedly refer the depravity of the affections not to the first cause,
which always acts most justly, but to the principles from which they proceed,
namely the corruption of Satan and man. We therefore do not say that any
affections, insofar as they are corrupt, are suggested by God. But when you say
that the Lord permits them, you truly ascribe to God what is most foreign to
his nature, even if we were to grant you this idle permission. For what sort
of justice does God possess if he in any way permits sins insofar as they are
sins, or affections insofar as they are corrupt? Thus, wretch, you slaughter
yourself with your own sword; and when you infer these things truly, you truly
convict yourself.

IV. CASTELLIO'S ARGUMENT FOR THE ELEVENTH CALUMNY

“James expressly writes that no one is tempted by God, but each by his own
desire.”

REFUTATION

Whether you will or not, you must acknowledge that the verb *to tempt* is
*polysēmōs*, multivalent. For concerning the evil prophet it is expressly
written in Deuteronomy, “The Lord tempts you”; and in the same book the Lord
says that he tempted the people in the wilderness for forty years; and
elsewhere, “God tempts Abraham.” Indeed, human beings too are said to tempt
God, and Paul admonishes us to tempt ourselves. Nevertheless, James denies
that anyone is tempted by God, but says rather by his own desire—namely, in the
manner in which Satan tempts us, or we are tempted by other people or by
ourselves.

For the Lord is said to tempt us in his own manner, that is, to test us, so
that, when an occasion is presented for disclosing our disposition, we
ourselves may understand what we are, lest by being pleased with ourselves we
deceive ourselves, but rather, once our weakness is recognized, begin to
repent; or so that our dissimulation may become manifest to others. Hence the
name *temptation* very often means affliction, because our faith is exercised.
Thus Paul admonishes us to tempt ourselves, that is, diligently examine
whether we are in the faith, as he himself expressly interprets it. Thus we
are forbidden to tempt God, that is, to test his mind as though we doubted his
faithfulness, and by this means to provoke his patience to anger.

Satan also tempts, and is therefore called the tempter. We human beings also
tempt one another, and lust is said to tempt us. But when these things are
said, the name *temptation* does not belong to God, since it signifies nothing
other than solicitation to evil, which cannot possibly befall God. Yet God is
said to lead into temptation, namely when he employs the agency of Satan or
others to accomplish his counsel. Does he therefore tempt? By no means. For
not even then is he the author of evil, unless perhaps he must be judged evil
when he executes his just judgments, or uses even our falls to raise and then
confirm us and to instruct the whole Church. Thus Adam was tempted and indeed
overcome by Satan not apart from God's ordination, but so that God might use
that fall both to display the riches of his goodness much

[Printed p. 405; PDF p. 70; logical scan 429; OCR object 0438]

more openly than in creation itself, and also to open the way for his just
judgments. Thus many of the saints long ago fell into the gravest temptations
by God's own command and will, and daily still do so; yet with great benefit to
themselves and no fault on God's part, as the saints themselves confess. Thus
an evil spirit from the Lord harasses Saul; and there is no doubt that this
spirit hurled Saul into those dreadful crimes. Did the Lord therefore tempt
Saul—that is, solicit him to evil? By no means; rather, he chastened Saul with
just penalties. For the evil proceeds from the corrupt principles of the
instruments; but the good elicited from the evil is owed entirely to God. In
sum, then, as a most learned writer has observed, God does not tempt—namely, in
the manner in which the devil and evil people are accustomed to tempt. And the
reason is that God is not tempted by evils. For whoever tempts another to evil
has himself first been tempted and overcome by evil. The devil and evil people
therefore tempt: that is, thinking wickedly and setting a wicked end before
themselves, they sharpen their malice and drive others toward evil. But God
tempts us for another end: that we may have our weakness or our strength
tested; that we may fall into some grave calamity and so begin to come to our
senses; and finally, when there is need, that he may punish sins by sins and by
other just penalties. Does someone who says this ascribe to God what belongs
to our own desire?

But a question remains: Why does James pass over Satan and say that we are
tempted by our own desire? Evidently because, although Satan solicits us and
we people incite one another, all these things nevertheless tend to this end:
that the desire which is in us should be kindled. But that inward motion is the
true and proper principle of our affections and consequently of our actions.
Since, therefore, Adam at the outset corrupted himself spontaneously and in
the proper sense—although the devil was prompting him—and since the people
descended from him, by this corrupt desire, likewise move themselves properly
and spontaneously and daily make themselves worse, why should they cast any
blame upon God, when they cannot properly accuse even Satan or anyone else as
causes of their destruction, although these are partners in the same crimes?

But to what end is all this? Let us hear Calvin himself speak. Explaining those
solemn words, AND LEAD US NOT INTO TEMPTATION, he says: “The term *temptation*
is often taken generally for any kind of testing. In this sense Abraham is
said to have been tempted by God when his faith was tested. Thus we are
tempted by adverse as well as prosperous circumstances, because on such an
occasion affections which had previously lain hidden emerge into the light.
But here an inward temptation is meant, which may aptly be called the devil's
fan for kindling our desire. For it would be absurd to ask God to render us
exempt from every proof of faith. Thus all corrupt motions which solicit us to
sin are included under the name of temptation. And although it is impossible
for us not to feel such stings in our minds, since throughout the whole course
of life our warfare with the flesh is unceasing, we nevertheless ask the Lord
not to subject us to temptations or allow us to be overwhelmed by them. And in
order to express more clearly how liable we are to continual falls and ruin
unless God sustains us with his hand, Christ used this form of speech: *Lead us
not into temptation*; or, as others translate it, *Do not bring us in*. It is
certain that everyone is tempted by his own desire, as James teaches; but
because God not only permits us to Satan's lust so that he may kindle the fire
of desire, but also employs Satan as a minister of his wrath whenever he wills
to drive people headlong into destruction, God himself also leads into
temptation in his own manner. In this sense an evil spirit of God is said to
have seized Saul, and numerous passages of Scripture tend to the same point.
Yet we shall not therefore call God the author of evils; for when he sends
people into a reprobate mind, he does not exercise an indiscriminate tyranny,
but executes his just judgments, though they are hidden.”

Do you recognize, Castellio, that polished and rounded brevity of Calvin? I
think not, since you are accustomed to disdain everyone's writings in
comparison with your own. Certainly, if you had read these things, you were
exceedingly wicked when you twisted these arguments against Calvin; but if you
had not read them—which I prefer to suppose—what sort of conscience is this
of yours, to fasten so great a blasphemy upon a man whose position you do not
understand?

V. CASTELLIO'S ARGUMENT FOR THE ELEVENTH CALUMNY

“As for your addition that God does this for his own glory, they say that it
is ridiculous, for glory is not normally bestowed on account of evil. Thus,
when Nebuchadnezzar experienced God's justice and power in being changed into
a brute nature because of his own pride, he gave God glory—namely, perceiving
and judging him to be just.”

REFUTATION

No falsehood proceeds from God, not even when he sends lying spirits with the
efficacy of error. For in falsehood, as in other vices and crimes, the defining
mark is an evil affection,

[Printed p. 406; PDF p. 71; logical scan 430; OCR object 0439]

which cannot possibly befall God. But the fact that a lying spirit and people
who are liars by nature tell lies must be attributed wholly to their own
principles, depraved not by creation but by voluntary corruption. What is a
lie with respect to Satan himself and to human beings, however, must with
respect to God be regarded as a just judgment. If you deny that God also
gathers a rich harvest of glory from human lies, I can first establish what
you deny by your own example. How greatly do you suppose your lies have
benefited our churches, as we carefully compare them with God's truth? Indeed,
if your purpose had not been entirely different, we would confess that in
this respect we owe much to you and your faction, because you have given us an
occasion to investigate more diligently those things from the knowledge of
which we certainly receive incredible fruit. The Church's increase is so
closely joined to God's glory that they plainly cannot be separated. Adam was
overcome by Satan's lie. What benefits came from this deed to us, and what
glory came to God? Certainly far greater glory than from the creation of the
world itself. God's wrath is revealed against all people who hold God's truth
in unrighteousness. This wrath is nothing other than God's just judgment. Do
you really suppose that God's glory is not enlarged when he exercises his
justice? What, then? When he takes away the prudence of the prudent, when he
hides his wisdom from the wise and reveals it to little children, what remains
for those miserable people except falsehood? Yet you cannot deny that this
greatly commends God's justice and wisdom. “Do you therefore,” you will say,
“commend falsehood to us?” By no means. Rather, I marvel at God's infinite
wisdom, which brings even those things in which he punishes the insult done to
him to a fitting end—that is, to his own glory—and elicits material for his
glory even from human crimes. If this does not satisfy you and you continue to
answer, “If God's truth abounded to his glory through my lie, why am I still
condemned as a sinner, and why should we not rather do evil that good may
come?”—if, I say, you continue to answer in that manner, I shall make this one
reply with Paul: you are one of those whose condemnation is just.

As for what you adduce concerning Nebuchadnezzar, what do you disclose except
the same disposition? When Nebuchadnezzar had paid the penalty for his pride,
he gave God the praise of justice. What else can you infer from this than that
God's glory is illustrated by his just judgments? Now, since the efficacy of
error, blinding, and hardening are numbered among his most just judgments, do
you not see that you are held entangled in the very nets which you had woven
for us?

VI. CASTELLIO'S ARGUMENT FOR THE ELEVENTH CALUMNY

“God wishes to be praised by all nations. *Praise the Lord, all nations.* He
must therefore do those things which all nations can recognize and then
praise. Yet no nation will ever acknowledge it to be just for a person to be
punished because of things which God himself suggested to him. For we ask: If
God were to punish us because we have a beard, would he not wrong us, since he
himself gave us a beard and the beard is not within our power of choice? What
bearded man could ever praise him?”

REFUTATION

I ask you: how ridiculous and even profane is this inference—that, since God
wishes to be praised by all nations, no ground for praising him remains
unless it comes from things which can be approved by the common judgment of
all nations? Yet Paul says that he preaches the gospel which is a stumbling
block to the Jews and is regarded as foolishness by the other nations. Whom
could you readily persuade that salvation should be expected from one who was
the most miserable of all people? Do you not hear how Paul's speech was
received at Athens? Do you not hear Paul cry out that the ψυχικός, the natural
person, does not receive the things of God? No, the matter stands thus: the
Lord shared the benefit of the gospel with all peoples, and for that reason
the Prophet rightly summons them to celebrate God's praises. For God does not
tolerate being praised by just anyone, but by the saints alone, as it is
written: *Rejoice in the Lord, you righteous; praise befits the upright.* And
elsewhere: *God said to the sinner, Why do you recount my ordinances? Why do
you proclaim my covenant with your mouth?* The praise which is pleasing and
delightful to God must therefore proceed from faith, since without faith
nothing can please God. But faith apprehends God not as we conceive him by our
common sense—for, as John says, the darkness does not comprehend the light—but
as he has set himself forth to be viewed and contemplated in his Word by us,
in whose hearts the day-star has shone. Now, since we have demonstrated so
plainly from almost innumerable testimonies of both Testaments that not only
things universally and in general, but also each particular thing, are
governed by God's providence and eternal decree, so that God justly wills and
justly does even those things which

[Printed p. 407; PDF p. 72; logical scan 431; OCR object 0440]

both Satan and human beings unjustly will and unjustly do, and so that no
contagion from the motions or actions reaches the first cause, but, because it
proceeds from the corrupt principles of the instruments, remains there;
finally, since we have plainly demonstrated that absolutely nothing occurs
which does not redound to God's glory—so much so that God's truth is
illustrated even by our falsehood—what remains except for you to get far away
with this utterly false argument of yours? As for your babbling about a beard
and a bearded man, does it not plainly prove what sort of disposition you have
brought to the treatment of these mysteries of God? For what do a beard or a
bearded man have in common with God's kingdom? Indeed, who would not think
that things so foolish proceeded from an infant rather than from a bearded
man? Nevertheless, I want to answer you briefly, lest you complain that we
evade arguments which we cannot seriously refute. A beard arises from a
merely natural motion; sin from a voluntary motion, and a corrupt one, because
the will is corrupt, the judgment is corrupt, the understanding is corrupt,
the appetite is corrupt, and finally the whole person is enslaved to sin. Yet
this fact does not result in the Lord's abdicating command over that effectual
providence, as we have demonstrated by the testimonies of Scripture already
cited. Hence it comes about that he no less justly and rightly inclines, bends,
and directs corrupt wills wherever he pleases than if they were not corrupt.
But you must consider what kind of judgment it is to compare things so
dissimilar as though they were entirely alike—that is, to compare what is
merely natural with what is voluntary.

VII. CASTELLIO'S ARGUMENT FOR THE ELEVENTH CALUMNY

“If Calvin says that this is God's hidden judgment and unknown to us, we shall
reply that God's mysteries are indeed unknown to us, but as far as justice is
concerned, it is known to us and revealed in the gospel. God will judge the
world according to that revealed gospel, as Paul teaches, and not according
to that hidden judgment of Calvin; and thus he will obtain from everyone, both
the pious and the impious. For everyone, both pious and impious, will see that
it is equitable for those who have not obeyed the truth—not a hidden truth
such as Calvin's, but a manifest one such as the gospel—to be punished, and
for those who have obeyed it to receive a reward.”

REFUTATION

Something is missing from this writing of yours, but the fault belongs to the
man to whom you entrusted the copying of your book. Otherwise, everything was
certainly printed in good faith from that very exemplar which you sent to
Paris to be secretly printed there. But to the matter. Why, I ask, do you
bring up these things? Do we say that there is any rule of good and evil, just
and unjust, other than God's Law? Do we show any way of salvation other than
the gospel concerning the Son of God, just as it stands in writing? Do we say
that God will judge the world by any prescription other than his Word? Are we
not rather those who three years ago defended to the best of our ability the
authority of God's Word not only against the papists but also against you
yourself, when you command us to await new revelations on the ground that
God's Word as delivered to us through the Prophets and Apostles does not
suffice to decide the churches' controversies? And are you not the very man
who, in your commentaries on the First Epistle to the Corinthians, affirms
that in his Epistles Paul delivered to us only the rudiments of religion and
possessed another, more perfect doctrine which he shared with certain of his
perfect disciples? How, then, does it happen that, suddenly changed, you rise
up against us as though we did not rest content in God's one Word? We are
certainly the same men we were, and nowhere and never have we learned to rest
except in God's one Word. God will judge the world by the gospel: that is,
whomever he finds to have embraced Christ by faith—which will indeed be
evident from their fruits—he will absolve and declare coheirs with Christ. The
rest, justly condemned, he will hand over to be tormented in eternal flames.
According to this will, I say, of the heavenly Father, Christ will judge the
living and the dead. But how can you infer from this that all God's counsels
or judgments, or his entire justice and will, have been disclosed to us—that
is, that whatever he does daily and whatever he determined from eternity to do
in and concerning every individual lies open to us? Or are you the one who
can explain to us the reasons of all God's counsels, or even of his justice,
which he has employed toward the pious as well as the impious from the
foundation of the world and which he will continue to employ? Do you, I ask,
know those mysteries of God's eternal providence? “No,” you will say, “I admit
that certain mysteries of God are unknown to us, but I say that his entire
justice has been revealed to us.” Yet a little before you utterly denied that
there was any hidden will of God. Do God's mysteries not pertain either to his
will or to his justice? Therefore first determine with yourself what you mean
before you attack things rightly and consistently handed down by others. But
we have more than sufficiently proved that we do not wander outside God's
Word, or turn even a nail's breadth away from it, when we say that God acts
even through them and always exercises his just judgments, although these are
sometimes hidden,

[Printed p. 408; PDF p. 73; logical scan 432; OCR object 0441]

upon those whom he deservedly punishes as rebels; and that not even those
things which are contrary to his will occur apart from God's will. We have
proved, I say, that when we proclaim with the Prophets and Apostles that the
Lord's ways are hidden and indeed unsearchable, we say nothing which God has
not expressly revealed in his Word. For the Lord has also revealed this to us:
there are boundless matters in the ordering of affairs which he employs, and
if anyone wishes to search them out, he will achieve only this—that the nearer
he comes to God's light, the more he is blinded, until at last he perishes,
swallowed up by the majesty of the divine light.

VIII. CASTELLIO'S ARGUMENT FOR THE ELEVENTH CALUMNY

“God's wrath, Paul says, is revealed against all the impiety and injustice of
people who hold the truth in injustice. But if Calvin's position is true,
God's wrath is revealed against all innocent people. For if he suggests
corrupt affections, he is angry with them and hates them before they have
corrupt affections. Now, to suggest corrupt affections belongs to hatred; he
therefore hates the innocent. For people are innocent before they have
corrupt affections, since sin comes from corrupt affections—or, rather, sin is
a corrupt affection.”

REFUTATION

First of all, you plainly twist the passage of Paul which you cite. For it does
not pertain only to some part of humanity; rather, by this statement Paul
equally condemns every single person, without even one exception, for
injustice. For all people, insofar as they are children of Adam and outside
Christ—and in this passage Paul considers them in this respect—stand convicted
of the crime which Paul discusses, because not even one of them either
worships God or helps his neighbor according to the prescription of that
knowledge of God which he has in part engraved on every mind and in part set
before everyone through contemplation of created things. This is the genuine
explanation of Paul's words, as is clear from the sequence and conclusion of
the entire argument. But what does this have to do with the matter we are
discussing? Is the Lord not offended by human injustice simply because he
employs it well, both to console the pious and to chastise the impious?
Moreover, why do you twist against us your babbling about the suggestion of
corrupt affections, when we teach nothing other than that God justly and wisely
moves, inclines, and governs even evil wills and brings them to the end
appointed by himself? For—as I have now said a thousand times—we locate the
whole depravity and defect of the affections and actions, as is proper, in the
corrupt and defective principles. Thus you contend not against us but against
your own shadow.

Now, what sort of things are those which you add? First you posit that people
are innocent before they have corrupt affections, “because,” you say, “sin
comes from corrupt affections.” When you say this, are you not manifestly a
Pelagian? Explain, then: since we are born children of wrath—indeed, are
conceived in iniquity—and since from our very origin we need the pledge of the
remission of sins and of regeneration, namely Baptism, how is there no sin
except from corrupt affections, and how are we neither guilty nor children of
wrath before we suffer from corrupt affections? But if these things are true
—though they are utterly false—from where, finally, will vicious affections
emerge? Plainly, from corrupt education, from imitation, and finally from a
merely free will which first enslaves itself to sin in individual people when
they begin to suffer from evil affections. Thus people would have no need of
regenerating grace, since sin does not come by propagation, if they wished to
follow nature. And are you not ashamed to discourse about the Christian
religion, when you serve up to us again those rancid poisons of Pelagius? You
see, moreover, that once this foundation has been overthrown, whatever you had
built upon it collapses.

“If Calvin's position is true,” you say, “God's wrath is revealed against all
innocent people.” Far from Calvin being associated with this blasphemy, on the
contrary he does not even agree with those who maintain that human nature was
only damaged and wounded, and who deny that concupiscence is sin even in the
regenerate. Rather, from God's Word he demonstrates that the whole person, in
all that he is, is from the very moment of conception nothing other than
Satan's slave and liable to God's just wrath. And since Calvin, if anyone in
our memory, has defended this doctrine most solidly and learnedly against
Pighius and all the Sophists, with what effrontery did you dare write, “If
Calvin's doctrine is true, it follows that God exercises his wrath against all
innocent people,” when on almost every page of his books Calvin cries out that
no one is innocent even from the moment of birth? “But then,” you will say,
“how does your doctrine cohere?” It certainly coheres perfectly. For we do not
say that God made or makes evil wills, or implants malice, or suggests corrupt
affections, or solicits anyone to evil. Rather, we say this: God also moves
human beings' innate malice well, and inclines, bends, and governs it wherever
he pleases. Thus it is plain that Gregory wrote most correctly that the wicked,
even when they wish to change God's will, do God's will and, by resisting his
counsel,

[Printed p. 409; PDF p. 74; logical scan 433; OCR object 0442]

obey God. Moreover, if we granted what you add—that to suggest corrupt
affections belongs to hatred—would it follow from this that God hates the
innocent? By no means. For we say what is most true: everyone is born guilty
and is therefore liable to the Lord's just judgments unless delivered through
Christ. But not even what you say—that God hates those whose corrupt
affections he moves—is always and absolutely true. For the Lord loved Paul
from eternity, and yet through Satan he incited and sharpened Paul's rage and
cruelty for as long as he pleased, until he had mercy upon him and graciously
disclosed to him, as he rushed toward his own destruction, that eternal will
by which he had set him apart from his mother's womb. Thus the Lord, through
Satan, incites David to number the people, although David was his elect and
most beloved king and Prophet. For although the Lord hates all iniquity, he
does not hate all those in whom iniquity is found and whose iniquity he also
uses well whenever, however, and as often as he pleases. Otherwise, whom could
he ever embrace? Thus, wherever you turn, everything you spew against us—or,
rather, against God's truth—is false and foolish.

CALUMNY XII

In their impiety the impious do God's work rather than their own.

I. CASTELLIO'S ARGUMENT FOR THE TWELFTH CALUMNY

“If this is so, God is angry with what is good. For if impiety is God's work,
impiety is good, since all God's works are good. And if impiety is good, piety
is therefore evil, since it is contrary to impiety. Consequently, when the
Sacred Writings say, *Hate evil; love good*, they command us to love impiety
and hate piety. They say, moreover, that this article has some Libertine savor
about it, and they marvel that you are so hostile to the Libertines.”

REFUTATION

I oppose this saying of Augustine to all your calumnies: “That the wicked sin
belongs to them; but that by sinning they do this or that is from the power of
God, who distributes the darkness as he sees fit.” Do you see how every
objection you have raised against us collapses at the slightest touch? For all
these things which you ascribe to us are false, and here too you contend
against your own shadow. But come, let us hear Calvin himself speak, so that
even children may judge this calumny of yours. These are certainly Calvin's
words:

“That God continually governs human hands—now holding them bound, now bending
them this way and that to execute what he has determined—will not be called
into question by anyone moderately practiced in Scripture. Indeed, it is
received by the almost universal judgment of all that, whatever people
undertake, the outcome is in God's hand. But because that knowledge is very
slight and fleeting amid the dense darkness of the human mind, Scripture
builds for us a higher watchtower from which we may behold God so directing
all human works that he applies them to the end appointed by himself. The sum
comes down to this: although people run wanton like untamed beasts restrained
by no bonds, they are nevertheless governed by a hidden bridle, so that they
cannot move even a finger except to execute God's work rather than their
own.”

You seized upon this last statement of Calvin in order to persuade people
either that Calvin abolishes every distinction between good and evil, or that
God is made the author of crimes. But how shamelessly you draw these
conclusions is evident from the points Calvin attached before it; because you
lost all sense of shame long ago, however, you passed them over without a
care.

“For,” Calvin says, “the faithful who render him voluntary obedience, no less
than the angels, must be regarded as his hands. For this reason I make special
mention here of those who intend nothing less than to possess anything akin
to God's counsel or in agreement with his will. The impious indeed applaud
themselves as though they had gained their wishes. But the outcome finally
proves that through them—though they are impious and unwilling—what had been
divinely ordained was fulfilled. Moreover, God himself at one time employs
the impious like scourges to punish human sins; at another, as though dragging
them by their twisted necks, he compels them to be ministers of his
beneficence. To gather examples of the former point would be an immense task;
it will be better to touch upon only a few. After God incites the Assyrian to
war, calls him the rod of his fury, and teaches that he is armed with no other
staff than that of God's wrath, he afterwards inveighs against the Assyrian's
pride because he did not recognize himself as an axe or saw driven from
elsewhere. For this reason those whom their own ambition, cruelty, and greed
otherwise drive are said both to be consecrated by God and to be his hired
soldiers and to perform his work; and the Lord in turn declares that by a
whistle or trumpet he calls them to arms. But even one passage from Moses
demonstrates clearly enough that a way is prepared for divine beneficence by
human crimes too. The conspiracy of Joseph's brothers when they sold him had
been more than criminal, treacherous, and cruel. Yet Joseph transfers the
cause of this sale to God, though in another respect. *It was not you*, he
says, *but the Lord who sent me ahead, that I might feed you.* It is therefore
plain that, while they

[Printed p. 410; PDF p. 75; logical scan 434; OCR object 0443]

acted impiously, God nevertheless worked through them so that they might find
life in death. As far as lay in them, they had killed their brother; from that
deed life shines upon them. The same thing may be perceived in Satan, the
chief of all crimes and head of the impious. God sends him to deceive Ahab
with this command: that he should be a lying spirit in the mouth of all the
prophets. Thus the deceiving spirit is a minister of God's wrath to blind the
impious who had refused to obey the truth. By contrast, Paul recalls that a
thorn in the flesh, a messenger of Satan by whom he is beaten with blows, was
given him so that he should not grow proud on account of the greatness of his
revelations. Here Satan's venom is an antidote for curing pride. What sort of
physician, I ask, is Satan, who has learned nothing but to destroy and kill?
Yet God, who once commanded light to shine out of darkness, whenever he sees
fit wondrously brings salvation even from hell itself, and so turns darkness
into light. Moreover, what Satan performs Scripture affirms, in another
respect, to be God's work: namely, insofar as God holds him bound to the
service of his providence, bends him wherever he sees fit, and applies his
labor to God's own uses.”

So far Calvin. When you have read his words, perhaps you will come to be
ashamed of those calumnies.

CALUMNY XIII

By God's intention we sin necessarily, while by our own we sin, as it were, by
chance.

CALUMNY XIV

Those things which people perpetrate by their own corrupt will also proceed
from the will of God.

RESPONSE

But nowhere have we said either that a person sins by God's intention, as you
indeed interpret it—that is, in such a manner that the sin pleases him—or
that sin occurs by chance, than which nothing more absurd can be said. These
monstrosities, then, are your own. If it had even entered our mind to say or
write that sins proceed from God's will insofar as they are sins, we would
confess that we deserve every punishment. But what Augustine's position was
concerning necessity—a position which we willingly embrace as most consonant
with God's Word—is more than sufficiently evident from many passages. “God's
will,” he says, “is the necessity of things.” And again: “God so created the
lower causes that the thing of which they are the causes can proceed from
them, but need not do so; but he has so hidden the higher and remote causes in
himself that what he made able to proceed from the lower causes must proceed
from the higher.” That this necessity does not impede the spontaneous motion
of the will, the same Augustine teaches as follows:

“We do not say that all things happen by fate; indeed, we say that nothing
happens by fate. For where speakers normally employ the term *fate*—that is,
of the configuration of stars under which each person is conceived or born—we
show that it has no force, because the thing itself is asserted in vain. But
we neither deny the order of causes, in which God's will is supremely
powerful, nor designate it with the term *fate*, unless perhaps we understand
*fatum* to be derived from *fando*, that is, from speaking. For we cannot deny
that it is written in the Sacred Writings, *Once God has spoken.* When it says,
*Once he has spoken*, it is understood that he has spoken immovably—that is,
unchangeably—just as he unchangeably knows all things which will be and which
he himself will do. In this sense, then, we could call it *fatum* from *fando*,
were it not that this term is now customarily understood of another matter,
toward which we do not wish human hearts to be inclined. But it does not
follow that, if the entire order of causes is certain to God, nothing is
therefore within the decision of our will. For our wills themselves belong to
the order of causes which is certain to God and are contained within his
foreknowledge, since human wills are also causes of human works.”

Likewise, in the same place: “Our wills have as much power as God willed and
foreknew them to have. Therefore, whatever power they have, they most
certainly have; and whatever they themselves will do, they will certainly do,
because he whose foreknowledge cannot be deceived foreknew that they would
have that power and would do it.” And again: “If that is defined as necessity
according to which we say that something must be in this manner or occur in
this manner, I do not know why we should fear that it will take away our
freedom of will.”

So far I have recited Augustine's words. What Calvin thinks about necessity
and contingency is plainly evident from his own words. For after he had taught
from God's Word that God directs by his counsel those things which appear most
fortuitous, and had shown that he, like Augustine long ago, was undeservedly
burdened by the calumny of Stoic fate, he finally adds the following:

“Farewell,” he says, “to the Stoics with their fate; for us let God's free will
be the ruler of all things. But it seems absurd to abolish contingency from
the world. I pass over the distinctions commonly used in the schools. What I
shall offer will, in my judgment, be simple and entirely unforced, and then
suited to the practice of life. What God has determined must occur, and yet it
is neither strictly nor by its own nature necessary. I have a familiar example
in Christ's bones. Scripture testifies that Christ assumed a body entirely
like ours; therefore no sane person will hesitate to confess that his bones
were breakable. But it seems to me another and separate

[Printed p. 411; PDF p. 76; logical scan 435; OCR object 0444]

question whether any one of his bones could have been broken. Since it had
been determined by God's fixed decree, all of them necessarily had to remain
whole and uninjured. I do not speak in this way because I shrink from the
received forms of speech concerning qualified and absolute necessity, and
likewise the necessity of the consequent and the necessity of the
consequence, but lest any subtlety hinder readers from recognizing the truth
of what I say, even if they are the least learned. Therefore, if you consider
nature in Christ's bones, they were breakable; but if you consider that decree
of God which was manifested at its proper time, they were no more liable to
breaking than angels are to human miseries. Now, because it befits us to
contemplate the divinely established order of nature, I by no means reject
contingency as far as our perception is concerned.”

So far Calvin—certainly so plainly and lucidly that anyone who doubts these
things is looking for a knot in a bulrush. But so that you may have no possible
complaint about our obscurity, and since scarcely any calumny is more
frequently on people's lips or more boldly thrown against us, especially by
the unlearned, than this one concerning the necessity of Stoic fate, I want it
to be explained fully and clearly, once and for all, what we think about these
matters. I shall do so not in my own words, but in those of the most learned
and in every respect most excellent Peter Martyr. Although he has not yet
published the things which I am about to transcribe, excerpted from his
lectures, I nevertheless hope he will not be unwilling to grant this too to
his Beza: that Beza may employ his protection, obtained from wherever he can,
to crush falsehood. Peter Martyr therefore taught as follows when he
interpreted the First Book of Samuel in the most illustrious church of Zurich,
in the presence and with the approval of many truly pious and learned men:

“The question,” he says, “is whether God's providence is immutable. Why should
it not be? For it is the rule of everything that occurs. *I am the Lord*, he
says, *and I do not change*; and with him *there is no change or shadow of
turning*. And Proverbs 19: *There are many thoughts in the human heart, but the
counsel of the Lord remains.* Isaiah 46: *I speak, and my counsel remains, and
I do all that I will.* For since providence is God's will and knowledge, and
these pertain to God's essence, it cannot change unless God himself changes.
Secondary causes, because they are diverse, frequently obstruct one another.
We see this occur in the influences of heaven, when some are hindered by
others. But God's will can be impeded by no force.

“Yet formerly he willed the Jewish ceremonies, while later he willed them to
be abolished. How, then, is God's providence not changeable? I answer that
there is altogether one and the same will in God, but that from eternity he
foresaw what would be fitting at different times. Augustine says to
Marcellinus that a farmer sows at one time, reaps at another, and manures the
soil at another; yet the art of farming is not therefore changeable. He says
that a certain physician, Vindicianus, gave medicine to a sick man and cured
him. After many years the man relapsed into the same illness and, without
consulting the physician, took the same medicine. But when he grew worse, he
went to the physician, reported the matter, and began to complain about the
medicine. Vindicianus then said, ‘No wonder, for I did not give it to you.’
When others marveled at this and supposed that he ordinarily employed some
kind of magical power, Vindicianus said, ‘There is nothing of the kind; he is
at a different age and has different humors than when I first gave him that
medicine.’ But will the art of medicine not be consistent for this reason? In
precisely the same way, although God foresees all things, he nevertheless has
not decreed that they all be done at the same time.

“Now let us come to the matter itself: If God's providence is certain, does it
admit any contingency? Here I shall first employ two distinctions, and then
answer. One necessity is simple; another is from hypothesis. For when we say
that God exists and that God is wise or just, we understand this to be simply
and absolutely necessary. Other things are necessary from hypothesis, as in
the saying commonly bandied about in the schools: ‘What exists, insofar as it
exists, is necessary.’ Christ and the Prophets predict that the city of
Jerusalem is to be overthrown; therefore it will necessarily be overthrown—not
because that necessity lies in the nature of the city, but because Christ and
the Prophets, who could not be mistaken, predicted it. *There must be
factions*, Paul says. And Christ says, *It is necessary that stumbling blocks
come.* For when these causes are posited—namely, people's corrupt dispositions
and the devil's hatred toward the human race—and the end is also posited,
namely that the elect should be tested, it is necessary from hypothesis that
those things occur. Things can also be considered in two ways: either as they
lie hidden in their causes, or as they actually exist; and in the latter
respect they possess necessity. They are no longer undetermined. To write or
not to write is contingent; but if you are now actually writing, it is no
longer contingent, but necessary. Thus we say that sensory knowledge is
certain, because the things themselves cannot be otherwise. But things can
also be considered as they lie hidden in their own causes. Since, however,
causes can sometimes produce effects and sometimes cannot, there is no
necessary power of producing in them. But if those things are referred to
God, the account is very different. For he calls the things that do not exist
as though they do. Because he encompasses all time and has neither beginning
nor end, all things which will occur even after an infinite span of time are
nevertheless present to him. To this is also added the will of God.

[Printed p. 412; PDF p. 77; logical scan 436; OCR object 0445]

“For we must posit in him not bare knowledge but purposive knowledge
(προαιρετική). And in this way I say that the things themselves possess a
certain necessity. Augustine, *On the Literal Meaning of Genesis*, book 6,
chapter 15: ‘There are many ways in which God could have made man and other
things; and those ways possessed a certain possibility, not necessity. But
this is from God's will, whose will is the necessity of things.’ Yet although
things of this sort, when referred to him, are necessary, we must nevertheless
evaluate them from their internal and proper causes and call them contingent.
For the effect need not be of the same kind as the efficient cause. If you ask
why these two kinds of causes exist in the nature of things, so that some are
determinate and necessary while others are indeterminate and contingent,
nothing else can be answered except that God imposed these modes upon all
things. God produces, defines, and determines everything, but not in such a
way as to disturb and scatter the nature of things. Boethius says in his
*Topics* that εἱμαρμένη is so called ‘from joining and proceeding in sequence’
(παρὰ τὸ εἴρειν καὶ χωρεῖν ἀκολούθως). For God indeed draws all things, but in
a manner he so yields as to disturb nothing. Thus, although things by their
own nature incline equally toward either side, they are nevertheless inclined
more toward one side by God. Saul's will was by its own nature no more
determined to go than to remain. But because God willed to send him to Samuel,
his will began to incline toward one side. Thus God sets his father's will
before his understanding and causes it to strike and move his mind
effectually, so that all other desires for ease and idleness—if any existed
which could have held him back—should give way. Thus it came about that Saul's
will obeyed God's providence. Yet meanwhile the nature of the thing is not
violated, since Saul's will was equally free toward either side. From this it
is evident how necessary God's grace is for us. For our will, since it is
depraved in every way, turns everything toward the worse side. There are also
many things which dull and blind our understanding, so that the will does not
readily follow. God therefore sets the good before our understanding; then he
kindles faith and rouses our dullness, so that we may will effectually.

“But why,” you will say, “is something called contingent when it has already
been determined toward one side by God and has thus been made necessary?” I
answer: From its own proper principles each thing is contingent; but the
providence which imposes necessity is an external cause, from which things
ought not to derive their name. I know that many maintain that things which
cannot be accomplished by human powers are brought by God to this point: that
our will can either choose or reject them. But they maintain that God's
providence stops there and advances no further, and that when God foresees
what each person will choose and what he will reject, his foreknowledge is not
impeded. These things, however, do not sufficiently agree with the Divine
Writings. For they teach that God does not so care for things that he abandons
them, but, as we have said, brings everything to its own ends. And those ends
serve God's providence. Thus Paul says that God did all things according to
the purpose of his will. Thus God himself says in Isaiah, *I do all that I
will.* And Christ says, *Not even a sparrow falls to the ground without the
will of our Father.*

“I know that Origen, Cyril, Chrysostom, and others, being pressed by the
plainest passages of Scripture—such as, *It was necessary for Christ to
suffer*, and, *It was necessary for the Scriptures to be fulfilled*—interpret
them in this way: these things did not occur because God foresaw them; rather,
God foresaw them because they were going to occur. If this position were
speaking about simple knowledge, it could not be criticized so greatly. A man
does not write because I see him writing; rather, I see him writing because he
is writing. But in God we cannot posit bare knowledge: we must also add the
will which directs and orders everything. Nevertheless, what they say will be
true if it is understood from the effect, or, as they say, *a posteriori*. For
from the fact that something occurred we understand that it was God's counsel
for it to occur so. Otherwise Scripture speaks plainly: *Christ had to die;
the Scriptures had to be fulfilled.* But how did they have to be fulfilled?
From hypothesis, because God had so foreseen, not because that necessity lay
in the nature of the thing.

“But you will perhaps say that the causes in the nature of the thing itself
are indeterminate for this reason: I do not take them as full and complete
causes, since God's providence had to be added. I answer that I take only the
internal and proper causes of each thing; because their effects could either
be produced or not be produced by them, they are contingent. But I do not add
providence because it is an extrinsic cause. If it is added, one cannot avoid
some necessity following from the hypothesis. For example, Saul met young men
carrying goat kids, loaves, and wine. Their will was by its own nature
undetermined, so that they might either give him something or not give it. But
by his providence God determined that will toward one side. They were going to
Bethel to offer sacrifice; Saul met them tired from the journey and oppressed
by hunger; it seemed humane to refresh him. God presented these things to
their minds, and if there were any things which could have hindered their will,
he restrained them. Thus far we have discussed only human wills. For in other
contingent things I know that there are innumerable ways which God is
accustomed to employ. We have another example of this matter from Ezekiel 21.
Nebuchadnezzar

[Printed p. 413; PDF p. 78; logical scan 437; OCR object 0446]

sets out into Syria and, when he is at a crossroads, begins to deliberate
whether he should march against the Jews or against Rabbah, the king of the
Ammonites. He entrusts the decision to lots; God governs them and brings him
to Jerusalem. The nature of the matter itself was contingent, but once
determined by God it became necessary. Joseph was sold and carried away to
Egypt in such a way that, as far as the nature of the matter was concerned,
he could either have remained in servitude forever or been freed at some
point. But God sends dreams to the baker and cupbearer, and Joseph interprets
them. Afterwards a vision is presented to Pharaoh; and when it could be
explained by none of the diviners, the cupbearer then reminded him about
Joseph. Thus it came about that Joseph was brought out of prison. Let what we
have now often said therefore be established: all things, if they are referred
to God's providence, are necessary, but from their own nature are contingent.
“But,” you will say, “will the effects be called contingent without
qualification, or rather necessary?” There are those who, because of the
dignity of providence, wish to call them necessary. But I would prefer to call
them contingent from their own nature. Yet I do not contend about the point,
provided that this necessity is understood to be only from hypothesis. The
Greeks therefore rightly called providence πεπρωμένη, from πεπερατῶσθαι,
because it passes through all things; others called it Ἀδράστεια, because
nothing escapes it.”

So far Peter Martyr. If his precise judgment does not satisfy you, you must
certainly blame yourself; and we shall never regret differing from you in
order to follow the truth. But come, let us hear what your arguments are.

I. CASTELLIO'S ARGUMENT FOR CALUMNIES XIII AND XIV

“If we sin necessarily, all admonitions are in vain. In vain the people are
told in Jeremiah: *I set before you the way of life and death. Whoever remains
in that city will die by sword, famine, and plague; but whoever defects to the
Chaldeans will live.* These things, I say, are spoken in vain if it was as
impossible for them to defect to the Chaldeans as to swallow a mountain.”

REFUTATION

Now indeed my business is with Pelagius. I therefore relegate you to
Augustine's school, for this discussion would be far too long. But if we do
not sin necessarily, we can certainly refrain from sinning and consequently
refrain from willing to sin. Yet Paul cries out that he is not sufficient even
to think anything good. And elsewhere, considering himself outside grace, he
says, *I am carnal, sold under sin.* Indeed, with great lamentations he
complains that he has not yet been fully emancipated. But where is
emancipation if no slavery preceded it? Where is the new man if the old did
not come before? Yet perhaps you will flee from Pelagius to the papist
Sophists, the semi-Pelagians, and acknowledge some slavery to sin—but one
which has only injured nature and lodged only in its lower parts. But what
does David say? *There is none who understands, not even one.* What does Paul
say? *The ψυχικός, the natural person, does not receive the things of God.*
The τὸ τῆς σαρκός φρόνημα, *mind of the flesh*, is enmity against God. And
elsewhere: *May God, the Father of glory, give you the Spirit of wisdom and
revelation, and the eyes of your mind enlightened.* Likewise, he says, *Be
renewed in the spirit of your mind.* Likewise: *When you were slaves of sin,
you were free from righteousness.* But if there is no necessity of sinning,
how did sin enter the world? Evidently by imitation, and not by propagation.
But if so, how did death reign even over those who did not sin in the likeness
of Adam? And why does the Church baptize infants if we are not born children
of wrath? Give glory to God, then, and, if you can, at last acknowledge God's
grace—not that by which we were created to be human beings, but that by which
we were justified to be righteous human beings. For it is God who through
grace works in us wholly both to will and to accomplish.

Why explain at greater length the knot you are tying, when Augustine wrote so
fully and lucidly on this very matter to Valentinus in *On Rebuke and Grace*?
And when after Augustine both that distinguished servant of Christ Martin
Luther, against Erasmus, and that Calvin—whether you like it or not, a
faithful defender of Christian truth—against Pighius, have resolved all these
things? Nevertheless, I shall say briefly what may satisfy you, if you love
