truth. Admonitions have many uses, a fact which you appear not to have
recognized sufficiently. The pious are admonished so that, whenever there is
need, they may be reproved; so that, shaking off their torpor, they may rouse
themselves; so that, convicted at least by shame, if ever they have strayed,
they may return to the way; and so that, once their weakness is recognized,
they may guard themselves better. “But we are not concerned,” you say, “about
those people. For it has been given to them to will and to do. Let us
therefore come to the rest.” There are two kinds of the rest. Some, although
not yet called, nevertheless belong to God's kingdom by his hidden election.
Others are destined to a just destruction. You indeed deny this distinction,
but we proved it from God's Word in its proper place, and experience itself
convicts it of being true.

[Printed p. 414; PDF p. 79; logical scan 438; OCR object 0447]

Although the rays of the sun are the same, they nevertheless melt wax and
harden clay. So it also happens here. Admonitions are set before the elect so
that, recognizing their miserable condition, they may run to the one by whom
alone they can be healed. *I did not come*, Christ says, *to call the
righteous, but sinners to repentance.* Likewise: *I came to save what had
perished.* Likewise: *Come to me, all who labor and are burdened, and I will
refresh you.* Thus Paul says that from the Law come the recognition and even
the increase of sin, but *kata symbebēkos* (incidentally); nevertheless this is
by no means so that the sinner should be swallowed up, but so that he may ask
the Lord all the more ardently for what he will in fact obtain. For as far as
these people are concerned, the efficacy of the Spirit is joined with the
external preaching of the Word. Hence, just as the Lord says, *I gave them
commandments*, elsewhere he also says, *I will cause you to walk in my
commandments*; and, *I will give them a new spirit, remove the stony heart from
their flesh, and give them a heart of flesh.*

As for the rest, although the Word is not destined for their correction, it is
nevertheless not preached to them in vain. By it they are both pressed in the
present by the testimony of conscience and rendered still more inexcusable
for the day of judgment. For necessity, as I shall soon explain, neither
abolishes the will nor diminishes the guilt of the impious. Thus Moses is sent
to Pharaoh and the Egyptians so that they may be hardened by their own
wickedness, which, though necessary, is nevertheless voluntary. Thus Isaiah is
sent to harden the people's heart, stop their ears, and blind their eyes—by no
fault of either Isaiah or the Word, but properly by their own fault—since they
are not dragged unwillingly but, although necessarily, nevertheless freely
resist the truth. But from where does this necessity come? Certainly not from
creation, but from corruption; and even if they have been left in that
corruption, they still have nothing for which to accuse God. But because you
suppose that admonition has only one use—namely, that those who are admonished
should return to the way—you therefore weave a false conclusion. “But this
very thing,” you will say, “is unjust.” As though we were not all worthy for
the Lord to avenge himself upon us by any means whatever. Therefore, let those
from whom he removes the stony heart and to whom he gives a heart of flesh
adore God's mercy; but let those whose stony hearts he hardens, and whom he
binds more and more tightly as guilty, remain silent, convicted by his just
judgment.

II. CASTELLIO'S ARGUMENT FOR CALUMNIES XIII AND XIV

“If Calvin says that commandments are set before people so that they may be
inexcusable, we shall reply that this is empty. For if you command your son to
eat a rock and he does not do it, he will be no more inexcusable after that
command than before. Similarly, if God says to me, *Do not steal*, and I
necessarily steal, and can no more refrain from stealing than eat a rock, I am
no more inexcusable after the commandment than before, nor more excusable
before than after.”

REFUTATION

If we are born children of wrath, are we not, I ask, inexcusable before the
commandment? *I do not pray for the world*, Christ says. Do you understand by
the designation *world* only those who, although they are able to believe the
gospel, nevertheless refuse? If the blind leads the blind, both will fall into
the pit. Or do you suppose that no one is born blind, when, on the contrary, a
human being is nothing other than the darkest gloom? The Apostle testifies
that a person is so enslaved to sin that he is not even fit to think anything
good; but to you he is excusable unless he can of himself not only will but
also act. Yet perhaps you will reply that everyone is indeed born blind to
some extent by nature, but that God gives this grace to all—that they can open
their eyes if they will. But who will grant you this? Where death reigns, sin
certainly reigns; and death reigns even over infants. Where, then, is that
universal grace of yours? As though people were not born in Adam sufficiently
inexcusable by their own nature unless a fresh rejection of grace were also
added. Next, do you suppose that any grace outside Christ which properly
pertains to salvation is conveyed to human beings? But how can those who are
neither included in the covenant nor have ever heard anything of Christ
obtain any grace through Christ? Far less have they embraced him by faith,
for faith comes from hearing. If you deny that innumerable such people have
always existed in the world, the truth itself, evident to all, sufficiently
convicts you. But what is the point of this? It is that all should confess
that by their own nature they are born inexcusable, even if they are born shut
up under the necessity of sin. For necessity does not abolish the will: though
the will acts necessarily, it is nevertheless not compelled, but goes of its
own accord and spontaneously; it is not dragged unwillingly, but knowingly
and deliberately is borne toward evil. “But,” you will say, “no one is so
wretched as to desire evil.” On the contrary, the miserable slavery of a
person not liberated from sin's yoke consists precisely in this: neither can
his understanding perceive what belongs to God and, consequently, what is
good, nor

[Printed p. 415; PDF p. 80; logical scan 439; OCR object 0448]

can his will approve it or his senses desire it. Rather, the whole person,
deceived by an appearance of good, rushes headlong into evil and summons
destruction upon himself. To this evil another is also added, which you do not
understand either. For the person who was already inexcusable before the
commandment is much more inexcusable after the commandment than before it.
Paul is a witness to this, testifying that the light with which people are
endowed, however small, was left to them for this reason: that they should
become inexcusable. For when inborn wickedness immediately suppresses and
extinguishes this light, who does not see that guilt is doubled in this way?
But if that clear exposition of the Divine Law is also added, then desire
truly boils over and, the reins being let loose, is carried into what is
forbidden. What if the preaching of the gospel is also added? Then desire
truly breaks out into manifest fury, as examples from every age and time
prove—namely, in those hearts in which the Lord does not kindle the light of
his Spirit.

“But,” you will say, “it is unjust to command what you know cannot be
performed, just as if a father were to command his son to eat a rock.” What a
wondrous subtlety! Human beings were never created such that they could
devour rocks. It would therefore be unjust to demand that they devour a rock.
But does this necessity of sinning, this inability which makes us unfit even
to think good things, proceed from that first creation? No, it proceeds from
depravity. And from where does this depravity come, except that the man from
whom the necessity of sinning invaded everyone voluntarily defected from his
Maker? “If all people are deservedly held guilty of this defection,” Calvin
says, “let them not suppose themselves excused by the necessity in which they
possess the clearest cause of their own condemnation.” Likewise elsewhere he
says: “What is the poor little man to do when softness of heart, which was
necessary for obedience, is denied him? Indeed, why does he make evasions,
when he can blame his hardness upon no one but himself?”

Finally, to settle the matter with you once and for all, you have heard all
that you can hear: how briefly and aptly that Calvin, through whose side you
attack the truth, decides this entire controversy. “It is not permissible,”
he says, “to offer the excuse that ability is lacking and that, like exhausted
debtors, we are unable to pay. For it is not fitting that we should measure
God's glory by our ability. Whatever we may be, he always remains like
himself, a friend of justice and hostile to injustice. Whatever he demands of
us—because he can demand nothing but what is right—the necessity of obeying
remains from the obligation of nature. But the fact that we cannot obey is our
own fault. For if our own desire, in which sin reigns, holds us bound so that
we are not free for obedience to our Father, there is no reason to plead
necessity in our defense, since its evil is both within us and to be imputed to
us.”

III. CASTELLIO'S ARGUMENT FOR CALUMNIES XIII AND XIV

“If Calvin's position is true, a person is inexcusable before the commandment;
thus there is no need of a commandment to make him inexcusable. For if the
impious person is reprobated before he is impious—that is, before he exists,
namely from eternity—and therefore necessarily sins, he is as inexcusable and
condemned before the commandment as after it, and that is contrary to every
divine and human law. For all laws condemn a person after and because of a
crime; but that God of Calvin condemned and reprobated the impious before they
existed, much less before they were impious or had sinned. And because he
condemned them before sin, he compels them to sin, evidently so that he may
appear to have condemned them justly.”

REFUTATION

We have abundantly proved that human beings are inexcusable even before the
commandment—that is, before knowledge of the commandment—since they are born
children of wrath. Your inference from this, that there is no need of the
commandment for this condition of excusability, is foolishly stated. For why
could a bad condition not become worse? And you err greatly if you suppose
that we think all people are equally inexcusable, since the Lord testifies
that some are to be punished with a graver judgment and beaten with more
blows. Your statement that the impious are reprobated—that is, destined for
reprobation—before they exist, that is from eternity, is not our doctrine but
the Apostle's. He testifies that Esau was hated—that is, destined for hatred,
as we explained in its proper place—before he was born or had done any evil,
and that God, like a potter, fashions vessels of dishonor. This doctrine, I
say, is true if you consider reprobation in God's purpose. But if you consider
it insofar as it has now actually come into effect, as they say, or insofar as
the Lord now begins actually to execute his purpose, or precedes its execution
by his foreknowledge, what you say is false. For God actually reprobates no
one except those corrupted in Adam and with whom he is therefore deservedly
angry. What you say—that the reprobate necessarily sin—is most true. For what
else can slaves of sin do? But when you teach that they sin because they are
reprobated, you do not speak truly. For the purpose of reprobation is not the
proper cause of that necessity, but

[Printed p. 416; PDF p. 81; logical scan 440; OCR object 0449]

Adam's sin, which has permeated all human beings. Your statement that the
reprobate are condemned before the commandment is false in every respect, for
no one is condemned except because of sin. Where there is no law, there is no
transgression. But the law given to Adam and violated by him renders everyone
liable to God's wrath and therefore to just condemnation. Your assertion that
God condemns people before a crime is, according to our doctrine, an utterly
false invention. From eternity the Lord destined for salvation whomever he
pleased; nevertheless, he does not save them except as justified in Christ,
since otherwise he would be unjust. Likewise from eternity he destined for
destruction whomever he willed; nevertheless, he does not consign any to
destruction except those corrupted in Adam, to which corruption are also added
the remaining fruits of corruption and obstinacy. For God destined them to
destruction, but to a just destruction. Therefore, although he does not
destine them to destruction because of corruption and the fruits of
corruption, but because it so seemed good to him, whose will, though hidden,
is nevertheless always just, yet he condemns them only because of corruption
and the fruits of corruption, since he placed these causes beneath the
execution of his eternal purpose. What ignorance is this, then, to mix
condemnation with the purpose of reprobating and even, in a certain place, to
put condemnation before the purpose of reprobating—that is, the outcome
before the principle?

Finally, when you say that it follows from our doctrine that people are
compelled by God to sin, what does this prove except that incredible
shamelessness of yours? We freely profess from God's Word that people sin
necessarily until they are emancipated from slavery to sin. But to you
necessity signifies nothing other than compulsion. It will thus follow that,
because everyone necessarily dies, you will also say that everyone is
compelled to die, although Christ willingly offered himself to death and many
martyrs daily die voluntarily for Christ. What more? On your view even the
devil will be evil neither spontaneously nor willingly, but will sin under
compulsion and against his will. Can someone who speaks in this way seem to
possess even the smallest portion of that common sense by whose norm and rule
you demand that we measure divine wisdom? Yet you dare to discourse on the
nature of the true and false God. But come, let us consider how skillfully you
do so.

THE NATURE OF THE FALSE GOD FROM CASTELLIO'S FALSE HYPOTHESIS

“The false God is slow to mercy and inclined to wrath. He created the greatest
part of the world for destruction and predestined them not only to
condemnation but also to the cause of condemnation. Thus he decreed from
eternity, wills, and brings it about that they sin necessarily, so that neither
thefts, adulteries, nor murders are committed except by his will and impulse.
For he suggests corrupt and dishonorable affections to them, not only
permissively but efficaciously, and hardens them, so that while they live
impiously they do God's work rather than their own and cannot do otherwise. He
makes Satan a liar, so that Satan is no longer the father of falsehood, but
Calvin's God is, since he often has one thing in his mouth and another in his
heart.”

THE NATURE OF THE TRUE GOD ACCORDING TO CASTELLIO'S OPINION

“But the God whom nature and reason as well as the Sacred Writings teach is
entirely contrary to him. For he is inclined to mercy and slow to wrath. He
created in his own image and likeness the man from whom all human beings have
arisen, so that he might place him in Paradise and give him a blessed life.
This God wishes all people to be saved and no one to perish. Therefore he sent
his Son to earth, whose righteousness would superabound wherever sin abounded;
the light of whose righteousness enlightens everyone coming into this world;
and who cries, *Come to me, all who labor and are burdened, and I will refresh
you.* This God suggests good and honorable affections and frees people from
the necessity of sinning into which they had cast themselves by their own
disobedience. He heals every weakness and disease among the people, so that he
has never denied a benefit to anyone who asked. And this God came to destroy
the works of that Calvinian God and cast him outside.

“And just as these two Gods are contrary to each other in nature, so they also
beget children contrary to each other. The former evidently begets the
merciless, proud, harsh, envious, bloodthirsty, slanderous, dissembling, those
who bear one thing in their heart and another in their mouth, the impatient,
malicious, seditious, contentious, ambitious, greedy, and those who love
pleasure more than God—in short, people full of every corrupt and dishonorable
affection which their own father suggests to them. But the other God begets
people who are merciful, modest, gentle, benevolent, beneficent, abhorrent to
bloodshed, open, speaking the truth from the abundance of the heart, patient,
kind, peaceful, peacemaking, abhorrent to quarrels and disputes, despisers of
honor, generous, and loving God more than pleasures—in short, people full of
every honorable affection which their own father suggests to them.”

REFUTATION

We shall see later what God belongs to you and your faction. For first I shall
refute your calumnies; then I shall depict your idol for you. That God is slow
to mercy,

[Printed p. 417; PDF p. 82; logical scan 441; OCR object 0450]

inclined to wrath, we have nowhere said, since we daily perceive the contrary
both in ourselves and even in the Church's most hostile enemies, as we taught
at greater length in the refutation of your third and tenth arguments for the
first Calumny. How the statement that God created the greatest part of the
world for destruction must be understood, and what cunning of yours is found
in this matter, we explained in the rebuttal of that first Calumny. As for
your addition that God predestined whomever he pleased not only to
condemnation but also to the causes of condemnation, we acknowledge with Paul
that this is indeed true, for he mentions vessels fashioned for destruction.
But we also acknowledge God to be just, because under the same decree he
subordinated just causes leading to destruction. Indeed, even if God had not
created a single person for salvation, who are you to call him cruel for this,
when everyone is born a child of wrath in Adam? But your further assertion
that, according to our doctrine, God decreed from eternity, wills, and brings
it about that sin occurs necessarily is utterly false, as we have demonstrated
on almost every page—unless you add that sins, insofar as they are sins,
proceed not from God, but from Satan and the evil human will as the proper
causes of sin; but insofar as they are either penalties for sins or exercises
of the just, they are ordained by God holily and justly. Similar is your
addition that crimes are committed by the will and impulse of our God, when we
testify everywhere that iniquity cannot please God, who avenges it with just
penalties, while the motions of the impious are also governed by God's just
providence. Thus the fiction about the suggestion of corrupt affections is
entirely yours, as we proved in its proper place. For God justly moves even the
hearts of the impious, so that, by wickedly resisting God's will, they perform
God's just will. Hence, although God performs his work through them, he
nevertheless justly punishes them as rebels and the obstinate. God does not
make Satan a liar, nor is he the father of falsehood; rather, he justly employs
Satan—though he is a liar and the father of falsehood—both to exercise his own
people and to scourge the impious, as he pleases. Let whoever says that God
bears one thing in his mouth and another in his heart be accursed. We
nevertheless deny that the Lord reveals all his counsels, but only so far as is
expedient, as appeared in the civil war waged against the Benjaminites. We
also distinguish the things which the Lord says for the sake of testing,
rebuking, and threatening from those which he commands without qualification
to be done. Because you understand none of these things, you therefore weave
utterly false conclusions and completely ensnare yourself while desiring to
entangle us.

But come now, let us contemplate your idol. First, I ask, what sort of God did
you determine to depict for us? Evidently, one such as nature and reason
teach. Yet we owe all idolatry to this nature and reason of yours, as Paul
testifies—and that while discussing not just anyone, but the wisest of all
human beings. The same Paul cries out that τὸ τῆς σαρκός φρόνημα, the mind of
the flesh, is nothing other than hatred of God; that those not included in
God's covenant are ἄθεοι, without God; that the world did not know God through
wisdom; that the gospel is a stumbling block to Jews and foolishness to
Gentiles; that the ψυχικός, the natural person, does not receive the things of
God; and that we are not even fit to think anything good. Christ everywhere
condemns our darkness and insists upon regeneration and the denial of
ourselves. Finally, the very men with whom God spoke most familiarly cry out
that God's height and depth are infinite and that his ways cannot be searched
out. What more? Philosophers openly admit that when the matter comes to God,
they are blind. But you, on the contrary, require no teachers for attaining
knowledge of God other than nature and reason. In doing this, you act exactly
as if you entrusted judgment about colors to none but the blind. Yet in third
place you add the Sacred Writings, by which nature and reason are evidently
aided, just as your Pelagius also used to speak long ago. But I ask how it can
happen that you join light with darkness—as though we did not first have to
become fools before we can taste God's wisdom. *You hid these things from the
wise*, Christ says, *and revealed them to little children*; and when discussing
the Scribes and Pharisees, who suffered from this very disease, he says, *If
you were blind*—that is, if you understood that you were blind—*you would
surely see.* Do you see what the principles of your theology are? But come,
let us hear what you have learned in this school and with what colors darkness
paints the light.

You therefore say that God is inclined to mercy and slow to wrath. You surely
begin well, since you drew these things from God's Word, and reason readily
admits what does not appear to terrify it. But it will soon become evident,
when we come to the dispensation of this mercy, that you also pervert this
truth of God. You therefore add that God created the man from whom all human
beings arose in his own image and likeness, so that he might place him in
Paradise and give him a blessed life. I concede what you say about man's
creation, and

[Printed p. 418; PDF p. 83; logical scan 442; OCR object 0451]

nature and reason could also tell you this to some degree. For God willingly
allows himself to be praised and adorned. But I disagree with you concerning
man's end, especially since you consider Adam not as a single individual but
as the human species, because all human beings were included in him. To pass
over that futile dream concerning Paradise which the vanity of your nature
and mind taught you and which we refuted in its proper place, from where did
you learn to judge God's counsel in creating man in this way? You will find
nothing of the kind in the Sacred Writings. Rather, you will find this: that
God created all things for his own glory, even the impious person for the day
of destruction. Your reason and understanding, then, dictated this fiction to
you. For otherwise, you say, God would be cruel and worse than any wolf if he
had created any people for misery. What if I deny that one who determines
something for just reasons is cruel, however hard and bitter it may be,
provided that its bitterness does not exceed the measure of justice? Will you
deny this? I think not. What if I add that God's will is the rule of justice,
not the reverse? I think you will agree. How does it happen, then, that when
the Sacred Writings openly testify that God created even the impious person
for the day of just destruction for the sake of his own glory, you maintain,
on the contrary, that God created everyone in Adam in order to give them a
blessed life? Is it because you do not know the causes by which God was moved
deservedly to destine those who were not yet born for the just infliction of
misery? Reason evidently dictates this to you—but how foolishly! For you
assume what is utterly foolish and entirely contrary to God's own nature:
namely, that God possesses no rationale for his counsels whenever human beings
cannot comprehend it. How much better you would have done if you had compelled
nature and reason to remain within their bounds and thrown a bridle upon them
whenever they dare to kick against the Holy Spirit.

But let us also see further how that vain human reason defeats itself. Does
not the very reason which a little before compelled us to judge man's end
otherwise than God's Word permits now judge it utterly foolish that God the
Maker should not be permitted, in creating any things whatever, what everyone
allows a potter in forming his vessels? For God certainly excels even the
angels far more than a potter excels his clay. But I also ask you this: Does
God seem to you always consistent with himself and omnipotent? Surely he does,
I think. How, then, can you admit a position which, once posited, makes it
impossible for him either always to be consistent with himself or to be
omnipotent? For if he created all people with the counsel of saving all, why
does he not save them? Is it because he no longer wills to save those whom he
created at the beginning? Then he has changed his will and truly regretted
his counsel. Is it because he cannot save them? Then he will not be
omnipotent. But what sort of deity is this, which neither always remains like
itself nor can do whatever it wills? I certainly grant you the objection you
might make, namely that the cause why all are not saved lies in human beings
themselves. But as I explained at greater length before, this does not help you
at all, because the outcome of the divine counsel is thus suspended from
secondary causes. This is no less absurd than if you said that the Lord had
created other lords who would command him, upon whose will the outcome of his
own decrees depends.

Finally, see how your later statements answer to the first. You said that by
nature God is inclined to mercy and slow to wrath. Yet if this counsel of his
which you report had been fixed, then, as far as human beings are concerned,
no place would have been left for this nature of his. Nature and will
therefore conflict in God. For his nature is to employ both mercy and wrath,
which his will would not have allowed him to do. What place would there have
been for either mercy or wrath if he had given eternal life to all people in
Adam? The truth, therefore, stands thus. Since the Lord illustrates his glory
by declaring both his wrath and his mercy, he also created man such that in
this creature especially he would manifest both the one and the other. He
therefore created him righteous—for how could he who is wholly good do
otherwise? Nor could he otherwise justly condemn evil if he were the author of
evil—but with this condition: that man would soon, of his own accord (for he
was endowed with a will), and therefore by his own fault, defect from his
Maker and carry away into the same precipice all those who were included in
him. To what end? That he might manifest his mercy in saving some miserable
people, his most just wrath in deservedly condemning others, and thus his
whole nature in man. But come, let us continue contemplating your idol.

You say that this God wishes all people to be saved. Yet again you see into
what precipices you are carried because you subject God's Word to reason,
whereas reason ought, on the contrary, to have been subjected to God's Word.
But let us see how elegantly this reason of yours argues: God would not be
consistent with himself unless, just as he willed at the beginning, he also
saved everyone after Adam's sin. But you posit something which we have shown,
even by the judgment of reason itself, to be necessarily false. You therefore
seek to avoid a difficulty which exists nowhere. “God,” you say, “would be
cruel unless he willed everyone to be saved.” But I say that not even reason
could regard him as cruel,

[Printed p. 419; PDF p. 84; logical scan 443; OCR object 0452]

even if he saved not one of Adam's descendants, since all deserve to perish.
Thus God is neither inconstant nor cruel even if he does not save all. Indeed,
if he saved all, he would disagree with himself, since he necessarily—his own
nature evidently requiring it—decreed from the beginning to manifest in man
not only his mercy but also his wrath for the illustration of his glory, and
therefore to save not all, but only some. Finally, if I grant you this point, I
shall entangle you in the same nets as a little before. For if he certainly
wills all to be saved but does not save them when they are to be saved, this
comes about either because he has changed his decision or because he cannot
save them. Even that guide and teacher of yours teaches that neither is
consistent with God's nature. These things being posited, then, what is the
purpose of this perverse fear that God might appear cruel if he does not will
someone to be saved, when you ought rather to marvel at God's goodness in
willing even one of Adam's descendants to be saved? But it is worth seeing the
testimonies of Scripture which you misuse to establish this error.

First you adduce that passage of Paul in which God is said to will all to be
saved. This was an old objection of the Pelagians, lest you think it first
entered your own mind. As though *all* signified the same as *each and every*,
and were not rather taken for *any kind*, so that every προσωποληψία,
partiality, is removed—just as the same Apostle says elsewhere that in Christ
there is neither male nor female, Jew nor Greek, free person nor slave. The
Lord commands the Apostles to preach the gospel πάσῃ τῇ κτίσει, *to all
creation*, which you will not deny, I think, must be taken for all people. Did
the Apostles, then, either fail to discharge their office, or announce the
gospel to every single human being? In Matthew the Lord is said to have healed
every weakness and every disease in Galilee. Do you really suppose that no
sick people remained, rather than that this signifies that every kind of
weakness and disease was healed by Christ?

Therefore, when this passage and similar others are cited by certain people
very unlike you, their purpose is not to maintain that God's kingdom pertains
to every individual. It is rather that all who undergo trial concerning their
particular election should know that the call is indefinite—that is, that
people of any kind, guilty of crimes however great and of whatever age, are
called by God—lest, deterred by consciousness of their vices, they despair or
seek the declaration of their election in the highest degree, that is, in
God's purpose, outside Christ and that voice of the gospel resounding in the
Church. For this most certain doctrine of eternal election must be applied to
our use in an entirely contrary order of causes: namely, so that we gradually
proceed from the effects to that primary cause of our salvation, where at last,
having found a firm and solid remedy against every storm, we may rest as in a
harbor. Otherwise, if the opinion of those most learned men, whose doctrine
you do not understand, were that, when a person undergoes trial concerning his
election, this saying of Paul and similar passages should be taken as though
the Lord willed to save every individual, they would instead furnish an
argument by which anyone could confirm himself in every crime and wickedness,
if he supposed that, because God wills to save every individual, it would be
within his own power to repent whenever he wished. Nor certainly could they
pacify consciences upon which this doctrine is inscribed as if by God's own
finger: that some have been elected from eternity, and therefore some also
reprobated. But when they teach, on the contrary, that universal propositions
of this kind must be set against thoughts especially of past sins and of our
unworthiness, which could terrify consciences, who does not see that they take
them in no other sense than to persuade people that God elects persons of any
kind, provided they apprehend the offered promises by faith?

Thus there is no difference in the matter itself between us and certain
others who teach that God's grace is universal—that is, offered
indiscriminately to persons of any kind without προσωποληψία, partiality. We,
however, teach the doctrine separately and then its use; they join its use
together with the doctrine in their teaching. But since you neither
understand what the doctrine is here nor what its use is, and have not even
read with sufficient care the writings of those whose name and authority you
use as a pretext for your blasphemies, what do you disclose except your
ignorance joined with great wickedness?

Moreover, the passage of Peter which you cite, in which God is said to will
that no one perish, was sufficiently explained by us in the refutation of the
ninth argument for the first Calumny. I therefore come to the third testimony.
For you say that God sent his Son to earth, whose righteousness would
superabound wherever sin abounded. Here I cannot sufficiently marvel at your
audacity. For in Paul I do not read, “Wherever sin abounded,” but that “where
sin abounded, grace abounded.” In these words the Apostle's purpose certainly
is not

[Printed p. 420; PDF p. 85; logical scan 444; OCR object 0453]

to explain how broadly Christ's benefit extends—that is, whether it pertains
to every individual or only to certain people. Rather, his purpose is to show
this one thing: the Law of Moses did not supervene upon the law of nature so
that anyone should be justified by it, but, on the contrary, so that people
should be held still more guilty and convicted. But when people were in this
miserable condition, the grace of the gospel appeared, abounding beyond sin
and bringing people a benefit correspondingly greater as the slavery of sin,
which had increased through the Law, was greater. Who, then, would not marvel
that anyone could twist these things so as to prove that God wills every
single person to be saved?

I now come to the passage of John in which you suppose it is said that the
light of Christ's righteousness enlightens everyone coming into this world.
But who will grant you this? For I admit that Christ is signified there by the
term *light*, but with respect to creation, not redemption. John describes the
excellence of the eternal Word, by whose power and goodness all things were
created and people especially live endowed with the light of understanding;
nevertheless, because this Light shines upon the blind, the blind did not
recognize the light. After John had said this at the beginning of the chapter,
he inserted mention of John the Baptist and then impressed the same point in
two verses of that chapter, the ninth and tenth. From the beginning of the
eleventh verse he then comes to Christ's ἐπιφάνεια, manifestation. I hope that
all who weigh carefully everything said there will grant me that this is the
genuine meaning of the passage. For besides the fact that this is the sequence
of the discourse, tell me, I ask, by what rationale do you prove that everyone
who is born is enlightened by Christ? Is it that all become participants in
Christ's grace? Yet John adds that Christ was not received by his own except
by certain people, who believed because they were born of God. Moreover, you
cannot deny that Christ's benefit is received by faith alone. But faith comes
from grace, not nature, and is not given to all. Thus not all are enlightened
by Christ—that is, become participants in the grace of the gospel. Or will you
flee to that universal preaching of the gospel and say that Christ's light is
offered to all, although most reject it when offered? Yet not even this could
ever be said of every single person. Tell me, before Christ's advent, how many
centuries passed during which, as Paul says, the Lord permitted the Gentiles
to walk in their own ways? Where then was that light of the coming Christ
among the Gentiles? I nevertheless acknowledge that in the meantime all had
as much light as was sufficient to render them ἀναπολόγητοι, inexcusable,
since conscience served them for law and, by contemplation of visible things
itself, they could and should have been recalled from idolatry.

But we are asking about the light of the gospel, by which you will every
person—that is, every individual—to be enlightened at least outwardly. I
affirm, however, that before Christ's advent the greatest part of the world
was illuminated by the rays of this light neither inwardly nor outwardly.
After Christ's advent I indeed acknowledge that the light of the gospel was
spread into every land; but do you suppose that every individual received
this benefit, rather than persons of any kind—that is, selected persons from
all peoples? Look at the face of the world even now, and recognize how many
throughout the whole globe die each day before they hear even the faintest
report of the gospel's doctrine. Is the Lord therefore cruel? To you, as I
see, he is; for one even among your close associates, in a booklet published
in previous years concerning the breadth of God's mercy, preferred to defend
that those to whom the gospel had not been preached are nevertheless saved
through Christ rather than suppose that so many people are consigned to
eternal punishments. But we have learned to locate salvation nowhere but in
Christ, and Christ nowhere but among the faithful and in the household of the
faithful. So far are we from accusing God of any cruelty on this account that,
on the contrary, even if he rejected everyone without exception, we would not
say that he was cruel, since we are all children of wrath by nature.

Let us advance still further. Even if we were not born children of wrath, yet
if God's will were to create all people for destruction in order to declare
his power and wrath, we would remain silently content in that will, as is
proper, as the supreme and sole rule of justice. For what is more just than
for human beings to regard as truly good and just precisely what God does,
even if they cannot comprehend his judgments? Nor, if he condemns more than
he saves, does he therefore appear to us more inclined to wrath than to mercy,
because God's mercy through Christ is so great that, even if it pertained to
only one person, as far as we are concerned this goodness of God would still
be infinitely greater than his just anger against all the rest. For in being
angry with sins he seems in a certain sense to act necessarily, because he is
just and sins deserve this; but in undertaking to save even one person,

[Printed p. 421; PDF p. 86; logical scan 445; OCR object 0454]

he made himself human; that indeed seems in some way more infinite by infinity
itself.

It remains for us to consider those sweetest words of Christ: “Come to me, all
you who labor.” You suppose that they pertain to every individual, as if Christ
were inviting every single person to himself. I, however, make many exceptions.
First, I say that Christ speaks as a minister. This is the minister's office:
since he does not know God's secret counsels, he is to teach indiscriminately
all who are entrusted to his care and invite them to the Lord, and so far as
lies in him, to desire them to be saved. But if you infer from this that all
belong to God's flock or that first grace is given equally to all, you are
greatly mistaken. For the Lord wills the gospel to be announced to many blind,
many deaf, mute, and obstinate people so that they may become more and more
blinded, deafened, and hardened. God is not therefore a hypocrite, as we
demonstrated in its proper place. Next, if these words of Christ pertain to
every individual without exception, why, when Christ frames that solemn prayer
which as eternal High Priest he offered shortly before the sacrifice of his
flesh, does he testify expressly that he does not pray for the world, but only
for those whom the Father gave him? Is it because by the designation *world*
he understands those who are able to believe but do not wish to? Certainly
not, since whatever the Father has given comes to the Son. It follows from this
that those whom the Father has not given to the Son are not even able to
believe; and that those who come to the Son are not given to the Son because
they believe, but, on the contrary, believe because they have been given to
the Son, in whom, to be sure, they were elected before the foundations of the
world were laid. Finally, those who are given to the Son cannot fail to
believe, although this τὸ δύνασθαι, *ability*, does not depend upon their nature
but upon power bestowed from God. For the power of the Holy Spirit is
effectual in those whom the Lord draws, so that not only the faculty of
believing but also the actual effect of the faculty—that is, faith—is given.
Since these things are so, how is it that you wish the particle, which is not
so much universal as indefinite, to pertain to every individual, and thus wish
Christ to disagree with himself?

For if the world—so that you include even those to whom the gospel either is
not announced or is announced in vain—is invited by Christ in these words, how
is it that elsewhere he testifies that he does not pray for the world? But why
pursue these points at such length? Christ does not invite absolutely everyone
to himself, but persons of any kind who are weary and burdened, that is, those
who groan beneath the burden of sins and succumb as if crushed, or, as David
says, those who are contrite and lowly in heart. Show that every individual is
such, or acknowledge that these words of Christ contribute nothing to
establishing your error.

But come, let us proceed to the rest. What you say—that God suggests good and
upright affections—we know from experience to be most true, since we experience
well enough that we are not competent of ourselves even to think anything
good. But we say that this is proper to the children of God, for they alone are
led by God's Spirit. Whatever proceeds from the rest we say is sin before God,
although in human judgment it has the appearance of good. Last, you add that
God frees people from the necessity of sinning into which they had cast
themselves headlong by their own disobedience. What you say I approve in such
a way that it does not satisfy me. First, concerning what you say about the
necessity of sinning, I truly cannot marvel enough that this slipped out of
you, who have so often accused us on the very ground that we say people sin
necessarily, though willingly. But such, of course, is the power of truth that
it often compels even liars to contradict themselves. Yet I require two things
here. I would like you to state expressly how God frees us from this necessity
of sinning: namely, through the Son, and that by sheer grace. For I suspect,
not without reason, that although perhaps you have never investigated either
what Pelagius thought or what Augustine especially answered the Pelagians,
nevertheless the same madness drives you by which Pelagius, once stirred up,
miserably troubled the West. Second, what you add about human disobedience
certainly does not satisfy me, because we have already refuted many arguments
from which one may readily gather that you either regard original sin, or the
propagation of sin, as a complete fable or at least diminish it so far as to
think that what belongs to propagation should rather be attributed to
imitation. Certainly, when the Apostle treats these matters—that is, the first
origin of our servitude—he attributes to the disobedience of one man what you
seem to credit to the rebellion of each individual, as if each person cast
himself into the fetters of this necessity through imitation and long habit,
rather than that we all are born slaves from conception itself. Accordingly,
I do not admit even this unless you explain your opinion on the matter more
clearly.

Finally, as for your addition that God has never denied a benefit to anyone
who asks, we acknowledge it as true, but with an explanation. For not all ask,
but only those who

[Printed p. 422; PDF p. 87; logical scan 446; OCR object 0455]

are touched by a sense of their need. Many also ask for what they do not
understand, or do not ask from faith, and consequently receive nothing.
Therefore, since no one asks—or, if he asks, does not ask rightly—unless he is
led by God's Spirit and is consequently a child of God, I affirm that this
benefit of God pertains not to every individual, as you wish, but only to
elect persons of any kind. Their peculiar token is that they alone rightly
invoke the Father and consequently they alone are heard.

Finally, why refute at greater length what you tacked on about the “Calvinian
God,” unless we wish to do what has already been done? Yet this will open a way
for me to consider rather more closely those “sons of the gods” of yours. I
profess myself to be one of those whom the Lord instructed in this true and
holy doctrine of his gospel through his faithful servant John Calvin. I will
certainly never be ashamed to profess myself his disciple and one among the
many thousands of people whom he won for Christ. And why indeed should we
regret this, when chiefly through this teacher—by God's grace—we learned not
only to detect and detest the errors of the Papists, Libertines, Anabaptists,
Schwenckfeldians, Servetists, and finally your own errors, compounded from most
of those rancid heresies, but also to refute them orally and with firm
arguments? You cannot detest our faults, which are both great and many, more
than we ourselves do. Yet, without arrogance and with very many competent
witnesses, we may say this about ourselves in general: our life is such that
the things you charge against us can, by God's kindness, be refuted without so
much difficulty. We certainly do not begrudge you and your people the virtues
of which you weave a long catalogue. And indeed, if the same thing happens to
your virtues as to money—so that the more sparing of them a possessor is, the
richer he is at home—then, I say, if it is so, you must abound in many
virtues, since, at least toward us, you employ them so sparingly. For of what
sort of modesty—or indeed of what sort of conscience and shame—is it, I ask,
to accuse Calvin of impiety, though he is, if nothing else, certainly the
fiercest single enemy of all the Libertines, and to accuse all our churches,
which profess one and the same doctrine, as if we made God the author of evil?
Of what sort of mercy, gentleness, and clemency is it, while you wish Servetus,
that is, impiety itself, and heretics of every kind to be spared, to make a
most innocent man—who, whether you wish it or not, is most upright and has
deserved so well of God's Church in every way that his memory will be most
precious among posterity—hateful to everyone by so many calumnies and insults,
and to wish him crushed once and for all? Of what sort of beneficence and
goodwill is it to assail with so many scurrilous insults the man from whom you
received so many benefits? Of what sort of truth and integrity is it to cloak
so malevolent a mind with the sacred name of friendship and familiarity; now
to call yourself Bellius, now Theophilus, now to append no name at all, and to
employ those childish absurdities to deceive people; to scatter booklets
secretly and send them surreptitiously through your agents to be printed in
foreign regions; to seek distant friendships with people to whom you know you
are unknown, and to misuse their solicited letters and testimonies to deceive
the simple; yet at home to distrust the most learned men, outwardly to attend
sermons religiously but privately to propound a new doctrine, gather
disciples, and keep men in lodgings as if hired to entrap arriving guests and
lay snares for the simplicity of the unsuspecting? But more than enough about
these matters. Whatever sort of people we are, God judges us; against your
calumnies we do not hesitate to appeal to his tribunal. By his singular mercy
toward us we possess more and surer testimonies that we worship from his own
word, in spirit and in truth, the true God and Father of our Lord Jesus Christ,
not that false idol which you both thrust upon us and fashion for yourself,
than either to allow us ourselves to waver or to make us suppose that people
of sound judgment and even the slightest learning can be alienated from our
churches by your futile inventions.

CASTELLIO'S CONCLUDING CALUMNIES

These, Calvin, are the things which your adversaries report about your
doctrine, and they admonish people to judge that doctrine by its fruit. They
say that you and your disciples bear many fruits of your God: that most are
quarrelsome, eager for vengeance, tenacious and mindful of injury, and endowed
with the other vices which your God suggests. If anyone answers them that the
fault does not lie in the doctrine, since it is sound and does not produce such
people, they reply that it does produce such people, and that this becomes
plain from the fact that many, after going over to that doctrine, become such,
although they were less evil before. But now by the doctrine

[Printed p. 423; PDF p. 88; logical scan 447; OCR object 0456]

of Christ those who believed were made better; but by your doctrine, they say,
people plainly become worse. Moreover, when you say that you have sound
doctrine, they reply that you are not to be believed. For if your God very
often says one thing but thinks and wills another, it is to be feared that you,
imitating your God, do the same and so deceive people.

I myself once favored your doctrine, Calvin, and defended it, although it was
not sufficiently clear to me, because I attributed so much to your authority
that I thought it impious even to think contrary to it. But now that I have
heard the adversaries' arguments, I have no answer to give. Your disciples do
indeed try to answer and, among their own people whom they have as supporters,
boldly boast of the truth. But when they come to the adversaries, they waver
and seek aid from your books, but it is feeble. For your arguments are obscure
and generally of such a kind that, as soon as the book is put down from one's
hand, they fall out of memory and do not convince the adversaries. The
adversaries' arguments, however, are clear and sharp, are readily committed to
memory, and can be grasped by the illiterate—such as those who followed Christ
generally were. Hence it happens that your disciples generally rely more upon
your authority than upon reason. And those who cannot defeat their adversaries
regard them as heretics and obstinate people, abstain from their company, and
warn everyone everywhere to abstain. I, however, think that attention should
be paid not to who speaks but to what is said; I judge that everyone must be
judged and everything tested, and that what is good must be retained.
Therefore, Calvin, if you have any true, clear, and firm arguments by which the
adversaries may be refuted, I ask you to bring them forward so that you may
defend the truth. You know what is written: “A mouth and wisdom will be given
to you, which no one will be able to resist.” Wherever I can discover the
truth, I am prepared to follow it and to exhort others to do the same. But if
perhaps you have erred—for we are human—I ask you, Calvin, to give glory to
God. That will be more glorious for you than persisting in error. As for my
part, if you are just and truthful, I do not think I need fear that you will be
angry with me because of this letter: first, because it is in your interest to
be admonished about the matter; second, because if you think, as you say, that
all things happen necessarily, you will believe that this letter too was
necessarily written by me. Farewell.

REFUTATION

These in turn, Castellio, are the things which a disciple in Christ's school
of God's faithful and steadfast servant John Calvin draws from God's word
against your profane and impious inventions; and he admonishes people to guard
themselves repeatedly against your doctrine, no longer disguised but open,
which is compounded from the monsters and monstrosities of the Papists,
Anabaptists, Servetus, Pelagius, and finally yourself. As for your attacking us
who are progressing under Calvin, I truly cannot be angry with you for that,
although I not only freely profess myself to be one of their number but also
give thanks to God for it every day. I pity you instead, for you have forgotten
two things; if only at least one of them had occurred to you. For, to pass over
the very many people of every rank who heard Calvin teaching in both France
and Germany, with great profit, these same things which he teaches, and who
even today preserve a grateful memory of this benefit; and to pass over the
countless host of most learned people who today hang upon the teacher's lips:
what corner of the Christian world do you think exists today in which there
are not very many people who feel that they have profited by reading the
writings of so great a theologian? What then will become of you, miserable man,
if even one in a thousand of so great a number undertakes to oppose your
ravings? Clearly a great field is opened to you for exercising your patience.
And perhaps it will happen that, whereas long ago among those people whom you
were perfecting you used to say in jest—as I suppose—that you were only two
thousand paces distant from the goal of perfection, you will pass beyond the
very goal itself. But I forgive you. For doubtless you were so occupied in
understanding and transcribing the arguments of our adversaries that you
unwittingly cast yourself into this danger, forgetting, of course, that your
business is with people who are proud, bloodthirsty, slanderous, deceitful,
impatient, above all tenacious of injuries, and abusive, so as to resemble
their master. But how could this have failed to occur to you: that you too were
Calvin's disciple and owe more to him than to any other single person, as you
yourself formerly declared very often—but in your own manner, that is,
sincerely and frankly, as the event shows? Moreover, you say that formerly,
moved solely by the authority of Calvin's name, you so favored his doctrine
that you considered it impious even to think against it. We do not know by
what rationale you can excuse this before those adversaries of ours,
especially such sharp disputants. For they will say that a person educated in
the superstition of the Roman clergy who goes over to the Evangelicals' side
before he has learned with sufficient clarity what the Evangelicals teach
must be either frivolous and futile by nature or perhaps not seriously enough
devoted to the Christian religion. But, of course, those adversaries of ours
will pardon this in an Academic without so much difficulty.

[Printed p. 424; PDF p. 89; logical scan 448; OCR object 0457]

I come to the remaining matters about which you are concerned. Your complaint
about the obscurity of our arguments, and your saying that the adversaries'
arguments can readily be committed to memory and grasped by the illiterate,
does not greatly disturb us. For we say from Paul that the ψυχικὸς, *natural*,
person does not grasp the things of God; and you are greatly mistaken if you
think that being illiterate contributes anything to grasping God's truth.
Those who followed Christ were generally illiterate, but they did not follow
him because they were illiterate, as that celebrated discourse recorded in
the sixth chapter of John sufficiently shows. Then let this be your maxim:
μηδὲν εἰδέναι ἥδιστος βίος, “to know nothing is the sweetest life.” But as for
us, just as we disdain no one, having learned by experience that the Spirit of
God has breathed upon whomever he pleased, nevertheless we have learned to
cultivate the liberal arts and disciplines to the best of our ability; nor do
we greatly fear those ignoramuses of yours among whom you place yourself.
Indeed, there was no great need for us to be taught that these arguments came
from people of this kind, since the matter itself proclaims that such nonsense
could not have proceeded from others. Yet we were mistaken in one thing: we
thought they were dogs, whom you write are asses. If you say seriously that
you are prepared to follow the truth, you have, I hope, somewhere to discover
it, if only you resolve not to disdain the writings of others and especially
Calvin. If you discover that we have erred anywhere, we will not only give
glory to God but will also give thanks to you. We read your calumnies partly
not without indignation and pain because of the impious blasphemies scattered
throughout, and partly not without laughter because of the endless
absurdities. If we have answered somewhat more harshly than you wished, you
must blame yourself, for as far as we were concerned you were free to remain
silent. Yet you will bear all this patiently, I hope, in keeping with your
singular modesty, especially if you allow the law which you prescribe for us
to be stated to you: namely, that we should bear what has happened necessarily.
For it was certainly more necessary for us to defend our reputation, joined as
it is with the defense of true doctrine, than for you to stab the truth through
the side of a most innocent and upright man.
